Algebraic cycles and functorial lifts from 𝐆 2 \mathbf{G}_{2} to 𝐏𝐆𝐒𝐩 6 \mathbf{PGSp}_{6} Thanks: A. Cauchi was supported by the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No. 682152) as well as by the NSERC grant RGPIN-2018-04392 and Concordia Horizon postdoc fellowship n.8009. F. Lemma was supported by the ANR Ferplay and the ANR ClapClap. J. Rodrigues Jacinto was financially supported by the ERC-2018-COG-818856-HiCoShiVa and by the project ANR-19-CE40-0015 COLOSS.
Antonio Cauchi, Francesco Lemma, Joaquín Rodrigues Jacinto

Abstract
. We study instances of Beilinson–Tate conjectures for automorphic representations of PGSp 6 {\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6} whose Spin L L -function has a pole at s = 1 s=1 . We construct algebraic cycles of codimension three in the Siegel–Shimura variety of dimension six and we relate its regulator to the residue at s = 1 s=1 of the L L -function of certain cuspidal forms of PGSp 6 {\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6} . Using the exceptional theta correspondence between the split group of type G 2 G_{2} and PGSp 6 {\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6} and assuming the non-vanishing of a certain archimedean integral, this allows us to confirm a conjecture of Gross and Savin on rank 7 7 motives of type G 2 G_{2} .



1.
Introduction

In this paper we establish a connection between algebraic cycles in Siegel sixfolds and the residue at
s=1
of Spin
L
-functions of automorphic representations of
\mathrm{GSp}_{6}
, as predicted by conjectures of Beilinson and Tate. Moreover, we exploit an exceptional theta correspondence between the split group of type
G_{2}
and
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
to answer a question of Gross and Savin.

1.1.
Motivation

Let
\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}
be a cohomological cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
, let
M(\pi_{f})
denote the Spin Chow motive with coefficients in a number field
L
conjecturally attached to
\pi
and let
L(s,M(\pi_{f})(3))
be its Hasse-Weil
L
-function. Let

r_{\mathcal{H}}:H^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}(M(\pi_{f})(4))\oplus N(M(\pi_{f})(3))\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})(4))

denote Beilinson-Deligne regulator. Here
H^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}(M(\pi_{f})(4))
denotes the first motivic cohomology group of
M(\pi_{f})(4)
, the group
N(M(\pi_{f})(3))
denotes algebraic cycles in
M(\pi_{f})(3)
up to homological equivalence and
H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})(4))
denotes the first absolute Hodge cohomology group of
M(\pi_{f})(4)
.

Conjecture 1.1
.

(Beilinson–Tate)

(1)

The map
r_{\mathcal{H}}
induces an isomorphism

(H^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}(M(\pi_{f})(4))\oplus N(M(\pi_{f})(3)))\otimes_{\bf Q}{\bf R}\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})(4)),

(2)

\mathrm{ord}_{s=0}L(s,M(\pi_{f})(3))=\dim_{L}H^{1}_{\mathcal{M}}(M(\pi_{f})(4)),

(3)

-\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(s,M(\pi_{f})(3))=\dim_{L}N(M(\pi_{f})(3))

(4)

\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{Im}\,r_{\mathcal{H}})=L^{*}(1,M(\pi_{f})(3))\mathcal{D}(M(\pi_{f})(4))
, where
\mathcal{D}(M(\pi_{f})(4))
denotes the Deligne
L
-structure of
\mathrm{det}(H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})(4))
.

In
[
BGCLRJ23
]
, we studied the contribution of the motivic cohomology to this conjecture. This corresponds to the case where
L(s,M(\pi_{f})(3))
does not have a pole at
s=1
. In this article, we focus on the contribution of algebraic cycles, which corresponds to the case where
L(s,M(\pi_{f})(3))
has a simple pole at
s=1
.

The
\ell
-adic étale realization
M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})
of
M(\pi_{f})
is expected to be a
\mathrm{GL}_{8}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
-valued Galois representation factoring through the Spin representation
\mathrm{Spin}:\mathrm{Spin}_{7}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{8}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
. If
L(s,M(\pi_{f})(3))
has a pole at
s=1
, Conjecture
1.1
(3) implies the existence of an invariant vector in this
8
-dimensional Galois representation. As the stabilizer in
\mathrm{Spin}_{7}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
of a generic vector in the Spin representation is the exceptional group
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
, by Langlands reciprocity principle,
\pi
should be a functorial lift from a group
G
of type
G_{2}
. In fact, we have
\mathrm{Spin}_{|_{G_{2}}}=\mathrm{Std}\oplus\mathbf{1}
, where Std denotes the standard representation of
G_{2}
and
\mathbf{1}
denotes the trivial representation. Then, if
\sigma
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G(\mathbf{A})
lifting to
\pi
, Gross and Savin
[
GS98
]
conjectured that the motive
M(\pi_{f})
decomposes as the direct sum of the rank
7
motive
M(\sigma_{f})
attached to
\sigma
and the rank
1
trivial motive generated by the class given in Conjecture
1.1
. Moreover, inspired by local calculations, they conjectured that this class should arise from a Hilbert modular threefold.

1.2.
Main results

Let
F
denote a real étale quadratic
{\bf Q}
-algebra, i.e.,
F
is either a quadratic extension of
{\bf Q}
or
{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
. Associated to the totally real étale cubic algebra
E={\bf Q}\times F
of
{\bf Q}
there is a Hilbert modular threefold
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}/{\bf Q}
, with underlying reductive group
\mathbf{H}=\{g\in\mathrm{Res}_{E/{\bf Q}}\mathrm{GL}_{2,E}\;|\;\mathrm{det}(g)\in\mathbf{G}_{m}\}
. The group
\mathbf{H}
embeds naturally into
\mathbf{G}={\mathrm{GSp}}_{6}
and one has a closed embedding
\iota:{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}
of codimension
3
in the Shimura variety attached to
\mathbf{G}
, which is the Siegel variety of dimension
6
. Let
V^{\lambda}
be the irreducible algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}
of highest weight
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)
(cf. §
2
for notations on algebraic representations). The representation
V^{\lambda}
contains the trivial
\mathbf{H}
-representation if and only if
c=0
and
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}
. When this holds
\iota^{*}V^{\lambda}
contains
\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
copies of the trivial representation of
\mathbf{H}
, which we index by the values
\lambda_{2}\geq\mu\geq\lambda_{3}
. Then, for any such
\mu
, the cycle
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}
of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}
induces a class

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}(3)),

where
\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}
is the Chow local system associated to
V^{\lambda}
and
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{M}}(3))
is the motivic cohomology group of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\lambda}(3)
.
We denote by
\mathcal{Z}^{[\lambda,\mu]}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}\in H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathcal{H}}(4))
, resp.
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{B}(3))
the image of
\mathcal{Z}^{[\lambda,\mu]}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}
in absolute Hodge cohomology, resp. Betti cohomology (see Definition
3.11
for the precise definition of
\mathcal{Z}^{[\lambda,\mu]}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}
). Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
whose archimedean component belongs to the discrete series
L
-packet of
V^{\lambda}
and has Hodge type
(3,3)
. For a cusp form
\Psi=\Psi_{\infty}\otimes\Psi_{f}
in the space of
\pi
, whose archimedean component
\Psi_{\infty}
is a highest weigth vector in the minimal
K
-type of
\pi_{\infty}
, we have a vector valued harmonic differential form
\omega_{\Psi}
whose cohomology class
[\omega_{\Psi}]
is an element of
H^{6}_{dR,c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}_{dR}^{\lambda})
. Poincaré duality induces maps

\langle\cdot,[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{B}:H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}_{B}^{\lambda}(3))\to{\bf C},

\langle\cdot,[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathscr{V}_{\mathcal{H}}^{\lambda}(4))\to{\bf C}.

The pairings
\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{B}
and
\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
are computed in terms of the residue of a certain adelic integral of Rankin-Selberg type considered in
[
PS18
]
. In
loc. cit.
it is shown that, if
\pi
supports certain Fourier coefficients associated to
F
, then the local factors at unramified places
v
of this integral represent the degree
8
local spin
L
-function
L(s,\pi_{v},\mathrm{Spin})
of
\pi_{v}
. The following result gives evidence for Conjecture
1.1
for the motive associated to
\pi
.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
5.11
)
.

Let
\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
such that
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series of Hodge type
(3,3)
in the discrete series
L
-packet of
V^{\lambda}
. Then

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{B}=\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=C\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})\right),

where
C
is an explicit non-zero constant independent of
\pi
,
S
is a sufficiently large set of places containing the ramified and archimedean places,
\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}=A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi
for some weight lowering operator
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}
defined in Proposition
4.8
,
\Phi
is a Schwartz-Bruhat function and
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},s)
is the integral defined in Theorem
5.8
.

Remark 1.3
.

We point out that, according to
[
GG09
, Proposition 12.1]
there exist a Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
and a vector
\Psi\in\pi
such that
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi,1)
is non-zero. However we do not know if this holds for
\Psi_{\infty}
in the minimal
K
-type of
\pi_{\infty}
.
Moreover, one can show that there exists a cusp form
\tilde{\Psi}\in\pi
, which coincides with
\Psi
at the archimedean place and away from
S
, such that

\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\tilde{\Psi}^{[\lambda,\mu]},s)=\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi_{\infty},\Psi_{\infty}^{[\lambda,\mu]},s).

Although we have not been able to calculate it, we expect that for a natural choice of
\Phi_{\infty}
the archimedean integral
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi_{\infty},\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}_{\infty},s)
is the Gamma factor of the Spin motive attached to
\pi
by the rule of Serre, and hence holomorphic and non-zero at
s=1
.

As a corollary of this theorem, one can deduce, under the additional assumption that
\pi
is the Steinberg representation at a finite place, a weak version of Conjecture
1.1
(1) (Corollary
5.15
) and Conjecture
1.1
(3) (Corollary
5.14
).

When
\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
is non-zero then (cf.
[
GS20
, Theorem 1.1]
)
\pi
is a weak functorial lift of a cuspidal automorphic representation
\sigma
of an exceptional group of type
G_{2}
. Moreover (cf. Proposition
8.1
), we have

\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})=L^{S}(1,\sigma,{\rm Std})\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\zeta^{S}(s).

Hence, up to controlling the value of the archimedean integral at
s=1
, Theorem
1.2
above gives a cohomological formula for the critical value
L^{S}(1,\sigma,{\rm Std})
.

Our second main result concerns the program of Gross and Savin on rank seven motives of Galois type
G_{2}
. The first step towards the conjecture of Gross and Savin was done by Kret and Shin in
[
KS22
]
, where they more generally constructed
\mathrm{GSpin}
-valued Galois representations associated to cohomological cuspidal automorphic forms of symplectic groups. Moreover, based on the calculations of
[
GS98
]
, they verified (
[
KS22
, Theorem 11.1]
) that, for suitable automorphic representations of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
in the image of the exceptional theta correspondence from the compact form
G_{2}^{c}
of type
G_{2}
, the image of their Galois representation lies actually in
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
. More precisely, let
\rho_{\pi}
be the
{\rm Spin}_{7}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
-valued Galois representation attached to
\pi
. Assuming that
\pi
is a non-trivial small theta lift of
\sigma
, we have

(1)

\mathrm{Spin}\circ\rho_{\pi}=\mathrm{Std}\circ\rho_{\sigma}\oplus\mathbf{1}

where
\mathrm{Std}\circ\rho_{\sigma}
is the standard Galois representation attached to
\sigma
and
\mathbf{1}
denotes the one dimensional trivial representation.

Remark 1.4
.

Technically speaking, only the dual pair
(G_{2}^{c},{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6})
is considered in
[
GS98
]
, but their conjecture also applies to the dual pair
(G_{2},{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6})
. Using the results of
[
KS22
]
and the study of the exceptional theta correspondence for
(G_{2},{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6})
(see Theorem
1.8
below), we construct (Theorem
8.3
), under some assumptions, Galois representations associated to cohomological cuspidal automorphic representations
\sigma
of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, which sit in a decomposition as that of Equation (
1
).

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
8.6
)
.

Let
\sigma
be an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}^{c}(\mathbf{A})
or
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
such that the big theta lift
\Theta(\sigma)
to
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
has an irreducible subquotient
\pi=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
, which is a cuspidal automorphic representation such that
\pi_{\infty}
is cohomological for
V
as above and
\pi_{p}
is the Steinberg representation for some prime number
p
. Assume that the integral
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)
is non-zero for some
\Phi
and
\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}
as above. Then, the trivial representation
\mathbf{1}
in (
1
) is generated by the étale realization of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
.

Remark 1.6
.

Note that the archimedean part
\pi_{\infty}
of
\pi
is not necessarily of Hodge type
(3,3)
. However, it is one of the main results of
[
KS22
]
that the
L
-packet of
\pi
is stable at infinity. In particular, there exists a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi^{3,3}=\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}\otimes\pi_{f}
whose archimedean part is cohomological and of Hodge type
(3,3)
and whose non-archimedean part is equivalent to
\pi_{f}
. In the integral appearing in the statement of Theorem
1.2
, the archimedean part of the cusp form
\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}
is a suitable vector in the minimal
K
-type of
\pi^{3,3}_{\infty}
.

Remark 1.7
.

In Proposition
8.4
we give a list of cases where
\sigma
is known to have a small theta lift
\pi=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
of
\sigma
to
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
which is a cuspidal automorphic representation such that
\pi_{\infty}
is cohomological for
V
as above and
\pi_{p}
is the Steinberg representation for some prime number
p
, as in the previous theorem.

We conclude this introduction explaining a result which provides cases where Theorem
1.5
can be applied and which has its own interest. Indeed, note that a necessary condition for the integral
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)
to be non-zero, is that
\pi
supports a rank
2
Fourier coefficient associated to
F
. By a result of Gan
[
Gan05
, Theorem 3.1]
, every cuspidal automorphic representation
\sigma
of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
supports a Fourier coefficient associated to an étale cubic algebra
E
.

Theorem 1.8

(Theorem
7.2
, Proposition
7.13
)
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
. Assume that

—

\sigma
is not globally generic;

—

\sigma_{p}
is generic at some finite place
p
.

Then the big theta lift
\Theta(\sigma)
is cuspidal. Moreover
\Theta(\sigma)
supports a rank
2
Fourier coefficient associated to
F
(and is in particular non-zero) if and only if
\sigma
supports a Fourier coefficient associated to
{\bf Q}\times F
.

1.3.
Overview of the proofs

The main difficulty for calculating the pairing of Theorem
1.2
between the motivic class and the cohomology class
[\omega_{\Psi}]
resides on the fact that the first class is constructed from the decomposition into irreducible components of the restriction of
V
to the subgroup
\mathbf{H}
, while the test vector is constructed from its restriction to the maximal compact subgroup
\mathrm{U}(3)
of
\mathbf{G}({\bf R})
. One needs to carefully study the relationship between these two different decompositions (Theorem
4.2
). As a consequence we get a formula for the pairing in terms of a period integral (Proposition
4.8
and Proposition
4.10
). These adelic integrals are in turn related to the residue of the partial Spin
L
-function of
\pi
by means of the work of Pollack and Shah (Proposition
5.10
), which allows to conclude the proof. Theorem
1.5
follows basically from Theorem
1.2
and
1.8
. The proof of Theorem
1.8
goes as follows. We first prove (Theorem
7.2
and Corollary
7.3
) that
\sigma
lifts to a cuspidal representation using the tower of exceptional correspondences for
G_{2}
studied in
[
GRS97b
]
, which reduces the problem to the vanishing of certain automorphic period integrals. Finally, we establish (Proposition
7.13
) a correspondence between Fourier coefficients of
\sigma
and its theta lift, which in particular implies the non-vanishing of the latter.

1.4.
Structure of the manuscript

In section
2
we fix notation, conventions, and basic results that will be useful in the body of the article. In particular, we prove that, under some mild assumptions, the localization at a maximal ideal of the Hecke algebra of the cohomology of the Siegel sixfold is cuspidal and concentrated in the middle degree. We also introduce Absolute Hodge cohomology and compute the dimension of its
\pi_{f}
-isotypical component. In section
3
we explain the construction of the motivic class
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
and its realizations. In Section
4
we construct the harmonic differential form
\omega_{\Psi}
associated to a suitable cuspidal form
\Psi
in the space of
\pi
and we prove our first main result concerning the calculation of the pairing between the motivic class and the cohomology class
[\omega_{\Psi}]
. In Section
5
, we use the results of Pollack and Shah to relate the pairing to the residue of the Spin
L
-function. Sections
6
and
7
are devoted to the study of the exceptional theta correspondence between
G_{2}
and
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
and contain the proof of Theorem
1.8
. Finally, in Section
8
we relate the pairing to a critical value of the standard
L
-function of
G_{2}
. We also deduce from the work of Kret and Shin the existence of Galois representations attached to certain cuspidal representations of
G_{2}
and we conclude with a proof of Theorem
1.5
.

1.5.
Acknowledgements

We would like to heartily thank David Ginzburg for sharing with us the proof of Lemma
7.1
. We also thank Nadir Matringe, Aaron Pollack and Armando Gutierrez for fruitful exchanges. We thank Marc-Hubert Nicole and Vincent Pilloni for comments on an earlier draft of the article. Finally, we thank the anonymous referee for his/her careful reading of the manuscript, comments and corrections which have significantly improved the content of this article.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Algebraic groups and algebraic representations

Let
\psi
denote an antisymmetric non-degenerate bilinear form on a finite dimensional
{\bf Q}
-vector space
V
. The symplectic group
{\mathrm{GSp}}(V,\psi)
is the
{\bf Q}
-group scheme defined by

{\mathrm{GSp}}(V,\psi)=\{g\in\mathrm{GL}(V)\,|\,\forall v,w\in V,\psi(gv,gw)=\nu(g)\psi(v,w),\nu(g)\in\mathbf{G}_{m}\}

Then
\nu:{\mathrm{GSp}}(V,\psi)\rightarrow\mathbf{G}_{m}
is a character. Let
I_{n}
denote the identity matrix of size
n
. When
V
is the
{\bf Q}
-vector space
{\bf Q}^{2n}
endowed with the bilinear form whose matrix is
J={{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
, we let
{\mathrm{GSp}}_{2n}
denote
{\mathrm{GSp}}({\bf Q}^{2n},J)
and we let
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{2n}
denote
\ker\nu
. In this paper, we are mainly interested in the case
n=3
. Hence we will denote by
\mathbf{G}
the group
{\mathrm{GSp}}_{6}
and by
\mathbf{G}_{0}
the group
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{6}
. Let
\mathbf{T}\subset\mathbf{G}
denote the maximal diagonal torus and
\mathbf{B}\subset\mathbf{G}
denote the standard Borel. We have

\mathbf{T}=\left\{\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1}^{-1}\nu,\alpha_{2}^{-1}\nu,\alpha_{3}^{-1}\nu),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\nu\in\mathbf{G}_{m}\right\}.

We associate to any
4
-uple
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)\in{\bf Z}^{4}
such that
c\equiv\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}\pmod{2}
the algebraic character
\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)
of
\mathbf{T}
defined by

\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c):\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1}^{-1}\nu,\alpha_{2}^{-1}\nu,\alpha_{3}^{-1}\nu)\mapsto\alpha_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\alpha_{3}^{\lambda_{3}}\nu^{\frac{c-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}{2}}.

This defines an isomorphism between the group of
4
-uples
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)\in{\bf Z}^{4}
such that
c\equiv\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}\pmod{2}
and the group of algebraic characters of
\mathbf{T}
. Let
\rho_{1}=\lambda(1,-1,0,0)
and
\rho_{2}=\lambda(0,1,-1,0)
denote the short simple roots and let
\rho_{3}=\lambda(0,0,2,0)
denote the long simple root. The set of roots of
\mathbf{T}
in
\mathbf{G}
is
R=R^{+}\cup R^{-}
where

R^{+}=\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{1}+\rho_{2},\rho_{2}+\rho_{3},\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}+\rho_{3},\rho_{1}+2\rho_{2}+\rho_{3},2\rho_{1}+2\rho_{2}+\rho_{3},2\rho_{2}+\rho_{3},\rho_{3}\}

is the set of positive roots with respect to
\mathbf{B}
and
R^{-}=-R^{+}
. A weight
\lambda=\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)
is dominant for
\mathbf{B}
if
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq 0
. For any such
\lambda
, there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible algebraic representation
V^{\lambda}
of
\mathbf{G}
of highest weight
\lambda
and every irreducible algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}
is obtained in this way (up to isomorphism). Similarly, irreducible algebraic representations of
{\mathrm{GSp}}_{4}
are classified by their highest weight which is a character of the shape
\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},c)
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq 0
and
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\equiv c\pmod{2}
(see for example
[
Lem17
, §2.3]
for more details). We will also use the classification of irreducible algebraic representations of the groups
\mathbf{G}_{0}={\mathrm{Sp}}_{6}
and
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
. Hence let us recall that the diagonal maximal torus
\mathbf{T}_{0}=\mathbf{T}\cap\mathbf{G}_{0}
of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
is

\mathbf{T}_{0}=\left\{\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1}^{-1},\alpha_{2}^{-1},\alpha_{3}^{-1}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\mathbf{G}_{m}\right\}.

and that its group of algebraic characters is isomorphic to
{\bf Z}^{3}
via
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\mapsto\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
where

(2)

\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}):\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1}^{-1},\alpha_{2}^{-1},\alpha_{3}^{-1})\mapsto\alpha_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\alpha_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\alpha_{3}^{\lambda_{3}}.

A weight
\lambda=\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
is dominant with respect to the standard Borel
\mathbf{B}_{0}=\mathbf{B}\cap\mathbf{G}_{0}
if
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq 0
and for any such
\lambda
there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible algebraic representation
V^{\lambda}
of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
of highest weight
\lambda
and every irreducible algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
is obtained in this way (up to isomorphism). Similarly, irreducible algebraic representations of
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
are classified by characters
\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}
, with obvious notation.

2.2.
Compact Lie groups and representations

Let
\mathrm{U}(n)=\{g\in\mathrm{GL}_{n}({\bf C})\,|\,^{t}\overline{g}g=I_{n}\}
denote the unitary group and let
K_{\infty}\subset\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
be the subgroup defined as

K_{\infty}=\left\{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
-B&A\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\;|\;AA^{t}+BB^{t}=1,AB^{t}=BA^{t}\right\}.

We have an isomorphism
\kappa:\mathrm{U}(3)\simeq K_{\infty}
defined by
A+iB\mapsto{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
-B&A\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
. In fact
K_{\infty}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
. Let
T_{\infty}\subset K_{\infty}
denote
\{\kappa(\mathrm{diag}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})),z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}\in\mathrm{U}(1)\}
. Then
T_{\infty}
is Cartan subgroup of
K_{\infty}
. Its group of algebraic characters is isomorphic to
{\bf Z}^{3}
via
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\mapsto\lambda^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
, where

\lambda^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}):\kappa(\mathrm{diag}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}))\mapsto z_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}z_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}z_{3}^{\lambda_{3}}.

An algebraic character is dominant if
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}
. For any dominant integral weight
\lambda^{\prime}
, there exists a unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible representation
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
of
K_{\infty}
in a finite dimensional
{\bf C}
-vector space and every irreducible representation of
K_{\infty}
is obtained in this way (up to isomorphism). In what follows, we will simply denote the irreducible representation of highest weight
\lambda^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
by
\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
. Let us explain the connection between the weights
\lambda
of
\mathbf{T}_{0}
defined by equation (
2
) in the previous section and the weights
\lambda^{\prime}
defined above. Let
J\in\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf C})
denote the matrix
J=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{3}&iI_{3}\\
iI_{3}&I_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
. Then we have

J^{-1}\kappa(\mathrm{diag}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}))J=\mathrm{diag}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})

and so, for any
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in{\bf Z}^{3}
, we have

\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})(J^{-1}\kappa(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})J)=\lambda^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})(\mathrm{diag}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})).

In brief, the character
\lambda^{\prime}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
of
T_{\infty}
is conjugated to the restriction of
\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
to
\mathrm{U}(1)^{3}\subset{\bf C}^{\times}\times{\bf C}^{\times}\times{\bf C}^{\times}=\mathbf{T}_{0}({\bf C})
.

2.3.
Lie algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, resp.
\mathfrak{k}
, denote the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
, resp.
K_{\infty}
, and let
\mathfrak{g}_{0,{\bf C}}
, resp.
\mathfrak{k}_{\bf C}
, denote its complexification. Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\in M_{6}({\bf R})\;|\;B=B^{t},C=C^{t},A=-D^{t}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
-B&A\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\in M_{6}({\bf R})\;|\;A=-A^{t},B=B^{t}\right\}.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
is the
1
-eigenspace for the Cartan involution
\theta(X)=-X^{t}
. The
(-1)
-eigenspace is
\mathfrak{p}=\left\{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&B\\
B&-A\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\in M_{6}({\bf R})\;|\;A=A^{t},B=B^{t}\right\}.
Letting

\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{\pm}=\left\{{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}A&\pm iA\\
\pm iA&-A\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\in M_{6}({\bf C})\;|\;A=A^{t}\right\},

we have
\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}=\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{-}
and one has the Cartan decomposition

\mathfrak{g}_{0,{\bf C}}=\mathfrak{k}_{{\bf C}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{{\bf C}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}^{-}_{{\bf C}}.

For
1\leq j\leq 3
, let
D_{j}\in M_{3}({\bf C})
be the matrix with entry
1
at position
(j,j)
and
0
elsewhere. Define

T_{j}={{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&D_{j}\\
-D_{j}&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
. Then the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
of
T_{\infty}
is
\mathfrak{h}={\bf R}\cdot T_{1}\oplus{\bf R}\cdot T_{2}\oplus{\bf R}\cdot T_{3}
. This is a compact Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Let
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
denote the basis of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}_{\bf C}
dual to
(-iT_{1},-iT_{2},-iT_{3})
. A system of positive roots for
(\mathfrak{g}_{0,{\bf C}},\mathfrak{h}_{\bf C})
is then given by

\{e_{1}\pm e_{2},e_{1}\pm e_{3},e_{2}\pm e_{3},2e_{1},2e_{2},2e_{3}\}.

The simple roots are
e_{1}-e_{2},e_{2}-e_{3}
and
2e_{3}
. We note that
\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{{\bf C}}
is spanned by the root spaces corresponding to the positive roots of type
2e_{j}
and
e_{j}+e_{k}
. We denote
\Delta=\{\pm 2e_{j},\pm(e_{j}\pm e_{k})\}
the set of all roots,
\Delta_{\mathrm{c}}=\{\pm(e_{j}-e_{k})\}
the set of compact roots and
\Delta_{\rm nc}=\Delta-\Delta_{\rm c}
the non-compact roots. Finally, we note
\Delta^{+},\Delta_{\rm c}^{+}
and
\Delta_{\rm nc}^{+}
the set of positive, positive compact and positive non-compact roots, respectively.

2.4.
Weyl groups

Recall that the Weyl group of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
is given by
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}=\{\pm 1\}^{3}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{3}
. The reflection
\sigma_{j}
in the hyperplane orthogonal to
2e_{j}
simply reverses the sign of
e_{j}
while leaving the other
e_{k}
fixed. The reflection
\sigma_{jk}
in the hyperplane orthogonal to
e_{j}-e_{k}
exchanges
e_{j}
and
e_{k}
and leaves the remaining
e_{\ell}
fixed. The Weyl group
\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}
of
K_{\infty}\cong U(3)
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
and, via the embedding into
\mathbf{G}
, identifies with the subgroup of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}
generated by the
\sigma_{jk}
. With the identification
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}=N(\mathbf{T}_{0})/Z(\mathbf{T}_{0})
, an explicit description of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}
is given as follows. The matrices corresponding to the reflections
\sigma_{jk}
are
{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}S_{jk}&0\\
0&-S_{jk}\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
,
where
S_{jk}
is the matrix with entry
1
at places
(\ell,\ell)
,
\ell\neq j,k
,
(k,j)
and
(j,k)
and zeroes elsewhere. The matrices corresponding to the reflection
\sigma_{j}
in the hyperplane orthogonal to
2e_{j}
are of the form

{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&U_{j}\\
-U_{j}&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
,
where
U_{j}
denotes the diagonal matrix with
-1
at the place
(j,j)
and ones at the other entries of the diagonal. This gives an explicit description of the elements of
\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}
and their length:

\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}=\{1,\sigma_{12},\sigma_{13},\sigma_{23},\sigma_{12}\sigma_{13},\sigma_{12}\sigma_{23}\}\xrightarrow{\ell(\bullet)}\{0,1,1,1,2,2\}.

2.5.
Discrete series

We recall standard facts on discrete series for
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})={\mathrm{Sp}}_{6}({\bf R})
and for
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
. For any non-singular weight
\Lambda
define

\Delta^{+}(\Lambda):=\{\alpha\in\Delta\;|\;\langle\alpha,\Lambda\rangle>0\},\;\;\;\Delta^{+}_{c}(\Lambda)=\Delta^{+}(\Lambda)\cap\Delta_{c},

where
\langle\;,\;\rangle
is the standard scalar product on
{\bf R}^{3}
. Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
be a weight of
T_{\infty}
such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq 0
and let
\rho=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}\alpha=(3,2,1)
. As
|\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}/\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}|=8
, by
[
Kna01
, Theorem 9.20]
, the set of equivalence classes of irreducible discrete series representations of
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
with Harish-Chandra parameter
\lambda+\rho
contains
8
elements. More precisely, choose representatives
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{8}\}
of
\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}/\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}
of increasing length and such that for any
1\leq i\leq 8
, then the weight
w_{i}(\lambda+\rho)
is dominant for
K_{\infty}
. The representatives, defined by their action on
\rho
, are
w_{1}(3,2,1)=(3,2,1)
,
w_{2}(3,2,1)=(3,2,-1)
,
w_{3}(3,2,1)=(3,1,-2)
,
w_{4}(3,2,1)=(2,1,-3)
,
w_{5}(3,2,1)=(3,-1,-2)
,
w_{6}(3,2,1)=(2,-1,-3)
,
w_{7}(3,2,1)=(1,-2,-3)
,
w_{8}(3,2,1)=(-1,-2,-3)
. Then, for any
1\leq i\leq 8
, there exists an irreducible discrete series
\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}
, where
\Lambda=w_{i}(\lambda+\rho)
, of Harish-Chandra parameter
\Lambda
and containing with multiplicity
1
the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type with highest weight
\Lambda+\delta_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}-2\delta_{K_{\infty}}
where
\delta_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}
, resp.
\delta_{K_{\infty}}
, is the half-sum of roots, resp. of compact roots, which are positive with respect to the Weyl chamber in which
\Lambda
lies, i.e.,
2\delta_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(\Lambda)}\alpha
,
2\delta_{K_{\infty}}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}_{c}(\Lambda)}\alpha
.
Moreover, for
i\neq j
,
\Lambda=w_{i}(\lambda+\rho)
,
\Lambda=w_{j}(\lambda+\rho)
, the representations
\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}
and
\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}
are not equivalent and any discrete series of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
is obtained in this way. Let
V^{\lambda}
be the irreducible algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
of highest weight
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
(for
\mathbf{T}_{0}
).

Definition 2.1
.

The discrete series
L
-packet
P(V^{\lambda})
associated to
\lambda
is the set of isomorphism classes of discrete series of
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
whose Harish-Chandra parameter is of the form
\Lambda=w_{i}(\lambda+\rho)
as
i
varies.

By
[
BW00
, Theorem II.5.3]
, for each
\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}\in P(V^{\lambda})
, the space

{\rm Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{0,{\bf C}}/\mathfrak{k}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}\right)

has dimension 1.
This is a consequence of the fact (cf. the proof of
[
BW00
, Theorem II.5.3]
) that the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type of
\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}
appears uniquely in
\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{0,{\bf C}}/\mathfrak{k}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda}
. Using the Cartan decomposition, we get

\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{0,{\bf C}}/\mathfrak{k}_{{\bf C}}=\bigoplus_{p+q=6}\bigwedge^{p}\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{{\bf C}}\otimes_{\bf C}\bigwedge^{q}\mathfrak{p}^{-}_{{\bf C}}.

Hence, there exists a unique pair
(p,q)
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{p}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{+}\otimes\bigwedge^{q}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{-}\otimes V^{\lambda},\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}\right)
is non-zero
and hence of dimension one. We call such a pair
(p,q)
the Hodge type of
\pi_{\infty}^{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There exist two elements
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
and
\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
in
P(V^{\lambda})
of Hodge type
(3,3)
. They are characterized by having Harish-Chandra parameters
(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+1,-\lambda_{1}-3)
and
(\lambda_{1}+3,-\lambda_{3}-1,-\lambda_{2}-2)
and minimal
K_{\infty}
-types
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+2,-\lambda_{1}-4)}
and
\tau_{(\lambda_{1}+4,-\lambda_{3}-2,-\lambda_{2}-2)}
respectively.

Proof.

The discrete series
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
and
\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
correspond to the Weyl representatives
w_{4}
and
w_{5}
. Since
w_{4}\lambda=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1})
and
w_{5}\lambda=(\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2})
, the Harish-Chandra parameters of
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
and
\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
are as desired. When
\Lambda=w_{4}(\lambda+\rho)
(resp.
\Lambda=w_{5}(\lambda+\rho)
), observe that
\delta_{\mathbf{G}_{0}}
equals to
(2,1,-3)
(resp.
(3,-1,-2)
), while
\delta_{K_{\infty}}=(1,0,-1)
in both cases. Hence, using the formula above, the minimal
K_{\infty}
-types of
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
and
\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
are
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+2,-\lambda_{1}-4)}
and
\tau_{(\lambda_{1}+4,-\lambda_{3}-2,-\lambda_{2}-2)}
respectively.

Recall that, after
[
VZ84
, Proposition 6.19]
, the Hodge type of a discrete series representation of Harish-Chandra parameter
\Lambda
is
(p,q)
, where
p
(resp.
q
) is the number of positive non-compact roots in
\Delta^{+}(\Lambda)
(resp.
\Delta^{-}(\Lambda)
). Using this, one easily checks that the Hodge type of
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
and
\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
is
(3,3)
.

∎

The picture for
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
is similar, but the set of its Harish-Chandra parameters changes slightly. This is due to the fact that, since its maximal compact subgroup has two connected components, the set of parameters has to be considered up to the action of
\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}
and of
w_{8}
, as the latter, which is the anti-diagonal matrix with all entries
-1
, now belongs to the connected component away from the identity of the maximal compact subgroup. Concretely, any parameter
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})
has to be identified with
w_{8}\mu=(-\mu_{3},-\mu_{2},-\mu_{1})
.
If
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
is such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq 0
and
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
, then the irreducible algebraic
\mathbf{G}
-representation
V^{(\lambda,0)}
of highest weight
\lambda(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},0)
defines a representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
. The corresponding discrete series
L
-packet
P(V^{(\lambda,0)})
for
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
has thus four elements. Any element
\pi_{\infty}\in P(V^{(\lambda,0)})
of Harish-Chandra parameter
\mu
, viewed as a
\mathbf{G}({\bf R})
-representation, decomposes when restricted to
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
as the direct sum of two discrete series in
P(V^{\lambda})
of Harish-Chandra parameters
\mu
and
w_{8}\mu
. As a consequence, for any such
\pi_{\infty}
, the space

H^{6}\left(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{(\lambda,0)}\right)=\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{{\bf C}}/\mathrm{Lie}(K_{\mathbf{G}})_{{\bf C}},\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{(\lambda,0)}\right),

where
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbf{G})
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\bf C}
is its complexification and
K_{\mathbf{G}}={\bf R}_{+}^{\times}K_{\infty}
, is
2
-dimensional. The discussion above implies the following.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
be a dominant weight for
\mathbf{G}_{0}
such that
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
. Then there exists a unique discrete series
\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}\in P(V^{(\lambda,0)})
of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
, with Harish-Chandra parameter
(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+1,-\lambda_{1}-3)
, such that

{\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}}_{|_{\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})}}=\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}\oplus\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}.

We will refer to
\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}
as the discrete series of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
in
P(V^{(\lambda,0)})
of Hodge type
(3,3)
.

2.6.
Shimura varieties

Let
F
denote a real étale quadratic
{\bf Q}
-algebra, i.e.
F
is either a totally real quadratic extension of
{\bf Q}
or
{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
.
Denote by
\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}/{\bf Q}
the subgroup scheme of
{\rm Res}_{F/{\bf Q}}\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}
sitting in the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Res}_{F/{\bf Q}}\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm det}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{G}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Res}_{F/{\bf Q}}\mathbf{G}_{m,F}.}

For instance, when
F={\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
, we have

\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}\;|\;{\rm det}(g_{1})={\rm det}(g_{2})\}.

Let
\mathbf{H}
denote the group

(3)

\mathbf{H}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}\boxtimes\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}\;|\;{\rm det}(g_{1})={\rm det}(g_{2})\}.

We embed
\mathbf{H}
into
\mathbf{G}
as follows. Let us consider the
{\bf Q}\times F
-module

V:={\bf Q}e_{1}\oplus Fe_{2}\oplus{\bf Q}f_{1}\oplus Ff_{2},

where
V_{1}:={\bf Q}e_{1}\oplus{\bf Q}f_{1}
and
V_{2}:=Fe_{2}\oplus Ff_{2}
are respectively the standard representations of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and
\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}.
We equip
V
with the
{\bf Q}\times F
-valued alternating form
\psi^{\prime}:V\times V\to{\bf Q}\times F,
such that
\psi^{\prime}(e_{1},f_{1})=(1,0)
,
\psi^{\prime}(e_{2},f_{2})=(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
and
V_{1}
is orthogonal to
V_{2}
. The group
\mathbf{H}
acts naturally on
V
and preserves
\psi^{\prime}
up to a scalar.
We can regard
V
as a
6
-dimensional
{\bf Q}
-vector space with
{\bf Q}
-valued symplectic form
\psi:={\rm tr}_{({\bf Q}\times F)/{\bf Q}}\circ\psi^{\prime}
.
Explicitly, we have

\psi(ae_{1}+\alpha e_{2},bf_{1}+\beta f_{2})=ab+\tfrac{1}{2}{\rm tr}_{F/{\bf Q}}(\alpha\beta).

This identification defines an embedding
\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{GSp}}(V,\psi)
.
We now identify
{\mathrm{GSp}}(V,\psi)
with
\mathbf{G}
by choosing a suitable
{\bf Q}
-basis of
V
. Recall that the set of real quadratic
{\bf Q}
-algebras is parametrized by
D\in{\bf Q}^{\times}_{>0}/({\bf Q}^{\times}_{>0})^{2},
via
D\mapsto F={\bf Q}\oplus{\bf Q}\sqrt{D}
. Using the decomposition
F={\bf Q}\oplus{\bf Q}\sqrt{D}
, we consider the
{\bf Q}
-basis of
V
given by

\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}:=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\sqrt{D}e_{2},f_{1},f_{2},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{D}}f_{2}\right\}.

In this basis,
\psi
is represented by the matrix
J={{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
, thus we obtain and isomorphism
{\mathrm{GSp}}(V,\psi)\simeq\mathbf{G}
and the embedding

\iota:\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}.

Note that the group

\mathbf{H}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{GL}_{2}\boxtimes{\mathrm{GSp}}_{4}:=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times{\mathrm{GSp}}_{4}\;|\;{\rm det}(g_{1})={\nu}(g_{2})\},

is also naturally embedded in
\mathbf{G}
and
\iota
factors through
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
.

Recall from
[
BGCLRJ23
, §2.2]
that there is a 3-dimensional Shimura variety
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}
associated to the
\mathbf{H}({\bf R})
-conjugacy class of

h:\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow\mathbf{H}_{/{\bf R}},\quad x+iy\mapsto\left({{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{smallmatrix}\big)}},{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{smallmatrix}\big)}},{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\right),

where
\mathbf{S}={\rm Res}_{{\bf C}/{\bf R}}{\mathbf{G}_{m}}_{/{\bf C}}
is the Deligne torus. The associated Shimura datum has reflex field is
{\bf Q}
and the Shimura variety
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}
can be described as follows. If
V\subseteq\mathbf{H}({\bf A}_{f})
is a fibre product (over the similitude characters)
V_{1}\times_{\mathbf{A}^{\times}_{f}}V_{2}
of sufficiently small subgroups, we have

{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(V)={\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathrm{GL}_{2}}(V_{1})\times_{\mathbf{G}_{m}}{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}}(V_{2}),

where
\times_{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
denotes the fibre product over the zero dimensional Shimura variety of level
W={\rm det}(V_{1})=\mathrm{det}(V_{2})
. The connected components are given by

\pi_{0}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(V)({\bf C}))=\hat{{\bf Z}}^{\times}/W.

Hence,
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}
can be thought as the fibre product of a modular curve and a Hilbert-Blumenthal modular surface.
We also recall that the complex points of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(V)
are given by

{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(V)({\bf C})=\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})/{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf R})K_{\mathbf{H},\infty}V,

where
{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{H}}
denotes the center of
\mathbf{H}
and
K_{\mathbf{H},\infty}\subseteq\mathbf{H}({\bf R})
is the maximal compact defined as the product
\mathrm{U}(1)\times\mathrm{U}(1)\times\mathrm{U}(1)
.

The embedding
\iota:\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
induces a Shimura datum for
\mathbf{G}
whose reflex field is
{\bf Q}
.
For any sufficiently small compact open subgroup
U
of
\mathbf{G}({\bf A}_{f})
, denote by
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
the associated Shimura variety of dimension
6
.
We also write
\iota:{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U\cap\mathbf{H})\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
the closed embedding of codimension
3
induced by the group homomorphism
\iota:\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
.

Remark 2.4
.

If
E/{\bf Q}
is a totally real cubic field extension of
{\bf Q}
then one can analogously define
\mathbf{H}=\{g\in{\rm Res}_{E/{\bf Q}}\mathrm{GL}_{2,E}\;|\;\mathrm{det}(g)\in\mathbf{G}_{m}\}
and there is a natural embedding
\iota:\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
(cf.
[
PSR87
, §1]
for details) inducing closed embeddings
\iota:{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U\cap\mathbf{H})\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
for sufficiently small open compact
U
. All our results up to Section §5 will hold for any real étale cubic algebra
E
over
{\bf Q}
. Our main interest in the case
E={\bf Q}\times F
for
F
a real étale quadratic algebra over
{\bf Q}
is motivated by the integral representation of the Spin
L
-function of
\mathbf{G}
of
[
PS18
]
.

2.7.
Cohomology of Siegel sixfolds

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
having non-zero fixed vectors by a neat compact open group
U\subseteq\mathbf{G}({\bf A}_{f})
. We assume that
\pi
has trivial central character and hence we regard it as a cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
. Our purpose is to establish that, under mild assumptions, suitable localizations at
\pi
of cuspidal,
L^{2}
, inner Betti and Betti cohomologies coincide and are concentrated in the middle degree. The assumptions are the following.

(DS)

the archimedean component
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
,

(St)

at a finite place
p
the component
\pi_{p}
is the Steinberg representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf Q}_{p})
.

Let us fix for the rest of this section
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})\in{\bf Z}^{3}
satisfying
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq 0
and
\sum\lambda_{i}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
. We will denote by
V
, without mentioning
\lambda
anymore, the irreducible algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}
of highest weight
(\lambda,0)
. As
V
has trivial central character, it will be considered as an irreducible representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
. Then
\pi_{\infty}
belongs to the discrete series
L
-packet
P(V)
. As a consequence

H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V)=\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{{\bf C}}/\mathrm{Lie}(K_{\mathbf{G}})_{\bf C};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V\right)\neq 0,

where
K_{\mathbf{G}}={\bf R}_{+}^{\times}K_{\infty}
.

There are natural inclusions of spaces of
{\bf C}
-valued functions

\mathcal{C}_{\rm cusp}^{\infty}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}))\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\rm rd}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}))\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{(2)}^{\infty}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}))\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})),

where these spaces denote, respectively, the space of cuspidal square-integrable functions, rapidly decreasing functions, square-integrable functions and smooth functions, and

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\rm c/center}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}))\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\rm rd}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})),

where the first space is the space of compactly supported modulo the center functions (for the precise definition of these spaces, we refer to
[
Bor80b
]
). Tensoring by
V
the inclusions above and applying the
(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}})
-cohomology functor, we obtain the natural maps

\textstyle{H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\bullet}_{\rm rd}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C}),}
\textstyle{H^{\bullet}_{\rm c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where
\mathcal{V}_{\bf C}
is the
{\bf C}
-local system associated to
V
. Let
H^{\bullet}_{!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})
denote the image of
H^{\bullet}_{c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})
in
H^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})
. Let
N
denote the positive integer defined as the product of prime numbers
\ell
such that
\pi_{\ell}
is ramified.
The fact that
\pi_{\infty}
is cohomological implies that there exists a number field
L
whose ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{L}
contains the eigenvalues of the spherical Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}^{sph,N}
away from
N
and with coefficients in
{\bf Z}
acting on
\bigotimes^{\prime}_{\ell\nmid N}\pi_{\ell}^{\mathbf{G}({\bf Z}_{\ell})}
. Let
\mathcal{H}^{sph,N}_{L}
denote the spherical Hecke algebra away from
N
with coefficients in
L
, let
\theta_{\pi}:\mathcal{H}^{sph,N}_{L}\to L
denote the Hecke character of
\pi
and let
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}:={\rm ker}(\theta_{\pi})
. Considering the localization at
\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}
of the above cohomology groups, we have the following result.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\pi
satisfy the hypothesis
(DS)
and
(St)
above. Then

H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}_{!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}.

Proof.

By
[
Bor80b
, Theorem 5.3 & Corollary 5.5]
, the compositions of the horizontal maps

H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})\hookrightarrow H^{\bullet}_{*}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C}),

for
*\in\{{\rm rd},(2),\emptyset\}
, are injections. By
[
Bor80b
, Theorem 5.2]
, one has an isomorphism

H^{\bullet}_{\rm c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})\cong H^{\bullet}_{\rm rd}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C}).

Hence, if the equality
H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}
holds, we have

H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}_{!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}.

We show the former equality as follows. By
[
Bor80a
, §4]
,

(4)

H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})=\bigoplus_{\sigma\subset L^{2}_{d}}\sigma_{f}^{U}\otimes H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\sigma_{\infty}\otimes V)^{m(\sigma)},

where
\sigma
runs over the set of isomorphism classes of automorphic representations appearing in the discrete spectrum
L^{2}_{d}
of
L^{2}(Z(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}))
. Similarly,

H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})=\bigoplus_{\sigma\subset L^{2}_{0}}\sigma_{f}^{U}\otimes H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\sigma_{\infty}\otimes V)^{m_{0}(\sigma)},

where
\sigma
runs over the set of isomorphism classes of automorphic representations in the cuspidal spectrum
L^{2}_{0}\subset L^{2}_{d}
. From (
4
), we can write

H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=\bigoplus_{\sigma=\sigma_{\infty}\otimes\sigma_{f}}\sigma_{f}^{U}\otimes H^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\sigma_{\infty}\otimes V)^{m(\sigma)},

where
\sigma\in L^{2}_{d}
is such that
\sigma_{\ell}^{\mathbf{G}({\bf Z}_{\ell})}\simeq\pi_{\ell}^{\mathbf{G}({\bf Z}_{\ell})}\neq 0
at all
\ell\nmid N
. Notice that the latter implies that
\sigma_{f}^{N}\simeq\pi^{N}_{f}
, where for any automorphic representation
\tau
we have denoted
\tau_{f}^{N}=\otimes_{\ell\nmid N}\tau_{\ell}
.
By
[
KS22
, Lemma 8.1(2)]
, the Steinberg condition implies that the representation
\pi_{\ell}
is tempered and unitary at each
\ell\nmid N
(as
\pi
has trivial central character). Thus, if
\sigma
contributes non-trivially to the above sum, its local component at a finite place
\ell\nmid N
is tempered. This implies that
\sigma
is necessarily cuspidal and thus appears in
H^{\bullet}_{\rm cusp}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}},\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}
with multiplicity
m_{0}(\sigma)=m(\sigma)
. This last statement follows from the fact that any non-cuspidal automorphic representation appearing in
L^{2}_{d}
is obtained as a residue of an Eisenstein series and in particular it is non-tempered at every place (cf.
[
Lab99
, Proposition 4.5.4]
). We are left to show that

H^{\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}.

Recall that Franke’s decreasing filtration on the space of automorphic forms for
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
(cf.
[
Wal97
, §4.7]
) yields a spectral sequence
E_{1}^{p,q}\Rightarrow H^{p+q}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})
, where

E_{1}^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(w,P)\in B(p)\\
\ell(w)\leq p+q\end{subarray}}\bigoplus_{\sigma=\sigma_{\infty}\otimes\sigma_{f}}({\rm Ind}_{P(\mathbf{A}_{f})}^{\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}_{f})}\sigma_{f})^{U}\otimes H^{p+q-\ell(w)}(\mathfrak{m},K_{M};\sigma_{\infty}\otimes W^{w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho}),

where, for all
p\in{\bf Z}_{\geq 0}
,
B(p)
denotes a certain subset depending on
p
of elements
(w,P)
(cf.
[
Wal97
, §4.8]
), with
w\in\mathfrak{W}_{\mathbf{G}}
and
P=M\cdot U_{P}
a standard parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
,
W^{w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho}
denotes the irreducible algebraic representation of
M
of highest weight
{w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho}
, and
\sigma
runs over the set of isomorphism classes of automorphic representations appearing in the discrete spectrum of
L^{2}(Z_{M}(\mathbf{A})M({\bf Q})\backslash M(\mathbf{A}))
. By the proof of
[
KS22
, Lemma 8.1(1)]
, we have that
E^{p,q}_{1,\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}
are zero unless when
(w,P)=(1,\mathbf{G})
, in which case there exists a unique
p_{0}\in{\bf Z}_{\geq 0}
, for which

E^{p,q}_{1,\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=\begin{cases}H^{p+q}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}&\text{ if }p=p_{0},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Thus, the spectral sequence for the localization degenerates at the first page and gives

H^{p_{0}+\bullet}_{(2)}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=E^{p_{0},\bullet}_{1,\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{p_{0}+\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}.

∎

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
\pi
satisfy the hypothesis
(DS)
and
(St)
above. Then, we have

H^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}=H^{6}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}\neq 0.

Proof.

Suppose that
\tau_{f}
contributes to
H^{i}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}
. As we noted in the proof of Proposition
2.5
, this implies that, for every
\ell\nmid N
,
\tau_{\ell}\simeq\pi_{\ell}
is tempered and unitary (cf.
[
KS22
, Lemma 8.1(2)]
).
Let us fix
\ell\nmid N
; the action of the Frobenius correspondence on intersection cohomology
{\rm Frob}_{\ell}
on
IH^{i}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})[\tau_{f}]
and thus on
H^{i}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})[\tau_{f}]
is pure of weight
i
, i.e. its eigenvalues all have absolute value
\ell^{i/2}
(cf.
[
Mor10
, Remark 7.2.5]
). On the other hand, by the congruence relation conjectured in
[
BR94
, §6]
and verified in
[
Wed00
]
,
{\rm Frob}_{\ell}
is a root of the Hecke polynomial

H_{\ell}(T):={\rm det}(T-\ell^{3}{\rm spin}({\rm Fr}_{\ell}\ltimes\hat{g})),

which is a polynomial in
T
whose coefficients are elements in the coordinate ring of the set of
{\rm Fr}_{\ell}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple elements of
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}({\bf C})=\mathbf{GSpin}_{7}({\bf C})
, for
{\rm Fr}_{\ell}
a Frobenius element in the Weil group of
{\bf Q}_{\ell}
. By the untwisted Satake isomorphism, we can see
H_{\ell}(T)
as a polynomial with coefficients in the spherical Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q}_{\ell})//\mathbf{G}({\bf Z}_{\ell}),{\bf Q})
(cf.
[
Wed00
, (2.2.1) & Corollary (2.8)]
) and thus we can denote by
H_{\ell}(T;\tau_{\ell})
the specialization of
H_{\ell}(T)
to
\tau_{\ell}
, i.e.

H_{\ell}(T;\tau_{\ell})={\rm det}(T-\ell^{3}{\rm spin}(\phi_{\tau_{\ell}}({\rm Fr}_{\ell}))),

where
\phi_{\tau_{\ell}}
is the unramified Langlands parameter of
\tau_{\ell}
. The congruence relation gives that
H_{\ell}({\rm Frob}_{\ell};\tau_{\ell})=0
on
IH^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})[\tau_{f}]
, which implies that the eigenvalues of
{\rm Frob}_{\ell}
on
IH^{\bullet}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})[\tau_{f}]
are a subset of the ones of
\ell^{3}{\rm spin}(\phi_{\tau_{\ell}}({\rm Frob}_{\ell}))
. As
\tau_{\ell}
is tempered, all the eigenvalues of
{\rm spin}(\phi_{\tau_{\ell}}({\rm Fr}_{\ell}))
have absolute value equal to 1 (cf.
[
Gro98
, §6]
). Hence the eigenvalues of
\ell^{3}{\rm spin}(\phi_{\tau_{\ell}}({\rm Fr}_{\ell}))
, and thus of
{\rm Frob}_{\ell}
, have all absolute value equal to
\ell^{3}
. In particular,
H^{i}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})[\tau_{f}]
is zero unless
i=6
. Finally, notice that
H^{6}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}\neq 0
as the assumption
(DS)
implies
H^{6}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})[\pi_{f}]\neq 0
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

The proof of Proposition
2.6
is similar to the one of
[
KS22
, Proposition 8.2]
, where the proof is carried on with a trace formula argument.

2.8.
Hodge theory

We keep the same notation as §
2.7
. In particular,
\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}
with trivial central character which satisfies
(DS)
and
(St)
, with
\pi_{\infty}\in P(V)
for some irreducible algebraic representation
V
of
\mathbf{G}
as above.

Let
\mathcal{V}
denote the
{\bf Q}
-local system on
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
attached to
V
. We can take the
\pi_{f}
-isotypic component

H^{6}_{B,*}[\pi_{f}]
of
H^{6}_{*}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf C})
, where
*\in\{\emptyset,!\}
and where
\mathcal{V}_{\bf C}
denotes
\mathcal{V}\otimes_{{\bf Q}}{\bf C}
. Propositions
2.5
and
2.6
imply

(5)

\displaystyle H^{\bullet}_{B}[\pi_{f}]=H^{\bullet}_{B,!}[\pi_{f}]=H^{6}_{B,!}[\pi_{f}]\neq 0.

By
[
BR94
, (2.3.1)]
(see also
[
ST14
, Proposition 2.15]
), if
L
is a sufficiently large number field,
H^{6}_{B}[\pi_{f}]
appears as a sub-quotient of
H^{6}_{!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{L})
, where
\mathcal{V}_{L}
denotes
\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\bf Q}L
. In particular, we have a projection

{\rm pr}_{\pi}:H^{6}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{L})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\pi}}(n)\twoheadrightarrow H^{6}_{B}[\pi_{f}](n).

Since
H^{6}_{!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{L})
is a pure
L
-Hodge structure of weight
6
, we have

H^{6}_{B}[\pi_{f}]=\pi_{f}^{U}(L)\otimes M_{B}(\pi_{f}),

with
\pi_{f}^{U}(L)
a realization of
\pi_{f}^{U}
over
L
and

M_{B}(\pi_{f})
a pure
L
-Hodge structure of weight
6
. Thus we have a decomposition

M_{B}(\pi_{f})\otimes{\bf C}=\bigoplus_{p+q=6}H^{p,q}(\pi_{f}).

Lemma 2.8
.

Under the hypothesis
(DS)
and
(St)

{\rm dim}_{\bf C}\,H^{p,q}(\pi_{f})=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }p\neq 3,\\
2&\text{ if }p=3.\end{cases}

In particular, we have
{\rm dim}_{L}M_{B}(\pi_{f})=8
.

Proof.

Thanks to (
5
), we have that

H^{6}_{B}[\pi_{f}]\otimes{\bf C}=H^{6}_{B,!}[\pi_{f}]\otimes{\bf C}=H^{6}_{B,{\rm cusp}}[\pi_{f}]\otimes{\bf C},

hence

H^{6}_{B}[\pi_{f}]\otimes{\bf C}=\pi_{f}^{U}\otimes\bigoplus_{\sigma_{\infty}}H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\sigma_{\infty}\otimes V)^{m(\sigma)},

where
\sigma_{\infty}
runs over the elements of the discrete series
L
-packet
P(V)
of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
and
m(\sigma)
denotes the multiplicity of
\sigma=\sigma_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}
.
Notice that
H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\sigma_{\infty}\otimes V)
equals

(6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{0}/\mathfrak{k},\sigma_{\infty}^{1}\otimes V\right)\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{g}_{0}/\mathfrak{k},\bar{\sigma}_{\infty}^{1}\otimes V\right)

where we have denoted
{\sigma_{\infty}}_{|_{\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})}}=\sigma_{\infty}^{1}\oplus\bar{\sigma}_{\infty}^{1}
. According to
[
BW00
, Theorem II.5.3 b)]
, each space in the decomposition above is 1-dimensional. Moreover there exists a unique pair of integers
(r_{\sigma_{\infty}},s_{\sigma_{\infty}})
satisfying
r_{\sigma_{\infty}}+s_{\sigma_{\infty}}=6
such that (
6
) equals

\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{r_{\sigma_{\infty}}}\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{\bf C}\otimes\bigwedge^{s_{\sigma_{\infty}}}\mathfrak{p}^{-}_{\bf C},\sigma^{1}_{\infty}\otimes V\right)\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{s_{\sigma_{\infty}}}\mathfrak{p}^{+}_{\bf C}\otimes\bigwedge^{r_{\sigma_{\infty}}}\mathfrak{p}^{-}_{\bf C},\bar{\sigma}^{1}_{\infty}\otimes V\right).

As we remarked in §
2.5
, the set
P(V)
has four elements and is in bijection with the set of Hodge types up to conjugation. Since the Hodge structure in
H^{6}_{B,{\rm cusp}}[\pi_{f}]
is induced by this splitting, we deduce that

{\rm dim}_{\bf C}\,H^{r_{\sigma_{\infty}},s_{\sigma_{\infty}}}(\pi_{f})=\begin{cases}m(\sigma)&\text{ if }r_{\sigma_{\infty}}\neq 3,\\
2m(\sigma)&\text{ if }r_{\sigma_{\infty}}=3.\end{cases}

By
[
KS22
, Theorem 12.1]
, the multiplicity of
\sigma
is either
0
or
1
, while thanks to
[
KS22
, Corollary 8.4 & Corollary 12.4]
the dimension of
M_{B}(\pi_{f})
equals 8. Hence
m(\sigma)=1
for all
\sigma_{\infty}\in P(V)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

2.9.
Absolute Hodge cohomology

Let us first recall some definitions from
[
Bei86
]
. A mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structure consists of a finite dimensional
{\bf R}
-vector space
M_{\bf R}
equipped with an increasing finite filtration
W_{*}
called the weight filtration and a decreasing finite filtration
F^{*}
on
M_{\bf C}=M_{\bf R}\otimes_{\bf R}{\bf C}
called the Hodge filtration, such that each pair
(\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{n}M_{\bf R},(\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{n}M_{\bf C},F^{*}))
is a pure
{\bf R}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
(
[
Del71
, Définition 2.1.10]
). The category of mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structures is an abelian category
[
Del71
, Téorème (2.3.5)]
and we denote it by
\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}
.

Definition 2.9
.

A real mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structure is given by a mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structure such that
M_{\bf R}
is equipped with an involution
F_{\infty}^{*}
stabilizing the weight filtration and whose
{\bf C}
-antilinear complexification
\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}=F_{\infty}^{*}\otimes c
, where
c
denotes the complex conjugation, defines an involution on
M_{\bf C}
stabilizing the Hodge filtration.

We will refer to
F_{\infty}^{*}
as the real Frobenius and to
\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}
as the de Rham involution. We denote by
\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}^{+}
the abelian category of real mixed Hodge
{\bf R}
-structures. For any pair of objects
M,N\in D(\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}^{+})
, one has
R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}^{+}}(M,N)=R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}}(M,N)^{\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}}
, since taking invariants by
\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}
is an exact functor.

Definition 2.10
.

If
M=(M_{\bf R},F_{\infty}^{*})\in C(\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}^{+})
is a complex of real mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structure, its absolute Hodge cohomology is defined as

R\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}(M)=R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}}({\bf R}(0),M_{\bf R}).

Its real absolute Hodge cohomology is defined as

R\Gamma_{\mathcal{H/{\bf R}}}(M):=R\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),M))=R\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}}(M_{\bf R})^{\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}}.

The cohomology groups
H^{i}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R})
, where
\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}=\mathcal{V}\otimes_{\bf Q}{\bf R}
, are equipped with a real Frobenius
F_{\infty}^{*}
acting as the complex conjugation on (the complex points)
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
and on
\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}
, define real mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structures. This can be deduced directly from
[
Del71
]
since the cohomology with coefficients is a direct factor of the cohomology of a fiber product of the universal abelian variety of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
, or from the theory of mixed Hodge modules of
[
Sai90
]
. We let
M\in C(\mathrm{MHS}_{\bf R}^{+})
be the complex of real mixed
{\bf R}
-Hodge structures given by
(\bigoplus_{i\in{\bf N}}H^{i}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R})[-i],F_{\infty}^{*})
and we define the absolute real Hodge cohomology
H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)/{\bf R},\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))
of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
and coefficients in
\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4)
to be
H^{1}(R\Gamma_{\mathcal{H}/{\bf R}}(M(4)))
.
Then we have the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4)))\rightarrow

H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)/{\bf R},\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),H^{7}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4)))\rightarrow 0

If
\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}
is as above, we denote by

H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})_{\bf R}(4)):=\left(H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)/{\bf R},\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))\otimes L\right)[\pi_{f}]

the
\pi_{f}
-isotypical component.

Lemma 2.11
.

Under the hypothesis
(DS)
and
(St)
, we have a canonical short exact sequence of finite rank free
{\bf R}\otimes_{{\bf Q}}L
-modules

0\rightarrow F^{4}H^{6}_{\rm dR}[\pi_{f}]\rightarrow H^{6}_{\rm B}[\pi_{f}]^{F_{\infty}^{\star}=-1}(3)\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})_{\bf R}(4))\rightarrow 0.

Moreover, we have

{\rm dim}_{{\bf R}\otimes_{\bf Q}L}\,H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})_{\bf R}(4))={\rm dim}_{\bf C}\,\pi_{f}^{U}.

Proof.

It follows from the existence of the short exact sequence above and from Proposition
2.6
that we have a canonical isomorphism

H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})_{\bf R}(4))\simeq\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))[\pi_{f}]).

Hence, the first statement of the Lemma follows as in
[
Lem17
, Lemma 4.11]
. In particular, the map
F^{4}H^{6}_{\rm dR}[\pi_{f}]\to H^{6}_{\rm B}[\pi_{f}]^{F_{\infty}^{\star}=-1}(3)
is defined by the composition of

F^{4}H^{6}_{\rm dR}[\pi_{f}]\rightarrow H^{6}_{\rm dR}[\pi_{f}]\otimes{\bf C}\simeq H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf C}})[\pi_{f}],

of the projection to

H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf C}})[\pi_{f}]^{\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}=1}
, where
\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}
is the complexification
F_{\infty}^{*}\otimes c
, with
c
denoting the complex conjugation, and of the projection

H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf C}})[\pi_{f}]^{\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}=1}=H^{6}_{\rm B}[\pi_{f}]^{F_{\infty}^{\star}=-1}(3)\oplus H^{6}_{\rm B}[\pi_{f}]^{F_{\infty}^{\star}=1}(4)\rightarrow H^{6}_{\rm B}[\pi_{f}]^{F_{\infty}^{\star}=-1}(3).

Finally, by Lemma
2.8
, we have that

{\rm dim}_{{\bf R}\otimes_{\bf Q}L}\,F^{4}H^{6}_{\rm dR}[\pi_{f}]=3{\rm dim}_{L}\,\pi_{f}^{U}(L)=3{\rm dim}_{\bf C}\,\pi_{f}^{U}.

On the other hand,

{\rm dim}_{{\bf R}\otimes_{\bf Q}L}\,H^{6}_{\rm B}[\pi_{f}]^{F_{\infty}^{\star}=-1}(3)=(3+h^{3,+}){\rm dim}_{\bf C}\,\pi_{f}^{U},

where
h^{3,+}
is the dimension of the
{\bf C}
-vector space
\{x\in H^{3,3}(\pi_{f})\,:\,F_{\infty}^{*}(x)=-x\}
(cf.
[
BGCLRJ23
, §3.4.2]
). By the proof of Lemma
2.8
, we have
h^{3,+}=1
, which implies the result.
∎

3.
Construction of the motivic class

3.1.
Cartan product

Before starting, we briefly recall some properties of the Cartan product of irreducible representations that will be needed (cf.
[
Sun17
, §2.5]
for more details). Let
A
denote either a connected compact Lie group or a semisimple algebraic group. Fix a Cartan subgroup of
A
and an orientation of the roots. Irreducible algebraic representations of
A
are parametrized by dominant weights. If
\lambda
and
\sigma
are two dominant weights with corresponding representations
V^{\lambda}
and
V^{\sigma}
, then the representation
V^{\lambda+\sigma}
appears in
V^{\lambda}\otimes V^{\sigma}
with multiplicity one. We denote it by
V^{\lambda}\cdot V^{\sigma}
and we call it the Cartan component of
V^{\lambda}\otimes V^{\sigma}
. Clearly, the tensor product of two highest weight vectors maps to a corresponding highest weight vector. We denote by
v\otimes w\mapsto v\cdot w
the projection from
V^{\lambda}\otimes V^{\sigma}
to its Cartan component
V^{\lambda}\cdot V^{\sigma}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

[
Sun17
, Lemma 2.12]

Every non-zero pure tensor in
V^{\lambda}\otimes V^{\sigma}
projects non trivially to the Cartan component.

3.2.
Branching laws

In what follows, we fix a totally real field
F
over which
\mathbf{H}
splits. Since
\mathbf{H}
is split over
F
, its finite dimensional irreducible representations are determined by the highest weight theory and we can thus use the branching laws for algebraic representations from
\mathbf{G}
to
\mathbf{H}
established in
[
CRJ20
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The
\mathbf{G}
-representation
V^{\lambda}
over
F
of highest weight
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)
contains the trivial
\mathbf{H}
-representation if and only if
c=0
and
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}
. When this holds the trivial representation of
\mathbf{H}
appears in
(V^{\lambda})_{|_{\mathbf{H}}}
with multiplicity
\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
.

Proof.

From
[
CRJ20
, Lemma 2.10]
, the sum of all irreducible sub-
\mathbf{H}
-representations of
V^{\lambda}
isomorphic (up to a twist) to
\operatorname{Sym}^{(k,0,0)}
for some
k\geq 0
is given by

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}|\\
k\equiv|\lambda|\,(\text{mod }2)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}}r\cdot\operatorname{Sym}^{(k,0,0)}\otimes\mathrm{det}^{\frac{|\lambda|-k}{2}},

for
r=\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
. From this we deduce that
V^{\lambda}

contains the trivial
\mathbf{H}
-representation with multiplicity
r=\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
if and only if
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}=0
.
∎

It will be useful to construct explicitly generators of the trivial
\mathbf{H}
-representations inside
V^{\lambda}
given by the branching laws. We achieve this by constructing some vectors in the representations
V^{(1,1,0,0)}
and
V^{(2,1,1,0)}
and then by taking their Cartan product. From now on, all the representations are defined over
F
. Moreover, since the branching laws are determined by the restriction to the derived subgroups, in the following we work with the groups

\mathbf{H}_{0}:={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{H}_{0}^{\prime}:={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\times{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{0}={\mathrm{Sp}}_{6}.

Recall that we associate to any
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in{\bf Z}^{2}
such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq 0
, the irreducible
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
-representation with highest weight
\lambda
. Applying the branching laws
[
CRJ20
, Proposition 2.8]
, we get the following decompositions of representations of
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle V^{(1,1,0)}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(0,0)})\oplus(\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)})\oplus(\operatorname{Sym}^{1}\boxtimes V^{(1,0)}),

\displaystyle V^{(2,1,1)}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)})\oplus(\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(2,0)})\oplus(\operatorname{Sym}^{1}\boxtimes V^{(1,0)})\oplus(\operatorname{Sym}^{1}\boxtimes V^{(2,1)})

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\oplus(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}).

By Lemma
3.2
,
V^{(1,1,0)}
contains two copies of the trivial
\mathbf{H}_{0}
-representation, each of which lies resp. in
\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(0,0)}
and
\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}
, while
V^{(2,1,1)}
contains a unique trivial
\mathbf{H}_{0}
-representation appearing in
\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}
. Using these facts, we can explicitly define generators of these three trivial representations of
\mathbf{H}_{0}
.

Let
V
be the standard representation of
\mathbf{G}_{0}
with its symplectic basis
\langle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\rangle
given in §
2.6
. According to our choice of embedding
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{0}
,
\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle
, resp.
\langle e_{2},e_{3},f_{2},f_{3}\rangle
, defines a basis of the standard representation of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
, resp.
{\mathrm{Sp}}_{4}
. We first recall how one can realize the representations
V^{(1,1,0)}
and
V^{(1,1,1)}
. As explained in
[
FH91
, §17.1]
,
V^{(1,1,0)}
is realized inside
\bigwedge^{2}V
as the complement of the
\mathbf{G}_{0}
-invariant subspace generated by the vector
e_{1}\wedge f_{1}+e_{2}\wedge f_{2}+e_{3}\wedge f_{3}
corresponding to the symplectic form or, in other words, as the kernel of the map
\bigwedge^{2}V\to V
sending
v_{1}\wedge v_{2}
to
\psi(v_{1},v_{2})
. By
[
FH91
, Theorem 17.5]
, the irreducible representation
V^{(1,1,1)}
is identified with the kernel of the map
\varphi:\bigwedge^{3}V\to V,\;\;v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge v_{3}\mapsto\sum_{i<j,k\neq i,j}\psi(v_{i},v_{j})(-1)^{i-j+1}v_{k}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
F(0)
denote the trivial
\mathbf{H}_{0}
-representation. We have the following

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle:=e_{2}\wedge f_{2}-e_{3}\wedge f_{3}\in F(0)\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}\subseteq V^{(1,1,0)},

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:=e_{2}\wedge f_{2}+e_{3}\wedge f_{3}-2e_{1}\wedge f_{1}\in F(0)\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(0,0)}\subseteq V^{(1,1,0)},

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle:=z_{1}-z_{2}\in F(0)\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}\subseteq V^{(2,1,1)},

where

z_{1}:=e_{1}\cdot(f_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge f_{2}-f_{1}\wedge e_{3}\wedge f_{3}),

z_{2}:=f_{1}\cdot(e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge f_{2}-e_{1}\wedge e_{3}\wedge f_{3}),

and
\cdot
denotes the Cartan product.

Proof.

The vector
v
is obtained from the highest weight vector
e_{1}\wedge e_{2}
in
V^{(1,1,0)}
by applying the composition
X_{(0,1,-1)}\circ X_{(0,-2,0)}\circ X_{(-1,0,1)}
, where
X_{(-1,0,1)},X_{(0,-2,0)},X_{(0,1,-1)}\in\mathfrak{sp}_{6}
denote the weight vectors for
\lambda(-1,0,1)
,
\lambda(0,-2,0)
, and
\lambda(0,1,-1)
respectively (cf.
[
FH91
, §16.1]
for the precise description). Moreover, the vector
X_{(-1,0,1)}(e_{1}\wedge e_{2})=-e_{2}\wedge e_{3}
is of weight
(0,1,1)
, which appears only in the component
\mathrm{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}
, and
X_{(0,1,-1)},X_{(0,-2,0)}\in\mathfrak{sp}_{4}\subseteq\mathfrak{sp}_{6}
so
v
still lies inside
\mathrm{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(1,1)}
. The vector
w
is
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{\prime}
-invariant and therefore it generates the only trivial
\mathbf{H}_{0}^{\prime}
-representation in
V^{(1,1,0)}
. We now explain the definition of
z
.
Note that
e_{1}\in V^{(1,0,0)}
and
f_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge f_{2}-f_{1}\wedge e_{3}\wedge f_{3}\in V^{(1,1,1)}
. Thus, by the properties of the Cartan product

V^{(1,0,0)}\otimes V^{(1,1,1)}=V^{(1,1,0)}\oplus V^{(2,1,1)}\to V^{(2,1,1)},\;\;v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\mapsto v_{1}\cdot v_{2},

z_{1}
is a non-zero vector in
V^{(2,1,1)}
by Lemma
3.1
. The vector
z_{1}
is fixed by
\{I_{2}\}\times{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}^{2}
, but not by
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\times\{I_{2}\}\times\{I_{2}\}
, however, as it is easy to verify, we have that

z=z_{1}+h\cdot z_{1}=z_{1}-z_{2}\in F(0)\subset V^{(2,1,1)},\;\;\text{ with }h=\left({{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{smallmatrix}\big)}},I_{2},I_{2}\right),

generates the unique trivial
\mathbf{H}_{0}
-representation of
V^{(2,1,1)}
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},0)
with
\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}\geq 0
. For each
\lambda_{2}\geq\mu\geq\lambda_{3}
, the vector

\displaystyle v^{[\lambda,\mu]}:=v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\cdot w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\cdot z^{\lambda_{3}}\in F(0)\subseteq(V^{\lambda})_{|_{\mathbf{H}}}

realizes a distinct copy of the trivial representation
F(0)
of
\mathbf{H}
inside
(V^{\lambda})_{|_{\mathbf{H}}}
.

Proof.

For
p,q,r\in{\bf N}
, we have

v^{p}\cdot w^{q}\cdot z^{r}\in F(0)\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(p+r,p+r)}\subseteq V^{(p+q+2r,p+q+r,r)}.

The vectors
v,w,z
are
\mathbf{H}
-highest weight vectors, thus
v^{[\lambda,\mu]}
is such one too. We are left to show that each of the vectors is different. This follows from the fact that each
v^{[\lambda,\mu]}
lies in
\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\boxtimes V^{(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\mu,\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\mu)}\otimes\nu^{\mu-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}
and these representations are all different as
\mu
varies.

∎

3.3.
The motivic class

As in the section above, we fix a totally real field
F
such that
\mathbf{H}
splits over
F
. For a smooth quasi-projective scheme
S
over a field of characteristic zero, let
\operatorname{CHM}_{L}(S)
denote the tensor category of relative Chow motives over
S
with coefficients in a number field
L
and denote by
M:\mathrm{Var}/S\to\operatorname{CHM}_{L}(S)
the contravariant functor from the category of smooth projective schemes over
S
to the category of relative Chow motives over
S
(cf.
[
Anc15
, §2.1]
). By
[
DM91
, Corollary 3.2]
, if
A/S
is an abelian scheme of relative dimension
g
, there is a decomposition
M(A)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2g}h^{i}(A)
in
\operatorname{CHM}_{L}(S)
.
Let
G
temporarily denote one of the groups
\mathbf{H}
or
\mathbf{G}
, and denote by
\operatorname{Rep}_{F}(G)
the category of finite dimensional algebraic representations of
G
defined over
F
. In
[
Anc15
]
, Ancona constructs an additive functor

\mu^{G}_{U}:\operatorname{Rep}_{F}(G)\longrightarrow\operatorname{CHM}_{F}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{G}(U)),

where
U
is a sufficiently small open compact subgroup of
G(\mathbf{A}_{f})
. We recall some of its properties.

Proposition 3.5

(
[
Anc15
, Théorème 8.6]
)
.

The functor
\mu^{G}_{U}
respects duals, tensor products and satisfies the following properties.

(1)

If
V
is the standard representation of
G
, then
\mu^{G}_{U}(V)=h^{1}(\mathscr{A}_{G})
, where
\mathscr{A}_{G}
is the universal abelian scheme over
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{G}(U)
.

(2)

If
\nu:G\to\mathbf{G}_{m}
is the multiplier, then
\mu^{G}_{U}(\nu)
is the Lefschetz motive
F(-1)
.

(3)

For a
G
-representation
V
defined over
F
, the Betti realization of
\mu^{G}_{U}(V)
is the local system
\mathcal{V}_{F}
associated to the vector bundle

G({\bf Q})\backslash(X_{G}\times V\times(G(\mathbf{A}_{f})/U))\to{\operatorname{Sh}}_{G}(U)({\bf C}).

(4)

For any prime
v
of
F
above
\ell
and
G
-representation
V
, the
v
-adic étale realization
\mathcal{V}_{v}
of
\mu^{G}_{U}(V)
is the étale sheaf associated to
V\otimes_{F}F_{v}
, with
U
acting on the left via
U\hookrightarrow G(\mathbf{A}_{f})\to G({\bf Q}_{\ell})
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
V^{\lambda}
be the finite dimensional irreducible algebraic representation over
{\bf Q}
of
\mathbf{G}
of highest weight
\lambda
. We denote by
\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}
the relative Chow motive associated to
V^{\lambda}\otimes F
.

Let
U\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}_{f})
be a sufficiently small compact open subgroup and let
U^{\prime}=U\cap\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A}_{f})
. Recall that we have a closed embedding
