\iota:{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime})\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
which is of codimension
3
. Let
V^{\lambda}
the algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}
(over
F
) of highest weight
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},c)
such that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}
and
c=0
. Using the branching laws of Lemma
3.2
and
[
Tor20
, Theorem 1.2]
, we get the following (cf.
[
CRJ20
, Proposition 2.17]
).

Proposition 3.7
.

For any
\lambda_{2}\geq\mu\geq\lambda_{3}
, we have a Gysin morphism

\iota_{*}^{[\lambda,\mu]}:H^{0}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime}),F(0))\to H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3)),

corresponding to the embedding of
F(0)\subset\iota^{*}V^{\lambda}
given by the
\mathbf{H}
-trivial vector
v^{[\lambda,\mu]}
of Lemma
3.4
.

Definition 3.8
.

We let
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))
be the image by
\iota_{*}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
of

\mathbf{1}_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime})}\in{\rm CH}^{0}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime}))_{F}=H^{0}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime}),F(0)).

3.4.
Realizations

3.4.1.
Étale realization

Let
\mathfrak{l}
be a prime of
F
above
\ell
.
We have an étale cycle class map

\rm{cl}_{\text{\'{e}t}}:H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))\to H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{l}}(3))\to H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{l}}(3))^{G_{\bf Q}},

where the last arrow is the natural map obtained from the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence. We define the following.

Definition 3.9
.

We let
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}:=\rm{cl}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]})\in H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{l}}(3))^{G_{\bf Q}}
.

Remark 3.10
.

—

Notice that
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
equals to the image of
\mathbf{1}\in H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime})_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},F_{\mathfrak{l}}(0))
via the étale Gysin map

\iota_{\text{\'{e}t},*}^{[\lambda,\mu]}:H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime})_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},F_{\mathfrak{l}}(0))\to H^{0}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}(U^{\prime})_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},\iota^{*}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{l}})\to H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\mathfrak{l}}(3)).

—

As the representation
V^{\lambda}
is self dual, we have a Galois equivariant perfect pairing

H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t},c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{{\overline{{\bf Q}}}{}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\lambda}(3))\times H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{{\overline{{\bf Q}}}{}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\lambda}(3))\rightarrow F_{\mathfrak{l}}(0).

Hence, by duality
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
determines a map

H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t},c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{{\overline{{\bf Q}}}{}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\lambda}(3))\to F_{\mathfrak{l}}(0).

3.4.2.
Betti realizations

As in the previous subsection, we define the class

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)({\bf C}),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))

as the image of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
via the Betti cycle class map

\rm{cl}_{B}:H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))\to H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)({\bf C}),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3)).

Note that, as
F
is totally real, the image

{\rm Im}(\rm{cl}_{B})\subset H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)({\bf C}),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf R}}(3))^{{F_{\infty}^{\star}}=1},

where
F_{\infty}^{*}
denotes the composition of the map induced by complex conjugation on the
{\bf C}
-points of
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
with complex conjugation on the coefficients.

3.4.3.
Absolute Hodge realizations

Let
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))^{0}=\ker(\mathrm{cl}_{B})
denote the subgroup of homologically trivial classes and let
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}
denote the quotient
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))/H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))^{0}
. Note that when
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}=0
, i.e. the representation
V^{\lambda}
is the trivial representation, then
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))=H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),F(3))=\mathrm{CH}^{3}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U))_{F}
is the usual Chow group of
3
-codimensional cycles modulo rational equivalence and the space
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}=\mathrm{N}^{3}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U))_{F}
is the space of
3
-codimensional cycles modulo homological equivalence, with coefficients in
F
. In this section, we define a natural injective map

(7)

H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}\rightarrow H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf R}}(4)).

The definition is similar to the one for smooth projective varieties (see
[
Sch88
, §5]
) and we recall it for the convenience of the reader. The cycle class map is an injection

\mathrm{cl}_{B}:H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}\rightarrow H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{F}(3))^{F_{\infty}^{*}=1}\cap H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf C}}(3))^{0,0}

where
H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf C}}(3))^{0,0}
denotes the subspace of

W_{0}H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf C}}(3))=\mathrm{Gr}_{0}^{W}H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf C}}(3))

of vectors which have Hodge type
(0,0)
. The composite of the inclusions

\displaystyle H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{F}(3))^{F_{\infty}^{*}=1}\cap H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\bf C}(3))^{0,0}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle W_{0}H^{6}_{dR}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(3))

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow

\displaystyle W_{2}H^{6}_{dR}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(3))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W_{0}H^{6}_{dR}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))

and of the projection

W_{0}H^{6}_{dR}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))\rightarrow

W_{0}H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))^{+}\backslash W_{0}H^{6}_{dR}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))/F^{0}W_{0}H^{6}_{dR}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))

is injective. As the last space above is canonically isomorphic to

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))),

we obtain a natural injective map

H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4))).

Composing this map with the canonical injection

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}^{+}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{{\bf R}}(4)))\rightarrow H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf R}}(4))

we obtain the map (
7
). We denote by
\overline{\rm cl}_{\mathcal{H}}:H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))\rightarrow H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf R}}(4))
the composition of the map (
7
) with the projection
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))\rightarrow H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}
.

Definition 3.11
.

We define

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}:=\overline{\rm cl}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]})\in H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{{\bf R}}(4)).

Remark 3.12
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
which satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
2.11
and let
S
be a finite set of places containing the ramified places of
\pi_{f}
and
\infty
. By the conjectures of Beilinson and Tate and the local calculations of Gross and Savin in
[
GS98
]
, there should exist a cubic étale algebra
E/{\bf Q}
such that the cycle
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
, with
\mathbf{H}
defined by
E/{\bf Q}
, and their Hecke translates are expected to generate
H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f})_{\bf R}(4))
when
{\rm ord}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})=-1
. Assuming the non-vanishing of the archimedean integral, Corollary
5.15
confirms this expectation.

4.
Construction of the differential form and pairing with the motivic class

The purpose of this chapter is to study the Betti and Hodge realizations of the cycle constructed in §
3.3
by relating their pairing with a suitable cuspidal harmonic differential form to an automorphic period.

4.1.
Test vectors

Recall that the discrete series
L
-packets for
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
have four elements, each indexed by a Hodge type (and its conjugate).
Let
\pi
denote a cuspidal automorphic representation of

{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
for which
\pi_{\infty}
is the discrete series of Hodge type (3,3) in the
L
-packet of
V^{\lambda}
where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},0)
and
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}
. This translates into saying that
\pi
is a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
with trivial central character for which

H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{\lambda})\neq 0,

and such that
\pi_{\infty}|_{\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})}=\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}\oplus\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
is the direct sum of the discrete series representations of respective Harish Chandra parameters
(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+1,-\lambda_{1}-3)
and
(\lambda_{1}+3,-\lambda_{3}-1,-\lambda_{2}-2)
. Recall that these discrete series contain with multiplicity one their minimal
K_{\infty}
-types
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+2,-\lambda_{1}-4)}
and
\tau_{(\lambda_{1}+4,-\lambda_{3}-2,-\lambda_{2}-2)}
respectively. On the other hand, as
K_{\infty}
-representations we have

\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\supseteq\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{p}_{\bf C}^{+}\otimes\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{-}=\bigoplus_{i}\tau_{i}\supseteq\tau_{(2,2,-4)}\oplus\tau_{(4,-2,-2)},

where the equality expresses the decomposition of the tensor product into irreducible
K_{\infty}
-representations.
This fact will be useful to construct an element in

H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{\lambda})=\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}},\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{\lambda}\right)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\pi_{\infty}\right),

where the last equality follows from the fact that
V^{\lambda}
is self-dual. Before stating the next result, let us fix the following data.

—

A highest weight vector
\Psi_{\infty}
, resp.
\overline{\Psi}_{\infty}
, of the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+2,-\lambda_{1}-4)}
, resp.
\tau_{(\lambda_{1}+4,-\lambda_{3}-2,-\lambda_{2}-2)}
of
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
, resp.
\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3}
.

—

A highest weight vector
X_{(2,2,-4)}
, resp.
X_{(4,-2,-2)}
, of
\tau_{(2,2,-4)}
, resp.
\tau_{(4,-2,-2)}
.

—

A highest weight vector
v^{\lambda^{\prime}}
, resp.
v^{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
, of
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}\subseteq V^{\lambda}
, resp.
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}\subseteq V^{\lambda}
, where
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
denote the irreducible algebraic
K_{\infty}
-representations of highest weight
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1})
and
\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}=(\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2})
respectively.

Lemma 4.1
.

The spaces
\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3})

are of dimension one and the elements

\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\wedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}),\omega_{\overline{\Psi}_{\infty}}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}(\wedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\overline{\pi}_{\infty,1}^{3,3})

defined by

\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}(X_{(2,2,-4)}\otimes v^{\lambda^{\prime}})=\Psi_{\infty}

\omega_{\overline{\Psi}_{\infty}}(X_{(4,-2,-2)}\otimes v^{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}})=\overline{\Psi}_{\infty}

are generators of these spaces.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
[
BW00
, Theorem II.5.3 b)]
and its proof.
∎

4.2.
Restriction to
\mathbf{H}

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},0)
, with
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}
and let
V^{\lambda}
be as above. Let
\mathfrak{h}
, resp.
\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{H}}
denote the Lie algebra of
\mathbf{H}({\bf R})
, resp. the maximal compact modulo the center
K_{\mathbf{H}}
. Observe that via the embedding
\iota:\mathbf{H}({\bf R})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}({\bf R})
, the group
K_{\mathbf{H}}
is isomorphic to
T_{\infty}
. One has a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{h}_{\bf C}=\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{H},{\bf C}}\oplus\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{H},{\bf C}}
, where
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{H},{\bf C}}
is 6-dimensional and is spanned by the non-compact root spaces. We fix once and for all a generator
X_{0}
of the
1
-dimensional
{\bf C}
-vector space
\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{H},{\bf C}}\subseteq\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}
as in
[
BGCLRJ23
, §5.2]
. The main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}
and
\omega_{\overline{\Psi}_{\infty}}
be the elements of
\mathrm{Hom}_{K_{\infty}}\left(\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\otimes V^{\lambda},\pi_{\infty}\right)
defined in Lemma
4.1
. Let
X_{0}
be as above and let
v
be any
\mathbf{H}
-invariant vector in
V^{\lambda}
. Then

\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}\left(X_{0}\otimes v\right)\neq 0,

\omega_{\overline{\Psi}_{\infty}}\left(X_{0}\otimes v\right)\neq 0.

The proof of Theorem
4.2
will be constructive and occupies the rest of this section. We start by recalling the following result.

Lemma 4.3

(
[
BGCLRJ23
, Lemma 5.4]
)
.

Let
X_{0}
be as above. Then the image of
X_{0}
by

\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{H},{\bf C}}\rightarrow\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}\rightarrow\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{p}_{\bf C}^{+}\otimes\bigwedge^{3}\mathfrak{p}_{{\bf C}}^{-}\rightarrow\tau_{(2,2,-4)},

where the first map is induced by the embedding
\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
and the second and the third maps are the natural projections, is non zero.

We next study the interaction between the branching laws of
V^{\lambda}
to the subgroup
\mathbf{H}
of
\mathbf{G}
and to its maximal compact subgroup. More precisely, we show that the
\mathbf{H}
-invariant vectors constructed in Lemma
3.4
project non-trivially to
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
and moreover that their projections form a basis of the corresponding
(0,0,0)
-weight spaces for the action of
T_{\infty}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
be the irreducible algebraic sub-
K_{\infty}
-representations of
V^{\lambda}
of highest weight
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1})
and
\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}=(\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2})
. Then the weight
(0,0,0)
appears in both
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
with multiplicity
\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
.

Proof.

Let
n_{0}(\lambda^{\prime})
denote the multiplicity of the weight
(0,0,0)
in
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. Kostant multiplicity formula reads as

n_{0}(\lambda^{\prime})=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{W}_{K_{\infty}}}(-1)^{\ell(w)}P(w(\lambda^{\prime}+\rho_{K_{\infty}})-\rho_{K_{\infty}}),

where
\rho_{K_{\infty}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}_{c}}\alpha=(1,0,-1)
and the function
\mu\mapsto P(\mu)
calculates the number of ways for which the weight
\mu
can be expressed as a linear combination

\alpha(e_{1}-e_{2})+\beta(e_{1}-e_{3})+\gamma(e_{2}-e_{3}),

with
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in{\bf Z}_{\geq 0}
(cf.
[
FH91
]
). Using this formula, it is a tedious but straightforward calculation to verify that
n_{0}(\lambda^{\prime})=\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
and the same for
\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}=w_{8}\lambda^{\prime}
.

∎

According to Lemma
4.4
, there are
\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
linearly independent vectors of weight
(0,0,0)
in
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. We now show that these weight vectors correspond one to one to the
\mathbf{H}
-invariant vectors of Lemma
3.2
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
v,w
be the vectors of
V^{(1,1,0)}
and let
z
be the vector of
V^{(2,1,1)}
defined in Lemma
3.3
. The irreducible algebraic representation
\tau_{(1,0,-1)}
, resp.
\tau_{(1,1,-2)}
and
\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}
, appear in the restriction of
V^{(1,1,0)}
, resp. of
V^{(1,1,1)}
, to
K_{\infty}
with multiplicity
1
. Moreover, we have
v,w\in\tau_{(1,0,-1)}\subseteq V^{(1,1,0)}
, and
z\in\tau_{(1,1,-2)}\oplus\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}\subseteq V^{(1,1,1)}
, with
z
projecting non-trivially to each factor of this decomposition.

Proof.

First observe that
v,w\in V^{(1,1,0)}
and
z\in V^{(2,1,1)}
are vectors of weight
(0,0,0)
both for the split and the compact tori of
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
. Indeed these vectors are fixed (up to a constant) by the matrix
J
sending the non-compact torus
\mathbf{T}_{0}
to the compact torus
T_{\infty}
defined in §
2.2
. Using branching laws from
\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})
to
K_{\infty}
, we have a decomposition of
K_{\infty}
-representations

V^{(1,1,0)}=\tau_{(1,1,0)}\oplus\tau_{(1,0,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(0,-1,-1)}.

The weight
(0,0,0)
appears only in
\tau_{(1,0,-1)}

and with multiplicity 2. Since it has also multiplicity
2
in
V^{(1,1,0)}
, we deduce that
\{v,w\}
forms a basis for the
(0,0,0)
-eigenspace of
\tau_{(1,0,-1)}
. On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle V^{(2,1,1)}

\displaystyle=\tau_{(-1,-1,-2)}\oplus\tau_{(1,-1,-2)}\oplus\tau_{(1,1,0)}\oplus\tau_{(1,1,-2)}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\oplus\tau_{(1,0,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(2,1,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(2,1,1)}\oplus\tau_{(0,-1,-1)}.

The weight
(0,0,0)
only appears in
\tau_{(1,1,-2)}\oplus\tau_{(1,0,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}
, which implies that

z\in\tau_{(1,1,-2)}\oplus\tau_{(1,0,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}.

Notice that the decomposition of the standard representation of
\mathbf{G}_{0}

V=\tau_{(1,0,0)}\oplus\tau_{(0,0,-1)}

of
K_{\infty}
-representations can be realized by picking the basis
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\}
, where
v_{r}:=e_{r}+if_{r}
and
w_{r}:=ie_{r}+f_{r}
. The set
\{v_{r}\}_{1\leq r\leq 3}
, resp.
\{w_{r}\}_{1\leq r\leq 3}
, defines a basis for
\tau_{(1,0,0)}
, resp.
\tau_{(0,0,-1)}
. We now write
z
in terms of this basis. By using the relations

\displaystyle e_{r}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}v_{r}-\tfrac{i}{2}w_{r},

\displaystyle f_{r}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}w_{r}-\tfrac{i}{2}v_{r},

we have that

e_{1}\otimes f_{1}\wedge(e_{2}\wedge f_{2}-e_{3}\wedge f_{3})-f_{1}\otimes e_{1}\wedge(e_{2}\wedge f_{2}-e_{3}\wedge f_{3})

equals to

\frac{1}{4}\left(v_{1}\otimes w_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})-w_{1}\otimes v_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})\right).

Thus,

z=z_{1}-z_{2}=\frac{1}{4}\left(v_{1}\cdot w_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})-w_{1}\cdot v_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})\right).

Notice that the vector
w_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})\in V^{(1,1,1)}
is of weight
(-1,0,0)
for
T_{\infty}
, while
v_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})\in V^{(1,1,1)}
is of weight
(1,0,0)
for
T_{\infty}
. As

V^{(1,1,1)}=\tau_{(1,1,1)}\oplus\tau_{(1,-1,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(1,1,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(-1,-1,-1)},

and the fact that the weight
(-1,0,0)
appears only in
\tau_{(1,-1,-1)}
and
(1,0,0)
only in
\tau_{(1,1,-1)}
, we have that

w_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})\in\tau_{(1,-1,-1)}

v_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})\in\tau_{(1,1,-1)}

By the properties of the Cartan product, the vector
s_{1}:=v_{1}\cdot w_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})
is non-zero in
\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}
, while
s_{2}:=w_{1}\cdot v_{1}\wedge(v_{2}\wedge w_{2}-v_{3}\wedge w_{3})
is non-zero in
\tau_{(1,1,-2)}
. This shows that the vector
z\in V^{(2,1,1)}
lives in
\tau_{(2,-1,-1)}\oplus\tau_{(1,1,-2)}
, thus finishing the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

The set
\{{\rm pr}_{\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})\}_{\mu}
, resp.
\{{\rm pr}_{\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})\}_{\mu}
, forms a basis of the weight
(0,0,0)
-eigenspace of
\tau_{{\lambda}^{\prime}}\subset V^{\lambda}
, resp.
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}\subset V^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Recall that we have defined

v^{[\lambda,\mu]}:=v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\cdot w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\cdot z^{\lambda_{3}}\in F(0)\subseteq(V^{\lambda})_{|_{\mathbf{H}}}.

By Lemma
4.5
, we have that
v,w\in\tau_{(1,0,-1)}\subseteq V^{(1,1,0)}
so that, for any
\lambda_{3}\leq\mu\leq\lambda_{2}
, we have
v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\otimes w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\in\tau_{(1,0,-1)}^{\otimes\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}}
and we deduce that the projection of
v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\cdot w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\in V^{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3},0)}
to
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3},0,\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}
coincides with their Cartan product with respect to
K_{\infty}
. Moreover, each of these projections is non-zero because of Lemma
3.1
. Since the vectors

v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\cdot w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\in\tau_{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3},0,\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}

are all different as they live in different
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}_{0}
sub-representations (cf. the proof of Lemma
3.4
), we conclude that they span the
\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+1
-dimensional weight
(0,0,0)
-eigenspace of
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3},0,\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2})}
. We now show that
z^{\lambda_{3}}
projects non-trivially to both
\tau_{(2\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{3})}
and
\tau_{(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3},-2\lambda_{3})}
.
Notice that, as the weights
(2\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{3})
and
(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3},-2\lambda_{3})
are extremal in
V^{(2\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3})}
and appear uniquely, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(V^{(2,1,1)})^{\otimes\lambda_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot}
\scriptstyle{({\rm pr}_{1},{\rm pr}_{1}^{\prime})}
\textstyle{V^{(2\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm pr}_{2}}
\textstyle{(\tau_{(2,-1,-1)})^{\otimes\lambda_{3}}\oplus(\tau_{(1,1,-2)})^{\otimes\lambda_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\tau_{(2\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{3})}\oplus\tau_{(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3},-2\lambda_{3})},}

where the horizontal arrows are the Cartan projections and the vertical arrows are the natural projections given by the decomposition of
V^{(2r,r,r)}
as
K_{\infty}
-representations. Thanks to the commutativity of the diagram, we know that the vector
z^{\otimes\lambda_{3}}\in(V^{(2,1,1)})^{\otimes\lambda_{3}}
maps to

{{\rm pr}_{2}}(z^{\lambda_{3}})={{\rm pr}_{1}}(z)^{\lambda_{3}}+{{\rm pr}_{1}^{\prime}}(z)^{\lambda_{3}}=s_{1}^{\lambda_{3}}+s_{2}^{\lambda_{3}},

where
s_{1},s_{2}
are as in Lemma
4.5
. This shows, again by Lemma
3.1
, that each
v^{[\lambda,\mu]}
projects non-trivially to both
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
and that each of these projections are different by Lemma
3.4
. Indeed,

{\rm pr}_{\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})=v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\cdot w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\cdot s_{1}^{\lambda_{3}},

{\rm pr}_{\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})=v^{\lambda_{2}-\mu}\cdot w^{\mu-\lambda_{3}}\cdot s_{2}^{\lambda_{3}}.

By Lemma
4.4
, this means that
\{{\rm pr}_{\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})\}_{\mu}
(resp.
\{{\rm pr}_{\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})\}_{\mu}
) defines a basis of the weight
(0,0,0)
-eigenspace of
\tau_{{\lambda}^{\prime}}
, resp.
\tau_{\overline{\lambda}^{\prime}}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We are now in condition of finishing the proof of Theorem
4.2

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

By construction, the map
\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}
factors through
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}+(2,2,-4)}\subseteq\tau_{(2,2,-4)}\otimes\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
. Lemma
4.3
shows that the projection of
X_{0}
to
\tau_{(2,2,-4)}
is non-zero, while Proposition
4.6
shows that
\mathrm{pr}_{\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})
is non-zero. Since
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}+(2,2,-4)}
is the Cartan product of
\tau_{(2,2,-4)}
and
\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, we deduce from Lemma
3.1
that the image of the pure tensor
\mathrm{pr}_{\tau_{(2,2,-4)}}(X_{0})\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{\tau_{\lambda^{\prime}}}(v^{[\lambda,\mu]})
is non-zero.

∎

4.3.
The pairing

Let
\pi
denote a cuspidal automorphic representation of

{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
for which
\pi_{\infty}
is the discrete series of Hodge type (3,3) in the
L
-packet of
V^{\lambda}
with
\lambda=(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},0)
.
Let
\Psi=\Psi_{\infty}\otimes\Psi_{f}
denote a cusp form in
\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}
. We assume that
\Psi_{\infty}
is a highest weight vector of the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+2,-\lambda_{1}-4)}
of
\pi_{\infty}|_{\mathbf{G}_{0}({\bf R})}
. We let
[\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}]\in H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{\lambda})
be the cohomology class of the harmonic differential form
\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}
defined in Lemma
4.1
. We also assume that
\Psi_{f}\in V_{\pi_{f}}
is
U
-invariant. Then we have

[\omega_{\Psi}]:=[\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}\otimes\Psi_{f}]\in H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi^{U}\otimes V^{\lambda}).

Lemma 4.7
.

There is a Hecke-equivariant inclusion

H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi^{U}\otimes V^{\lambda})\subset H^{6}_{dR,c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\bf C}).

Moreover, if
\pi_{w}
is the Steinberg representation for some finite palce
w
, such inclusion is unique.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{rd}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})/U,V^{\lambda})
denote the space of
V^{\lambda}
-valued
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
-functions on the double quotient
\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})/U
which, together with all their right
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{{\bf C}})
-derivatives, are rapidly decreasing in the sense of
[
Har90
]
. As
\pi
is cuspidal and cusp forms are rapidly decreasing, we have

H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\pi_{\infty}\otimes V^{\lambda}_{\bf C})^{m(\pi)}\otimes\pi_{f}^{U}\subset H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{rd}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})/U,V^{\lambda})).

Thus the result follows from the fact that, according to
[
Bor80b
, Theorem 5.2]
(see also
[
Har90
, Theorem 1.4.1]
), there exists a canonical Hecke equivariant isomorphism

H^{6}_{dR,c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}^{\lambda})\simeq H^{6}(\mathfrak{g},K_{\mathbf{G}};\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{rd}(\mathbf{G}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})/U,V^{\lambda})).
Finally, if
\pi_{w}
is Steinberg at a finite place
w
, we have, as in Lemma
2.8
, that
m(\pi)=1
.
∎

4.3.1.
The pairing in Betti cohomology

Poincaré duality is a perfect pairing

\langle\,,\,\rangle:H^{6}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{F}^{\lambda}(3))\times H^{6}_{B,c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{F}^{\lambda})\to F(-3),

which is a morphism of mixed
F
-Hodge structures.
Fix the choice of a measure
dh
on
\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})
as follows. For each finite place
p
, we take the Haar measure
dh_{p}
on
\mathbf{H}({{\bf Q}_{p}})
that assigns volume
1
to
\mathbf{H}({{\bf Z}_{p}})
. For the archimedean place, we let
X_{0}\in\bigwedge^{6}\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbf{H},{\bf C}}
be the generator fixed at the beginning of section
4.2
. The choice of
X_{0}
induces an equivalence between top differential forms on
X_{\mathbf{H}}=\mathbf{H}({\bf R})/K_{\mathbf{H},\infty}
and invariant measures
dh_{\infty}
on
\mathbf{H}({\bf R})
assigning measure one to
K_{\mathbf{H},\infty}
(cf.
[
Har97
, p. 83]
for details). We let
dh_{\infty}
denote the measure associated in this way to the pullback of
\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]*}\omega_{\Psi}
to
X_{\mathbf{H}}
and we then define
dh=dh_{\infty}\prod_{p}dh_{p}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

We have

\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle=\frac{h_{{U^{\prime}}}}{(2\pi i)^{3}\cdot\mathrm{vol}({U^{\prime}})}\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}){\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi(h)dh,

where
h_{U^{\prime}}=4^{-1}|{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf Q})\backslash{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A}_{f})/({\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A}_{f})\cap{U^{\prime}})|
and
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bf C})
is an element for which
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi_{\infty}=\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}(X_{0}\otimes v^{[\lambda,\mu]})
.

Proof.

By
[
Bor80b
, Corollary 5.5]
, there exists a
\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}
-valued rapidly decreasing differential form
\eta
of degree five on
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)
such that
\omega_{c}:=\omega_{\Psi}+d\eta
is compactly supported. We have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\rm cl}_{B}(\iota_{*}^{[\lambda,\mu]}\mathbf{1}_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}),[\omega_{c}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\iota_{*}^{[\lambda,\mu]}{\rm cl}_{B}(\mathbf{1}_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}),[\omega_{c}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\rm cl}_{B}(\mathbf{1}_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}),\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]*}[\omega_{c}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{3}}\int_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]*}\omega_{c},

where
\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]*}:\iota^{*}V^{\lambda}\rightarrow F(0)
is the
\mathbf{H}
-equivariant projection dual to the inclusion
\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]}:F(0)\rightarrow\iota^{*}V^{\lambda}
defined by
1\mapsto v^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in V^{\lambda}
, where
v^{[\lambda,\mu]}
is the vector defined in Lemma
3.4
. According to
[
Bor80b
, §5.6]
, we have

\int_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]*}d\eta=0.

Hence, using Theorem
4.2
we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{3}}\int_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}\iota^{[\lambda,\mu]*}\omega_{\Psi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{3}}\int_{{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{H}}({U^{\prime}})}\omega_{\Psi}\left(X_{0}\otimes v^{[\lambda,\mu]}\right)(h)dh

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{3}}\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})/{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf R})K_{\mathbf{H},\infty}{U^{\prime}}}A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi(h)dh

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{{U^{\prime}}}}{(2\pi i)^{3}}\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}){\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})/{U^{\prime}}}A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi(h)dh

\displaystyle=\frac{h_{{U^{\prime}}}}{(2\pi i)^{3}\cdot\mathrm{vol}({U^{\prime}})}\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}){\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi(h)dh,

where the third equality follows from Theorem
4.2
as
\omega_{\Psi_{\infty}}(X_{0}\otimes v^{[\lambda,\mu]})
is non-zero and thus it is of the form
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi_{\infty}
, for some
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bf C})
, because
\Psi_{\infty}
is the highest weight vector of the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type
\tau_{(\lambda_{2}+2,\lambda_{3}+2,-\lambda_{1}-4)}
. Moreover, the fourth equality follows from the fact that
\Psi
is fixed by the center of
\mathbf{G}
, whence, using that
|{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf R})/({\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}\cap\mathbf{H})({\bf R})|=4
, the constant
h_{U^{\prime}}
is equal to
4^{-1}|{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf Q})\backslash{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A}_{f})/({\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A}_{f})\cap{U^{\prime}})|
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

In view of Proposition
4.8
, we immediately notice that if
\pi
is not
\mathbf{H}
-distinguished, namely

\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}){\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\varphi_{\pi}(h)dh=0,

for any cusp form
\varphi_{\pi}
in the space of
\pi
, we have that
{\rm pr}_{\pi^{\vee}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]}=0
.
As we discuss later in §
8
, the
\mathbf{H}
-distinguishability is related to the property of
\pi
being a (functorial) lift from
G_{2}
, which is (conjecturally) equivalent to the fact that the Spin
L
-function of
\pi
has a pole at
s=1
.

4.3.2.
The pairing in absolute Hodge cohomology

Let

\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)/{\bf R},\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))\times H^{6}_{\mathcal{H},c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)/{\bf R},\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(3))\to{\bf R}

be the natural pairing between absolute Hodge cohomology and compactly supported cohomology as constructed in
[
Bei86
, §4.2]
. In order to ease notations, we will denote by
H^{*}_{B,c}(i)
and
H^{*}_{B}(i)
the cohomology groups
H^{*}_{B,c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{F}(i))
and
H^{*}_{B}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{F}(i))
, respectively. Recall from §
2.9
that absolute Hodge cohomology and compactly supported cohomology live in exact sequences

(8)

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}(4))\to H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{7}_{B}(4))\rightarrow 0,

(9)

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{5}_{B,c}(3))\to H^{6}_{\mathcal{H},c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(3))\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B,c}(3))\rightarrow 0,

which are deduced from the description of absolute Hodge cohomology as a cone of a diagram of complexes of Hodge structures. Let
[\omega_{\Psi}]\in H^{6}_{B,c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}^{\lambda}(3))
be the compactly supported cohomology class of the harmonic differential form
\omega_{\Psi}
. This class is of Hodge type
(3,3)
and hence, since
W_{0}H^{6}_{B,c}(3)=H^{6}_{B,c}(3)
, it naturally lives in the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B,c}(3))=W_{0}H_{B,c}(3)\cap F^{0}H_{B,c}(3)_{\bf C}
. Denote by
\widetilde{[\omega_{\Psi}]}
any lift of
[\omega_{\Psi}]
in
H^{6}_{\mathcal{H},c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(3))
via the surjection of the exact sequence (
9
).

Proposition 4.10
.

The pairing
\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},\widetilde{[\omega_{\Psi}]}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
depends only on
[\omega_{\Psi}]
and not on the choice of lift. We denote this value by
\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Moreover, the pairing is given by the natural Poincaré duality pairing. In particular, we have

\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\frac{h_{U^{\prime}}}{(2\pi i)^{3}\cdot\mathrm{vol}(U^{\prime})}\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}){\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi(h)dh.

Proof.

We give a sketch of the proof and we refer to
[
Bei86
]
or to
[
BGKK07
, §5.1]
for the facts used here.
It follows from the description of the pairing between absolute Hodge cohomology and compactly supported cohomology given in
[
Bei86
, §4.2]
that, since our cycle class
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
lives in the subspace
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}(4))
of

H^{7}_{\mathcal{H}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(4))
, the map

\langle[\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}],-\rangle:H^{6}_{\mathcal{H},c}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathcal{V}_{\bf R}(3))\to{\bf R}

factors through
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B,c}(3))
and coincides with the natural Poincaré duality pairing

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}(4))\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B,c}(3))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{6}_{B}(4)\otimes H^{6}_{B,c}(3))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cup}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),H^{12}_{B,c}(7))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{{\bf R}}}({\bf R}(0),{\bf R}(1))={\bf R},

This shows the first two assertions. The last formula follows from Proposition
4.8
.
∎

5.
Integral representation and residue of the Spin
L
-function

In this section, using the the result of
[
PS18
]
, we explain the precise connection between the period integral appearing in the statement of Proposition
4.8
and the residue of the spin
L
-function of
\pi
in the case where the cubic totally real étale algebra
E
over
{\bf Q}
defining
\mathbf{H}
is of the form
{\bf Q}\times F
, with
F
a quadratic real étale algebra over
{\bf Q}
. We start by recalling well known analytic properties of some Eisenstein series for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
.

5.1.
Eisenstein series for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}

Let
\mathbf{T}_{2}
denote the maximal diagonal torus of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and let
\mathbf{B}_{2}=\mathbf{T}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}
denote the standard Borel. We denote by
\delta
the character of
\mathbf{T}_{2}
defined by
\mathrm{diag}(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto{t_{1}}/{t_{2}}
and we regard
\delta
as a character of
\mathbf{B}_{2}
by extending it trivially to the unipotent radical. Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A}^{2})
be a Schwartz-Bruhat function. Following Jacquet, for any
s\in{\bf C}
, we attach to
\Phi
the function
f_{\Phi}\in\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathbf{B}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}^{\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\delta^{s}
defined by

f_{\Phi}(h,s)=|\mathrm{det}(h)|^{s}\int_{\mathbf{A}^{\times}}\Phi((0,t)h)|t|^{2s}d^{\times}t

and the Eisenstein series

E_{\Phi}(h,s)=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{B}_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\bf Q})}f_{\Phi}(\gamma h,s).

In the statement of the following Lemma, we denote by
\widehat{\Phi}(0)=\int_{\mathbf{A}^{2}}\Phi(x,y)dxdy

the value at
0
of the Fourier transform of
\Phi
.

Lemma 5.1
.

(1)

The Eisenstein series
E_{\Phi}(h,s)
is absolutely convergent for
Re(s)
big enough and has a meromorphic continuation to
{\bf C}
.

(2)

We have

E_{\Phi}(h,s)=\frac{|\mathrm{det}(h)|^{s-1}\widehat{\Phi}(0)}{2(s-1)}+R(h,s)

where
R(h,s)
is an entire function in
s
for any
h\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Statement (1) is
[
Jac72
, Proposition 19.2]
. According to
[
JS81
, Lemma (4.2)]
and its proof, we have

E_{\Phi}(h,s)=\frac{c|\mathrm{det}(h)|^{s-1}\widehat{\Phi}(0)}{s-1}+R(h,s)

where
R(h,s)
is holomorphic for
Re(s)>0
and
c=(s-1)\int_{|t|\leq 1}|t|^{2(s-1)}d^{\times}t
, the integral being over the set
\{t\in\mathbf{A}^{\times}/{\bf Q}^{\times}\,:\,|t|\leq 1\}
. By Iwasawa–Tate we have
c=\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

5.2.
Fourier coefficients

Here we discuss the definition and basic properties of some Fourier coefficients for cusp forms for
\mathbf{G}
, which appear in the integral representation of the Spin
L
-function of
[
PS18
]
.

5.2.1.
The Siegel parabolic

We let
Q=L_{3}U_{3}
denote the standard Siegel parabolic subgroup of
\mathbf{G}
, with Levi
L_{3}\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{3}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1}
. Explicitly,

L_{3}=\left\{m(g,\mu)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}g&\\
&\mu{{}^{t}g^{-1}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\;|\;g\in\mathrm{GL}_{3},\mu\in\mathrm{GL}_{1}\right\},

U_{3}=\left\{n(u)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I_{3}&u\\
&I_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\;\;u\in M_{3}\;|\;u^{t}=u\right\}.

Denote
{\rm Sym}(3)=\{\alpha\in M_{3}\;|\;\alpha^{t}=\alpha\}
. To each
\alpha\in{\rm Sym}(3)({\bf Q})
, we associate the unitary character

\psi_{\alpha}:U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
by
n(u)\in U_{3}(\mathbf{A})\mapsto e({\rm Tr}(\alpha u))

where
e:{\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A}\to{\bf C}^{\times}
is the additive character with
e_{\infty}(x):=e^{2\pi ix}
for
x\in{\bf R}
, and conductor 1 at the finite places.
For each
\alpha\in{\rm Sym}(3)({\bf Q})
, we define a Fourier coefficient along
U_{3}
for a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
as follows.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\Psi
be a cusp form in the space of
\pi
. Define

\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{\alpha}}(g):=\int_{U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}\psi_{\alpha}^{-1}(u)\Psi(ug)du.

We let
L_{3}({\bf Q})
acts on
{\rm Sym}(3)({\bf Q})
via the right action
\alpha\cdot m(g,\mu)=\mu^{-1}g^{t}\alpha g
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in{\rm Sym}(3)({\bf Q})
. If there exists
m\in L_{3}({\bf Q})
such that
\beta=\alpha\cdot m,
then

\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{\beta}}(g)=\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{\alpha}}(mg)

Proof.

Suppose that
\beta=\alpha\cdot m
with
m=m(g,\mu)
. The result follows from the equality

\psi_{\beta}(n(u))=e({\rm Tr}(\mu^{-1}g^{t}\alpha gu))=e({\rm Tr}(\alpha gu\mu^{-1}g^{t}))=\psi_{\alpha}(mn(u)m^{-1}).

∎

In this manuscript, we are interested in Fourier coefficients associated to the set of rank two elements of
{\rm Sym}(3)({\bf Q})
, which we denote by
{\rm Sym}^{{\rm rk}2}(3)({\bf Q})
. Let
D\in{\bf Q}^{\times}
and let
F
denote the étale quadratic extension
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
of
{\bf Q}
. If
D
is not a square then
F
is a field, else
F={\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
.

Definition 5.4
.

We let
\psi_{D}:U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
be the unitary character

\psi_{D}:n(u)\mapsto e({\rm Tr}(\alpha_{D}u))=e(u_{33}-Du_{22})

associated to
\alpha_{D}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&&\\
&-D&\\
&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in{\rm Sym}^{{\rm rk}2}(3)({\bf Q})
.

We have the following:

Lemma 5.5
.

A set of representatives of
{\rm Sym}^{{\rm rk}2}(3)({\bf Q})/_{\sim M_{3}({\bf Q})}
is given by

\{\alpha_{D}\;:\;D\in{\bf Q}^{\times}/({\bf Q}^{\times})^{2}\}.

In view of these two lemmas, the set of Fourier coefficients associated to the Siegel parabolic and a rank 2 symmetric matrix is parametrized by the set of étale quadratic algebras of
{\bf Q}
.

5.2.2.
Fourier coefficients of type
(4\,2)

We now turn our attention to Fourier coefficients associated to the unipotent orbit of
\mathbf{G}
associated to the partition
(4\,2)
. The corresponding unipotent subgroup is the unipotent radical subgroup of the non-maximal standard parabolic
P=L_{P}\cdot U_{P}
, which arises as the intersection of the Siegel parabolic
Q
with the Klingen parabolic. Notice that
P
has Levi
L_{P}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1}^{2}
, given by

\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&&&\\
&g&&\\
&&\mu a^{-1}&\\
&{}&&\mu{{}^{t}g^{-1}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\;:\;a,\mu\in\mathrm{GL}_{1},\;g\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}\right\}.

Following
[
PS18
, §2.1]
, we define a unitary character which we still denote
\psi_{D}:U_{P}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{P}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
as follows. Every element of
U_{P}/[U_{P},U_{P}]
can be expressed as the product of
n(v)\tilde{n}(u)
, where

n(v)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&v_{1}&v_{2}&&&\\
&1&&&&\\
&&1&&&\\
&&&1&&\\
&&&-v_{1}&1&\\
&&&-v_{2}&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbf{G},\;\tilde{n}(u)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&&&&&\\
&1&&&u_{22}&u_{23}\\
&&1&&u_{23}&u_{33}\\
&&&1&&\\
&&&&1&\\
&&&&&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in U_{3}.

We will denote by
N_{v}
(resp.
N_{u}
) the set of the
n(v)
’s (resp.
\tilde{n}(u)
’s).
If
n\equiv n(v)\tilde{n}(u)
modulo
[U_{P},U_{P}]
, define

\psi_{D}(n):=e(v_{1}+u_{33}-Du_{22})=e(v_{1})\psi_{D}(n(u)).

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
. We define the following Fourier coefficients.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\Psi
be a cusp form in the space of
\pi
. Define

\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(g):=\int_{U_{P}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{P}(\mathbf{A})}\psi_{D}^{-1}(u)\Psi(ug)du.

In the following proposition, we relate these Fourier coefficients to the ones for the Siegel parabolic associated to rank 2 symmetric matrices.

Proposition 5.7
.

For a cusp form
\Psi
in the space of
\pi
, the following two conditions are equivalent.

(1)

\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(g)\not\equiv 0
.

(2)

There exists
\alpha\in{\rm Sym}^{{\rm rk}2}(3)({\bf Q})
with
\alpha\sim_{L({\bf Q})}\alpha_{D}
such that
\Psi_{U_{3},\alpha}(g)\not\equiv 0
.

Proof.

Fourier expand
\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(g)
over
N_{v}
to get

\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(g)=\int_{({\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A})^{2}}\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(n(v)g)dv+\sum_{\gamma\in{\rm Stab}_{L}(\psi_{D})({\bf Q})\backslash L({\bf Q})}\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(\gamma g).

The term

\int_{({\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A})^{2}}\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(n(v)g)dv=\int_{N_{u}({\bf Q})\backslash N_{u}(\mathbf{A})}\psi_{D}^{-1}(\tilde{n}(u))\int_{U_{K}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{K}(\mathbf{A})}\Psi(n_{k}\tilde{n}(u)g)dn_{k}d\tilde{n}(u)

and the inner integral vanishes because of cuspidality of
\Psi
along the unipotent radical
U_{K}
of the Klingen parabolic. Thus

\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(g)=\sum_{\gamma}\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(\gamma g).

This relation implies the result as follows. If
\Psi_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(g)\not\equiv 0
, the Fourier coefficient
\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(g)
does not vanish identically. Viceversa, if
\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(g)\not\equiv 0
then there is a character
\psi^{\prime}
in the
L({\bf Q})
-orbit of
\psi_{D}
such that
\Psi_{U_{3},\psi^{\prime}}(g)\not\equiv 0
.
∎

5.3.
The Spin
L
-function and its residue at
s=1

Let
\pi
denote any cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
with trivial central character. Let
S
denote a finite set of places of
{\bf Q}
containing the ones where
\pi
is ramified and the archimedean place. If
{\rm Spin}:{\rm Spin}_{7}({\bf C})\to\mathrm{GL}(V_{8})
denotes the
8
-dimensional spin representation, the partial Spin
L
-function of
\pi
is defined to be

L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin}):=\prod_{\ell\not\in S}\frac{1}{{\rm det}(1-\ell^{-s}{\rm Spin}(s_{\pi_{\ell}}))},

where
s_{\pi_{\ell}}
denotes the Satake parameter of the unramified local component
\pi_{\ell}
. Let
\mathbf{H}
be the group (
3
) associated to the étale cubic algebra
{\bf Q}\times F
, where
F={\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
with either
D\not\equiv 1\in{\bf Q}^{\times}_{>0}/({\bf Q}^{\times})^{2}
, in which case
F
is a real quadratic field, or
D\equiv 1
mod
({\bf Q}^{\times})^{2}
, in which case
F={\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
. For any cusp form
\Psi\in V_{\pi}
, Pollack-Shah
[
PS18
]
give an integral representation

\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\Psi,s)=\int_{{\bf Z}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}E_{\Phi}(h_{1},s)\Psi(h)dh.

of
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
. For any
\Phi
and
\Psi
, the integral
\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\Psi,s)
is absolutely convergent for
Re(s)
big enough and has a meromorphic continuation to
{\bf C}
. According to
[
GG09
, Proposition 7.1]
, for
Re(s)
big enough we have the unfolding

\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\Psi,s)=\int_{U_{B_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{A})Z(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}f_{\Phi}(h_{1},s)\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(h)dh

where
U_{B_{\mathbf{H}}}
is the unipotent radical of the upper triangular Borel subgroup
B_{\mathbf{H}}
of
\mathbf{H}
and
\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}
is the Fourier coefficient of Definition
5.6
.

Theorem 5.8

(
[
PS18
]
)
.

For a set
\Sigma
of places of
{\bf Q}
, denote

\mathcal{I}_{\Sigma}(\Phi,\Psi,s)=\int_{U_{B_{\mathbf{H}}({\bf Q}_{\Sigma})}Z_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf Q}_{\Sigma})\backslash\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}_{\Sigma})}f(h_{1},\Phi_{\Sigma},s)\Psi_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(h)dh.

Let
\Psi
be a cusp form in the space of
\pi
. Then, for any factorizable Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
on
\mathbf{A}^{2}
and up to enlarging
S
, we have

\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\Psi,s)=\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi,s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin}).

Moreover, there exists a cusp form
\tilde{\Psi}
in the space of
\pi
and a factorizable Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
on
\mathbf{A}^{2}
such that

\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\tilde{\Psi},s)=\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi,\Psi,s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin}).

Note that if
\pi
does not support a rank two Fourier coefficient (for the Siegel parabolic
Q
) and thus, by Proposition
5.7
, a Fourier coefficient for
P
, the integral
\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\Psi,s)
is identically zero.

Corollary 5.9

(
[
PS18
]
)
.

Suppose that
\pi
supports a rank two Fourier coefficient. Then, the partial Spin
L
-function
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
has meromorphic continuation in
s
, is holomorphic outside
s=1
, and has at worst a simple pole at
s=1
.

As we explain in the later sections, using results of Gan and Gan-Gurevich, they further prove that when
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
has a simple pole at
s=1
,
\pi
lifts to the split
G_{2}
under the exceptional theta correspondence. This observation is based on the following key relation between the residue at
s=1
of
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
and the automorphic period we have introduced in §
4.3
.

Proposition 5.10
.

For any factorizable Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
on
\mathbf{A}^{2}
, we have

\frac{\widehat{\Phi}(0)}{2}\cdot\int_{{\bf Z}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\Psi(h)dh=\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi,s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})\right).

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
5.1
, the residue at
s=1
of
\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\Psi,s)
equals

\frac{\widehat{\Phi}(0)}{2}\cdot\int_{{\bf Z}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\Psi(h)dh.

The result then follows from Theorem
5.8
.
∎

We now state our first main result. Let
\pi
denote a cuspidal automorphic representation of

{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
for which
\pi_{\infty}
is the discrete series of Hodge type (3,3) in the
L
-packet of
V^{\lambda}
with
\lambda=(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},0)
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},
and
\omega_{\Psi}
be as in §
3.4
and §
4.3
. Let
\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}
denote
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi
, where
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\in U(\mathfrak{k}_{\bf C})
is the operator defined in Proposition
4.8
.

Theorem 5.11
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle=C\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})\right),

where

C=\frac{\widehat{\Phi}(0)h_{{U^{\prime}}}}{2(2\pi i)^{3}\cdot\mathrm{vol}({U^{\prime}})}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.8
and Proposition
4.10
, we have that

\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle=\frac{h_{{U^{\prime}}}}{(2\pi i)^{3}\cdot\mathrm{vol}({U^{\prime}})}\int_{\mathbf{H}({\bf Q}){\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}(h)dh,

where
U^{\prime}=U\cap\mathbf{H}({\bf A}_{f})
and
h_{U^{\prime}}=4^{-1}|{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf Q})\backslash{\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A}_{f})/({\bf Z}_{\mathbf{G}}({\bf A}_{f})\cap{U^{\prime}})|
. By Proposition
5.10
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle

\displaystyle=C\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})\right)

where

C=\frac{\widehat{\Phi}(0)h_{{U^{\prime}}}}{2(2\pi i)^{3}\cdot\mathrm{vol}({U^{\prime}})}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Let us fix a Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
such that
\widehat{\Phi}(0)\neq 0
.

Corollary 5.12
.

Suppose that
\pi
satisfies the following hypotheses:

—

\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)\neq 0
for some
\mu
,

—

the partial
L
-function
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
has a pole at
s=1
.

Then

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle=\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\neq 0.

Proof.

By
[
PS18
, Theorem 1.3]
the function
L^{S}(s,\pi,\mathrm{Spin})=1
has at most a simple pole. As a consequence
\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})\neq 0
. By Theorem
5.11
, under the assumption that
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)\neq 0
, this implies that
\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle\neq 0
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

If the automorphic representation
\pi
supports a rank two Fourier coefficient and its partial Spin
L
-function has a (necessarily simple) pole at
s=1
, by the results of
[
PS18
]
it is
\mathbf{H}
-distinguished, namely the map
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{H}}\in{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}(\pi,\mathbf{1})
defined by

\Psi\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{H}}(\Psi):=\int_{Z(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\Psi(h)dh

is not identically zero. Then, asking
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)\neq 0
for some
\mu
is equivalent to asking that the map obtained as the composition of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{H}}
with an
\mathbf{H}({\bf R})
-equivariant embedding
\pi_{\infty}\to\pi
restricts non-trivially to the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type of
\pi_{\infty}
.

Let
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}
denote the
F
-vector space defined in section
3.4.3
and let
H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}[\pi_{f}^{\vee}]
denote its
\pi_{f}^{\vee}
-isotypical component. This is a finite dimensional
L
-vector space, where
L
is the number field introduced in
\lx@sectionsign

2.8
. Tate conjecture for the motive attached to
\pi
(see Conjecture
1.1
(3)) predicts the equality

-\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L(s,\pi,\mathrm{Spin})=\dim_{L}H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}[\pi_{f}^{\vee}].

Corollary 5.14
.

If
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)\neq 0
, then we have

-\mathrm{ord}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,\mathrm{Spin})\leq\dim_{L}H^{6}_{\mathcal{M}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U),\mathscr{V}^{\lambda}_{F}(3))_{hom}[\pi_{f}^{\vee}].

Proof.

If
L^{S}(s,\pi,\mathrm{Spin})
does not have a pole at
s=1
, there is nothing to prove. If not, Corollary
5.12
implies that the projection of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},B}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
to the
\pi_{f}^{\vee}
-isotypical component is non-zero, showing the result.
∎

The following result verifies a weaker form of Conjecture 1.1(3) for the motive
M(\pi_{f}^{\vee})(3)
at the cost of supposing that
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)
is non-zero for some
\mu
.

Corollary 5.15
.

Suppose that
\pi
satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary
5.12
and that
(St)
holds. Then
{\rm pr}_{\pi^{\vee}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
and its Hecke translates generate
H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f}^{\vee})_{{\bf R}}(4))
.

Proof.

If
\pi_{p}
is the Steinberg representation, it follows from the second statement of Lemma
2.11
and its proof, that
H^{1}_{\mathcal{H}}(M(\pi_{f}^{\vee})_{\bf R}(4))
is a rank one module over the full Hecke algebra of level
U
. Hence the result follows by Corollary
5.12
.
∎

6.
Exceptional theta lifts from
G_{2}
to
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}

In this section, we discuss the exceptional theta correspondence for the dual reductive pair
(G_{2},{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6})
and describe the set of Fourier coefficients associated to the Heisenberg parabolic for cuspidal automorphic forms of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
. It has as its solely purpose to fix notations and to recall some well known results that will be used later so that the knowledgeable reader might skip it.

6.1.
Split
G_{2}
and
E_{7}

In this section we will follow the exposition of the Appendix of
[
HKT23
]
by Savin.

6.1.1.
The group
G_{2}

Let
\mathbb{H}
be the algebra of Hamilton quaternions over
{\bf Q}
with the usual basis
\{1,i,j,k\}
. The conjugate
\bar{a}
of an element
a=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}i+\alpha_{2}j+\alpha_{3}k\in\mathbb{H}
is
\bar{a}=\alpha_{0}-\alpha_{1}i-\alpha_{2}j-\alpha_{3}k
. The split octonion algebra over
{\bf Q}
is
\mathbb{O}=\mathbb{H}\oplus\mathbb{H}
with multiplication

(a,b)\cdot(c,d)=(ac+d\bar{b},\bar{a}d+cb).

Then
\mathbb{O}
is a non-commutative, non-associative
{\bf Q}
-algebra. However it is alternative, which means that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{O}
we have
x\cdot(x\cdot y)=(x\cdot x)\cdot y
and
(x\cdot y)\cdot y=x\cdot(y\cdot y)
(see
[
Jac58
]
). If
x=(a,b)
, let
\overline{x}=(\overline{a},-b)
. Then
x\mapsto\overline{x}
is a
{\bf Q}
-linear involution on
\mathbb{O}
satisfying
\overline{x\cdot y}=\overline{y}\cdot\overline{x}
. The norm
\mathrm{N}:\mathbb{O}\rightarrow{\bf Q}
is the quadratic form defined by
x\mapsto x\cdot\overline{x}=\overline{x}\cdot x
.
The trace
\mathrm{Tr}:\mathbb{O}\rightarrow{\bf Q}
is defined by
x\mapsto x+\overline{x}
.
For any
x,y,z\in\mathbb{O}
, the properties

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}(x\cdot y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{N}(x)\mathrm{N}(y),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(x\cdot y)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}(y\cdot x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(x\cdot(y\cdot z))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}((x\cdot y)\cdot z)

are satisfied. For
x,y\in\mathbb{O}
, we write
y\in x^{\perp}
if
y
is orthogonal to
x
with respect to the bilinear form
(x,y)\mapsto{\rm Tr}(x\cdot\overline{y})
, which means that
x\cdot\overline{y}+y\cdot\overline{x}=0
.

Let
l=(0,1)\in\mathbb{O}
so that
\{1,i,j,k,l,li,lj,lk\}
is a basis of
\mathbb{O}
. From this, one constructs another useful basis
\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}\}
, where

s_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(i+li),s_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(j+lj),s_{3}=\frac{1}{2}(k+lk),s_{4}=\frac{1}{2}(1+l),

t_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(i-li),t_{2}=\frac{1}{2}(j-lj),t_{3}=\frac{1}{2}(k-lk),t_{4}=\frac{1}{2}(1-l).

The following multiplication table for this basis is given in Table 1 of the Appendix of
[
HKT23
]
.

s_{1}

s_{2}

s_{3}

t_{1}

t_{2}

t_{3}

s_{4}

t_{4}

s_{1}

0

-t_{3}

t_{2}

s_{4}

0

0

0

s_{1}

s_{2}

t_{3}

0

-t_{1}

0

s_{4}

0

0

s_{2}

s_{3}

-t_{2}

t_{1}

0

0

0

s_{4}

0

s_{3}

t_{1}

t_{4}

0

0

0

s_{3}

-s_{2}

t_{1}

0

t_{2}

0

t_{4}

0

-s_{3}

0

s_{1}

t_{2}

0

t_{3}

0

0

t_{4}

s_{2}

-s_{1}

0

t_{3}

0

s_{4}

s_{1}

s_{2}

s_{3}

0

0

0

s_{4}

0

t_{4}

0

0

0

t_{1}

t_{2}

t_{3}

0

t_{4}

We define

G_{2}:=\{g\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{O})\,|\,g(x\cdot y)=(gx)\cdot(gy),\forall x,y\in\mathbb{O}\}.

to be the group of automorphisms of
\mathbb{O}
. We note that
G_{2}
acts transitively on non-zero elements of trace zero and norm zero. We will denote the set of trace zero octonions by either
\mathbb{O}^{0}
or
V_{7}
, where the latter notation emphasises that this set defines the standard irreducible 7-dimensional representation of
G_{2}
and induces an embedding

G_{2}\hookrightarrow{\rm SO}_{7}.

6.1.2.
The dual reductive pair

We consider the Albert algebra
J
over
{\bf Q}
, which is the set of matrices

A=\begin{pmatrix}d&\overline{z}&y\\
z&e&\overline{x}\\
\overline{y}&x&f\end{pmatrix}

where
d,e,f\in{\bf Q}
and
x,y,z\in\mathbb{O}
. The algebra
J
is equipped with a cubic form, called the determinant, which is given by

\mathrm{det}(A)=def-dN(x)-eN(y)-fN(z)+\mathrm{Tr}(zyx).

The group of isogenies of this form is a group of type
E_{6}
and its orbits on
J
are classified by the rank. We will need to consider the set
\Omega
of rank
1
elements
A\in J
, i.e. those
A\neq 0
such that
A^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}(A)\cdot A
. This condition means that the entries of
A
satisfy the equalities

(10)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathrm{N}(x)=ef,\;\;\mathrm{N}(y)=df,\;\;\mathrm{N}(z)=de,}\\
{dx=\overline{y}\cdot\overline{z},\;\;ey=\overline{z}\cdot\overline{x},\;\;fz=\overline{x}\cdot\overline{y}.}\end{array}

Let
G
denote the split adjoint group of type
E_{7}
, which is constructed from
J
by the Koecher-Tits construction (see Section 3 of
[
KS15
]
). The group
G
has a maximal parabolic
P=MN
and its opposite
\bar{P}=M\bar{N}
, with
N\simeq J
and such that the action under conjugation of the Levi
M
on
N
gives an isomorphism of
M
and the group of similitudes of the cubic form on
J

M\cong\{g\in\mathrm{GL}(J)\,|\,\mathrm{det}(gA)=\lambda\mathrm{det}(A)\text{ for some }\lambda\in\mathbf{G}_{m}\text{ and }\forall A\in J\}.

The group
G_{2}
can be realized as a subgroup of
M
via its action on
J
by the rule

g\cdot\begin{pmatrix}d&\overline{z}&y\\
z&e&\overline{x}\\
\overline{y}&x&f\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d&g\overline{z}&gy\\
gz&e&g\overline{x}\\
g\overline{y}&gx&f\end{pmatrix}.

This action has fixed points
J_{3}
, the Jordan algebra of symmetric
3\times 3
matrices with entries in
{\bf Q}
. Note that the left action of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
on
J_{3}\cong N
given by

(11)

g\cdot A=\mathrm{det}(g)^{-1}gAg^{t}

extends to an action on
J
preserving the determinant form up to scalar, thus defining an embedding of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
into
M
. Then
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
is the centraliser of
G_{2}
in
M
and
Q=\mathrm{GL}_{3}U_{3}
(which is the Siegel parabolic of
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
) is the centralizer of
G_{2}
in
P
. Similarly, the opposite
\overline{Q}
is the centralizer of
G_{2}
in
\overline{P}
. This gives the dual reductive pair
(G_{2},\mathrm{PGSp}_{6})
in
G
.

6.2.
Fourier coefficients for
G_{2}

6.2.1.
Root system and parabolic subgroups

Let
T
be a (rank 2) maximal split torus over
{\bf Q}
in
G_{2}
and let
\Delta
, resp.
\Delta^{+}\subset\Delta
, be the set of roots, resp. a subset of positive roots, for
G_{2}
. Let
a
, resp.
b
, denote the long, resp. short, simple root in
\Delta^{+}
. Then

\Delta^{+}=\{a,b,a+b,a+2b,a+3b,2a+3b\}.

We let
B=TU
denote the Borel subgroup of
G_{2}
associated to
\Delta^{+}
. Other than
B
, there are two proper standard parabolic subgroups
P_{a}
and
P_{b}
of
G_{2}
, such that
P_{a}\cap P_{b}=B
. They are characterized by the following. For any
\alpha\in\Delta^{+}
, denote by
x_{\alpha}:\mathbf{G}_{a}\hookrightarrow U

the one parameter unipotent subgroup associated to
\alpha
. Then, for each
r\in\{a,b\}
, the Levi
L_{r}
of
P_{r}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
and contains
x_{r}
. We fix an isomorphism
\mathrm{GL}_{2}\simeq L_{r}
such that
{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&u\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}\mapsto x_{r}(u)
.

Let
U_{a}
be the unipotent radical of
P_{a}
. It is a 3-step nilpotent group of dimension 5 with filtration

U_{a}\supset U_{1}\supset U_{2}\supset\{1\},

where
U_{a}/U_{1}
is generated by
\{x_{b},x_{a+b}\}
,
U_{1}/U_{2}
is isomorphic to the one parameter unipotent subgroup
x_{a+2b}
, and
U_{2}
is generated by
\{x_{a+3b},x_{2a+3b}\}
. As representations of
L_{a}
,
U_{a}/U_{1}
is the standard representation, while
U_{1}/U_{2}
is the determinant (cf.
[
GS23
, §2.4]
).

We denote by
H:=P_{b}
the so-called Heisenberg parabolic and let
L_{H}U_{H}
denote its Levi decomposition. The unipotent radical
U_{H}
is of dimension 5 and admits the filtration

U_{H}\supset[U_{H},U_{H}]\supset\{1\},

with
U_{H}/[U_{H},U_{H}]
being the four dimensional abelian unipotent group generated by

\{x_{a},x_{a+b},x_{a+2b},x_{a+3b}\},

while
[U_{H},U_{H}]
is isomorphic to the one parameter unipotent subgroup
x_{2a+3b}
.

6.2.2.
An embedding of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
into
G_{2}

The group
G_{2}
acts transitively on the set

\Gamma_{c}:=\{x\in\mathbb{O}^{0}\,|\,N(x)=-c\}.

By
[
Jac58
, Theorem 4]
, the stabilizer of an element
y_{0}\in\Gamma_{1}
is isomorphic to
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
. Choose
y_{0}
such that the unipotent radical
U_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}}
of the upper triangular Borel of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
is generated by the one-parameter subgroups

\{x_{a},x_{a+3b},x_{2a+3b}\}.

In terms of the basis chosen in §
6.1.1
, this is achieved by choosing
y_{0}=s_{4}-t_{4}
. In this case, one shows (cf.
[
RS89
, Lemma 2]
) that the stabilizer of
y_{0}
leaves invariant the subspace
\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle
and is identified with
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}={\mathrm{SL}}(\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle)
.

6.2.3.
The Lie algebra of
G_{2}

The multiplication map on
\mathbb{O}
induces a map
V_{7}\otimes V_{7}\to V_{7}
given by
x\otimes y\mapsto\frac{xy-yx}{2}
. This map is alternating, hence it induces a
G_{2}
-equivariant map
\wedge^{2}V_{7}\to V_{7}
which is surjective.
Then the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
of
G_{2}
can be identified with the kernel of this map. Under this identification, one has an explicit description of the action of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
on
V_{7}
, namely

(w\wedge x)\cdot v=\langle x,v\rangle w-\langle w,v\rangle x.

We will also need (cf.
[
FH91
, §22.2]
) the decomposition

(12)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{2}=\mathfrak{sl}_{3}\oplus{\rm Std}_{3}\oplus{\rm Std}_{3}^{*},

where
{\rm Std}_{3}
is the standard representation of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
with basis
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and
{\rm Std}_{3}^{*}
is its dual with basis
\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3}\}
and where we denote by
E_{ij}
,
1\leq i<j\leq 3
the standard basis vectors of
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
. The identification between the two descriptions (cf.
[
Pol21
, §2.2]
) of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is given by
E_{ij}=t_{j}\wedge s_{i}
,
1\leq i<j\leq 3
,
v_{i}=(s_{4}-t_{4})\wedge s_{i}+t_{i+1}\wedge t_{i+2}
and
\delta_{i}=(s_{4}-t_{4})\wedge t_{i}+s_{i+1}\wedge s_{i+2}
,
1\leq i\leq 3
, where indices are taken modulo
3
. Moreover, the component
\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
is the Lie algebra of the copy of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
embedded into
G_{2}
as above. In particular,
E_{12},E_{13}
and
E_{23}
are root vectors for the roots
a,2a+3b
and
a+3b
respectively. Moreover, the vectors
v_{1},v_{2}
and
\delta_{3}
are root vectors for the roots
a+b,b
and
a+2b
, respectively. Via (
12
), the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}_{H}
of
U_{H}
is

(13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}_{H}=\mathfrak{u}_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}}\oplus{\bf Q}v_{1}\oplus{\bf Q}\delta_{3},

Under (
12
) the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{l}_{H}
of the Levi
L_{H}
is generated by the Cartan subalgebra and the root vectors
v_{2}
,
\delta_{2}
.

6.2.4.
Fourier coefficients

We now describe the Fourier coefficients for
G_{2}
associated to the Heisenberg parabolic. We closely follow
[
Pol21
]
and refer to it for more details. In order to describe the Fourier coefficients associated to
H
, we need to study the
L_{H}
-representation
V_{H}:=U_{H}/[U_{H},U_{H}]
. As a
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
-representation,
V_{H}
is isomorphic to
{\rm Sym}^{3}({\rm Std}_{2})\otimes{\rm det}^{-1}({\rm Std}_{2})
, where
{\rm Std}_{2}
denotes the standard representation of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
. Under the identification of (
13
), (a representative of) an element of
V_{H}({\bf Q})
can be written as

x_{a}(\lambda_{1})x_{a+b}(\lambda_{2}/3)x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{3}/3)x_{a+3b}(\lambda_{4}),\;\text{ with }\lambda_{i}\in{\bf Q},

which corresponds to the binary cubic polynomial

p(x,y)=\lambda_{1}x^{3}+\lambda_{2}x^{2}y+\lambda_{3}xy^{2}+\lambda_{4}y^{3}

where
x,y
form a basis of
{\rm Std}_{2}
. Associated to
p
, there is the cubic
{\bf Q}
-algebra
R
with basis
\{1,i,j\}
with multiplicative table

\displaystyle ij

\displaystyle=-ad

\displaystyle i^{2}

\displaystyle=-ac+bi-aj

\displaystyle j^{2}

\displaystyle=-bd+di-cj.

Example 6.1
.

(1)

(
[
GL09
, 3.2]
)
If
p(x,y)=x^{2}y-xy^{2}
then the associated
{\bf Q}
-algebra
R
is isomorphic to
{\bf Q}^{3}
.

(2)

(
[
GL09
, 3.3]
) If
p(x,y)=x^{3}-Dxy^{2}
(or equivalently
p(x,y)=-Dx^{2}y+y^{3}
using the action of
{{\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)}}
) then the associated
{\bf Q}
-algebra
R
is isomorphic to
{\bf Q}\oplus{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
.

There is an action of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\bf Q})
on the set of bases
\{1,i,j\}
of a given cubic algebra
R
, which makes the association
p(X,Y)\mapsto(R,\{1,i,j\})

\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\bf Q})
-equivariant. Since any cubic algebra admits a basis of this shape, we have the following.

Proposition 6.2

(
[
GL09
, Proposition 2.1]
)
.

There is a bijection between the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\bf Q})
-orbits on
V_{H}({\bf Q})
and the set of isomorphism classes of cubic
{\bf Q}
-algebras. Moreover, each orbit has a well-defined discriminant in
{\bf Q}^{\times}/({\bf Q}^{\times})^{2}
.

Let
e:{\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A}\to{\bf C}^{\times}
be the additive character introduced in §
5.2.1
. Let
\langle,\rangle
denote the symplectic pairing on
V_{H}
defined as follows. If
v,v^{\prime}\in V_{H}
correspond to
p(x,y)
and
p^{\prime}(x,y)
respectively, then

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{4}^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{3}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{3}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}-\lambda_{4}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}.

Any character
\psi:U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
factors through
V_{H}(\mathbf{A})
, hence we consider the projection
\bar{n}
of
n\in U_{H}(\mathbf{A})
to
V_{H}(\mathbf{A})
, which, by (
13
), can be written as

\overline{n}=x_{a}(\lambda_{1}^{\prime})x_{a+b}(\lambda_{2}^{\prime}/3)x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{3}^{\prime}/3)x_{a+3b}(\lambda_{4}^{\prime}).

If
v\in V_{H}({\bf Q})
corresponds to
p(x,y)
, we then define
\psi_{v}:U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
by

n\mapsto e(\langle v,\overline{n}\rangle)=e(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{4}^{\prime}-\tfrac{1}{3}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}^{\prime}+\tfrac{1}{3}\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}-\lambda_{4}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}).

The character
\psi_{v}
is non-degenerate if and only if
v
corresponds to an étale cubic algebra over
{\bf Q}
. In this manuscript, we are interested in étale cubic algebras of the form
{\bf Q}\times F
, with
F
of either the form
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
(with
{\bf Q}^{\times}/({\bf Q}^{\times})^{2}\ni D\not\equiv 1
) or
{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
(with
D\equiv 1
mod
({\bf Q}^{\times})^{2}
).

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\psi_{H,D}:U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
denote the character associated to
{\bf Q}\times F
. Given a cusp form
\varphi
for
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, define

\varphi_{U_{H},\psi_{H,D}}(g):=\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\psi^{-1}_{H,D}(n)\varphi(ng)dn.

6.3.
The theta lift from
G_{2}
to
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}

Let
\Pi=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\Pi_{v}
denote the restricted tensor product of the minimal representations
\Pi_{v}
of
E_{7}({\bf Q}_{v})
over all places
v
of
{\bf Q}
. A unitary model of the minimal representation is given by
L^{2}(\Omega)
, where recall that
\Omega
denotes the subset of rank 1 elements in
J
. There is a unique up to a non-zero scalar embedding

\theta:\Pi\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(E_{7}({\bf Q})\backslash E_{7}(\mathbf{A}))

of
\Pi
in the space
\mathcal{A}(E_{7}({\bf Q})\backslash E_{7}(\mathbf{A}))
of automorphic forms of
E_{7}
(see
[
GRS97a
]
,
[
KS15
]
). For
f\in\Pi
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A}))
, we define a function
\Theta(f,\varphi)
on
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
by

\Theta(f,\varphi)(g)=\int_{G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)(g^{\prime}g)\varphi(g^{\prime})dg^{\prime}.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
.

(1)

Define
\Theta(\sigma)
to be the span of the functions
\Theta(f,\varphi)
, where
f\in\Pi
and
\varphi
runs through the cusp forms in the contragradient
\sigma^{\vee}
of
\sigma
.

(2)

We say that a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
is a
\Theta
-lift of
\sigma
if it appears as an irreducible subquotient of
\Theta(\sigma)
.

If a
\Theta
-lift of
\sigma
exists, then its local constituents are compatible with the local Theta correspondence between
G_{2}
and
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\pi
be a
\Theta
-lift of
\sigma
, then
\pi_{v}
is an irreducible subquotient of
\Theta(\sigma_{v})
.

Proof.

See
[
HKT23
, Theorem 1.7 (i)]
.
∎

After imposing certain local conditions on
\sigma
, in the next section we use one of the main results of
[
GRS97b
]
to show that
\Theta(\sigma)
is non-zero and cuspidal, thus proving the existence of a non-trivial
\Theta
-lift of
\sigma
. Before doing so, we first recall the properties of the local theta correspondence needed later.

6.3.1.
Discrete series and a conjecture of Gross

Let
T_{c}
denote a compact torus in
G_{2}({\bf R})
, which is contained in the maximal compact subgroup
K_{G_{2}}\simeq(\mathrm{SU}_{2}\times\mathrm{SU}_{2})/\mu_{2}
of
G_{2}({\bf R})
. We abuse notation denoting again by
a,b
the simple positive roots for
T_{c}
(with the short root
b
which we assume to be compact) and
\Delta^{+}
the resulting set of positive roots. Then,
\rho=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}\alpha=3a+5b
. The set of positive compact roots is given by

\Delta_{c}^{+}=\{b,2a+3b\},

which, in the notation of
[
Li97
]
, is
\{2\varepsilon_{2},2\varepsilon_{1}\}
. The Weyl group
\mathfrak{W}_{G_{2}}
is isomorphic to the dihedral group
D_{6}
of 12 elements and it is generated by
w_{a}
and
w_{b}
, where
w_{\alpha}
denotes the reflections around the line orthogonal to
\alpha
. The Weyl group
\mathfrak{W}_{K_{G_{2}}}\simeq({\bf Z}/2{\bf Z})^{2}
is generated by
w_{b}
and
w_{2a+3b}=w_{a}w_{b}w_{a}w_{b}w_{a}
.

Let
\gamma
be a dominant weight for
G_{2}
with respect to
T_{c}
. The set of equivalence classes of irreducible discrete series of
G_{2}({\bf R})
associated to
\gamma
has cardinality equal to
|\mathfrak{W}_{G_{2}}/\mathfrak{W}_{K_{G_{2}}}|=3
. Choose representatives
\{w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\}
of
\mathfrak{W}_{G_{2}}/\mathfrak{W}_{K_{G_{2}}}
such that

w_{i}\rho
is dominant for
K_{G_{2}}
. Then, for any
1\leq i\leq 3
, there exists an irreducible discrete series
\sigma_{\infty}^{\Gamma}
of Harish-Chandra parameter
\Gamma=w_{i}(\gamma+\rho)
and minimal
K_{G_{2}}
-type
\Gamma+\delta_{G_{2}}-2\delta_{K_{G_{2}}}
, where
\delta_{G_{2}}
, resp.
\delta_{K_{G_{2}}}
, is the half-sum of roots, resp. compact roots, which are positive with respect to the Weyl chamber in which
\Gamma
lies. Precisely, if we let
w_{1}={\rm id}
,
w_{2}=w_{a}
, and
w_{3}=w_{b}w_{a}
, then

\displaystyle w_{1}\rho

\displaystyle=\rho=3\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2},

\displaystyle w_{2}\rho

\displaystyle=2a+5b=2\varepsilon_{1}+4\varepsilon_{2},

\displaystyle w_{3}\rho

\displaystyle=a+4b=\varepsilon_{1}+5\varepsilon_{2}.

We let
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2,4}
, and
\mathcal{D}_{1,5}
denote the sets of discrete series of
G_{2}({\bf R})
whose Harish-Chandra parameter lies in the Weyl chamber corresponding to
w_{1}\rho
,
w_{2}\rho
, and
w_{3}\rho
respectively. Elements of
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
are the quaternionic discrete series, while elements of
\mathcal{D}_{2,4}
are the generic discrete series.

Gross has given a precise conjectural description of the entire discrete spectrum of the dual pair
(G_{2},{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6})
(cf.
[
Li97
, Conjecture 1.2]
). Recall that there are four families of discrete series for
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
, indexed by the set of Hodge types up to conjugation. In particular, the discrete series of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
of Hodge type
(4,2)
, resp.
(6,0)
, are the generic, resp. holomorphic discrete series.

Conjecture 6.6

(Gross)
.

Let
\Pi_{\infty}
be the minimal representation of
E_{7}({\bf R})
. The discrete spectrum of the restriction of
\Pi_{\infty}
to the dual pair
G_{2}({\bf R})\times{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
is the direct sum of all tensor products
\sigma_{\infty}\otimes\theta(\sigma_{\infty})
, where
\sigma_{\infty}
belongs to the discrete series of
G_{2}
. If
\sigma_{\infty}
has infinitesimal character
\gamma+\rho=r\varepsilon_{1}+s\varepsilon_{2}
and belongs to either
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2,4}
, or
\mathcal{D}_{1,5}
, then
\theta(\sigma_{\infty})
is the discrete series of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
with infinitesimal character
(r,\tfrac{1}{2}(r+s),\tfrac{1}{2}(r-s))
and Hodge type
(3,3)
,
(4,2)
, or
(5,1)
respectively.

Partial results towards the conjecture of Gross were showed by Li for discrete series in
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
(cf. §
6.3.2
below) and for the generic family
\mathcal{D}_{2,4}
by Harris-Khare-Thorne in
[
HKT23
, Theorem 1.5 and Theorem 1.7(ii)]
using the main result of Savin’s appendix to
[
HKT23
]
and the non-vanishing of the global theta lift given by
[
GRS97b
, Corollary 4.2]
. Li also gave evidence to the predictions of Gross for a proper subset
\mathcal{D}_{1,5}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{D}_{1,5}
in
[
Li97
, Theorem 4.3]
. We also note that the remaining equivalence class of holomorphic discrete series of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
(of Hodge type
(6,0)
) is realized in an exceptional theta correspondence studied by Gross-Savin between the compact real form
G_{2}^{c}({\bf R})
of
G_{2}
and
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf R})
and moreover this is the only Hodge type that appears in that correspondence (cf.
[
GS98
, Theorem 3.5]
).

6.3.2.
Quaternionic discrete series and their theta lift

We describe the main result of
[
Li97
]
. We first notice that a discrete series
\sigma_{\infty}^{x,y}
of Harish-Chandra parameter
x\varepsilon_{1}+y\varepsilon_{2}
lies in the set of quaternionic discrete series
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
if
x,y
are two non-negative integers such that
x-3\geq y-1\geq 0
and
x-y
is even. The minimal
K_{G_{2}}
-type of
\sigma_{\infty}^{x,y}\in\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
is given by

{\rm Sym}^{x+1}({\rm Std}_{\varepsilon_{1}})\boxtimes{\rm Sym}^{y-1}({\rm Std}_{\varepsilon_{2}}),

where
{\rm Std}_{\varepsilon_{1}}
(resp.
{\rm Std}_{\varepsilon_{2}}
) is the standard representation of the
\mathrm{SU}_{2}
corresponding to the long root
\varepsilon_{1}
(resp. the short root
\varepsilon_{2}
).

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
\Pi_{\infty}
denote the minimal representation of
E_{7}({\bf R})
. We have

{\Pi_{\infty}}|_{{G_{2}({\bf R})\times\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}({\bf R})}}\supseteq\bigoplus_{\sigma_{\infty}^{x,y}\in\mathcal{D}_{3,1}}\sigma_{\infty}^{x,y}\otimes\theta(\sigma_{\infty}^{x,y})

where
\theta(\sigma_{\infty}^{x,y})\in P(V^{\lambda}),
with
\lambda=(x-3,\tfrac{1}{2}(x+y)-2,\tfrac{1}{2}(x-y)-1,0)
, is the discrete series
\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}
of Hodge type
(3,3)
and Harish-Chandra parameter
(\tfrac{1}{2}(x+y),\tfrac{1}{2}(x-y),-x)
.

Proof.

See
[
Li97
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
HPS96
, Theorem 5.4]
.
∎

The set
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
contains an important family of discrete series, which were studied by Gross and Wallach in
[
GW94
]
and
[
GW96
]
.

Definition 6.8
.

For every
n\geq 2
, the quaternionic discrete series
\sigma_{n}
is the element of
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
of Harish-Chandra parameter
(2n-1)\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}
and minimal
K_{G_{2}}
-type

{\rm Sym}^{2n}({\rm Std}_{\varepsilon_{1}})\boxtimes\mathbf{1}.

A fundamental property of the members of this family is that they admit (unique) models with respect to the unipotent radical of the Heisenberg parabolic and non-degenerate characters corresponding to totally real étale cubic algebras. Recall, as in §
6.2.4
, that a non-degenerate character
\psi:U_{H}({\bf R})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
corresponds to a cubic algebra, whose discriminant is either positive or negative. The first type corresponds to the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\bf R})
