-orbit on
V_{H}({\bf R})
given by
{\bf R}^{3}
, while the second to the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\bf R})
-orbit of
{\bf R}\times{\bf C}
. A representative
\psi:U_{H}({\bf R})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
of the totally real orbit is given by
e^{2\pi if}
, where
f:U_{H}({\bf R})\to{\bf R}
is non-zero on the one parameter unipotent subgroups
x_{a+b}
and
x_{a+2b}
and trivial on
x_{a}
and
x_{a+3b}
(cf.
[
GGS02
, §6]
). A special case of the main result of
[
Wal03
]
gives the following.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
\psi
be a non-degenerate character of
V_{H}({\bf R})
. There is (at most) a one dimensional space of
\psi
-equivariant linear functionals on
\sigma_{n}
. Moreover,

{\rm dim}\;{\rm Hom}_{U_{H}({\bf R})}(\sigma_{n},\psi)=1,

exactly when
\psi
corresponds to a totally real cubic algebra.

6.3.3.
The non-archimedean theta correspondence

We describe the properties of the non-archimedean theta correspondence which will be later needed to study the global theta correspondence. Let
\sigma
be an irreducible admissible representation of
G_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
. The maximal
\sigma
-isotypic quotient of the minimal representation
\Pi_{p}
of
E_{7}({{\bf Q}_{p}})
can be expressed as
\sigma\boxtimes\Theta(\sigma)
, with
\Theta(\sigma)
a smooth representation of
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}({\bf Q}_{p})
which is called the big theta lift of
\sigma
.

Proposition 6.10
.

For an irreducible admissible representation
\sigma
of
G_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
,
\Theta(\sigma)
has finite length with unique irreducible quotient (if non-zero)
\theta(\sigma)
. Moreover, one has the following.

(1)

Let
\sigma
be an unramified generic representation of
G_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
with Satake parameter
s
, then
\pi=\theta(\sigma)
is the unramified representation of
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}({\bf Q}_{p})
whose Satake parameter is
\varphi\circ s
, where
\varphi:G_{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{Spin}_{7}}
is the map of
L
-groups.

(2)

Let
{\rm St}_{G_{2}}
(resp.
{\rm St}_{\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}}
) be the Steinberg representation of
G_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
(resp.
{\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}}({\bf Q}_{p})
); then
\theta({\rm St}_{G_{2}})={\rm St}_{\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}}
.

Proof.

See
[
GS23
, Theorem 1.2, Theorem 15.3(v)]
and
[
GS98
, Proposition 3.1]
.
∎

7.
Cuspidality and Fourier coefficients of the global theta lift

In this section, based on the works
[
GRS97b
]
,
[
GS98
]
, and the appendix of Savin in
[
HKT23
]
, we give a criterion on the cuspidality of representations in the image of the exceptional theta lift and on their possession of Fourier coefficients of type
(4\,2)
.

7.1.
Cuspidality of the global lift

Let
V
denote the unipotent subgroup of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
(embedded into
G_{2}
as in §
6.2.2
) generated by the roots
a+3b
and
2a+3b
. We further consider the subgroup
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
embedded into
G_{2}
via the Levi of the “long root” parabolic
P_{a}
and denote, for any cusp form
\varphi
for
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
,

\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g):=\int_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{V({\bf Q})\backslash V(\mathbf{A})}\varphi(vmg)dvdm.

We will now show that the above period vanishes whenever
\varphi
is not globally generic. We are thankful to David Ginzburg for kindly sharing with us a proof of this fact.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, which is not globally generic. For any cusp form
\varphi\in V_{\sigma}
and
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, we have
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g)=0
.

Proof.

Let
Z
denote the unipotent subgroup of
G_{2}
generated by the roots
a+2b,a+3b,
and
2a+3b
. Let
\varphi\in V_{\sigma}
. If we Fourier expand the period
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g)
along the 1-dimensional unipotent subgroup
x_{a+2b}(r)
of
G_{2}
, we get

\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g)=\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z}(g)+\sum_{\psi}\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(g),

where the sum runs over non-trivial additive characters
\psi:Z({\bf Q})\backslash Z(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
supported on the root
a+2b
,
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z}(g)
is the period of
\varphi
over
[{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z]
, and

\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(g):=\int_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{Z({\bf Q})\backslash Z(\mathbf{A})}\varphi(umg)\psi(u)dudm.

By
[
GRS97b
, Lemma 2.1]
,
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z}(g)=0
for all
\varphi
in
\sigma
and
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
. Hence,
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g)=0
if and only if
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(g)=0
for all non-trivial
\psi
. We now argue by contradiction. Suppose that
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(g)\neq 0
for a certain
\psi
. We claim that this implies that
\sigma
supports Whittaker Fourier coefficients, thus contradicting our hypothesis.

Let
U_{a}
be the unipotent radical of
P_{a}
introduced in §
6.2.1
. Since
V
is normal in
U_{a}
, we can consider the quotient
V_{0}=U_{a}/V
, which is isomorphic to the Heisenberg group in three variables and it is generated by the roots
b,a+b,
and
a+2b
. The center of
V_{0}
is generated by the root
a+2b
and is identified with the quotient
Z_{0}:=Z/V
. As
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
is embedded into
G_{2}
via the Levi
L_{a}
of
P_{a}
, it acts trivially on the quotient
Z_{0}
. Therefore,
D:={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V_{0}
is a Jacobi group in the sense of
[
Ike94
, Definition in p.619]
. Let
\widetilde{D(\mathbf{A}}):=\widetilde{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A}})V_{0}(\mathbf{A})
, with
\widetilde{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A}})
denoting the metaplectic cover of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, and denote by
C^{\infty}_{\psi}(D({\bf Q})\backslash\widetilde{D(\mathbf{A}}))
the space of functions
f
on
D({\bf Q})\backslash\widetilde{D(\mathbf{A}})
such that
f(zvh)=\psi(z)f(vh)
for any
z\in Z_{0}(\mathbf{A})
,
v\in V_{0}(\mathbf{A})
,
h\in\widetilde{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A}})
. For any Schwartz function
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A})
, we let
\theta^{\Phi}_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}\in C_{\psi}^{\infty}(D({\bf Q})\backslash\widetilde{D(\mathbf{A})})
be the theta function defined in
[
Ike94
, p. 620]
.
By
[
Ike94
, Proposition 1.3]
, if
W
is a closed subspace of
C^{\infty}_{\psi^{-1}}(D({\bf Q})\backslash\widetilde{D(\mathbf{A}}))
which is invariant under right translation of
V_{0}(\mathbf{A})
, the functions of the form

(14)

vh\mapsto\overline{\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{1}}}(vh)\int_{V_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash V_{0}(\mathbf{A})}f(uh)\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{2}}(uh)du,

with
v\in V_{0}(\mathbf{A})
,
h\in\widetilde{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A}})
,
f\in W
,
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A})
, generate a dense subspace of
W
. We apply this to the space
W
given by the closure of the subspace generated by the right
V_{0}(\mathbf{A})
-translations of

\varphi^{Z,\psi}(g):=\int_{Z({\bf Q})\backslash Z(\mathbf{A})}\varphi(ug)\psi(u)du.

Assume that
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}
is not identically zero. By considering right translates of
\varphi
we can assume that
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(1)
is non-zero. This implies that the integral

\displaystyle I_{1}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}):=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\overline{\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{1}}}(m)\int_{V_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash V_{0}(\mathbf{A})}\varphi^{Z,\psi}(um)\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{2}}(um)dudm

is non-zero for some choice of data
(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
. Note that the integral
I_{1}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
is well defined because as the functions in Equation (
14
) are not genuine for our space
W
. Since
\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{2}}(zg)=\psi(z)\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{2}}(g)

for all
z\in Z_{0}(\mathbf{A})
, we can write
I_{1}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})

as

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}

\displaystyle\int_{({\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A})^{5}}\varphi(x_{b}(v_{1})x_{a+b}(v_{2})x_{a+2b}(r_{1})x_{a+3b}(r_{2})x_{2a+3b}(r_{3})m)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{2}}(x_{b}(v_{1})x_{a+b}(v_{2})x_{a+2b}(r_{1})m)\overline{\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{1}}}(m)dv_{i}dr_{i}dm.

The integral
I_{1}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
is the residue of the global zeta integral which calculates the standard
L
-function for
\varphi
when
\varphi
admits Whittaker coefficients. Namely, by the Siegel-Weil formula
\overline{\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{1}}}(m)
is the residue at
s=3/4
of an Eisenstein series
{\rm Eis}_{\widetilde{{\mathrm{SL}}}_{2}}(m,s)
(depending on
\Phi_{1}
) on the metaplectic cover of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
normalized as in
[
Gin93
, §2]
. By
[
Gin93
, Theorem 4]

I_{1}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
is the residue of

\displaystyle I_{2}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},s):=\int_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\mathbf{A})}

\displaystyle\int_{({\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A})^{5}}\varphi(x_{b}(v_{1})x_{a+b}(v_{2})x_{a+2b}(r_{1})x_{a+3b}(r_{2})x_{2a+3b}(r_{3})m)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\overline{\theta_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}}^{\Phi_{2}}}(x_{b}(v_{1})x_{a+b}(v_{2})x_{a+2b}(r_{1})m){\rm Eis}_{\widetilde{{\mathrm{SL}}}_{2}}(m,s)dv_{i}dr_{i}dm.

We can now prove our claim. Suppose that
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(1)\neq 0
, then
I_{1}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
is not zero for some choice
(\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
. This implies that, for
{\rm Re}(s)
large enough, the integral
I_{2}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},s)
is not zero. By
[
Gin93
, Theorem 1]
,
I_{2}(\varphi,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},s)
unfolds to the Whittaker model and thus contains a Whittaker coefficient of
\varphi
as an inner integration. This shows that if
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}Z,\psi}(1)\neq 0
for some choice of data, the Whittaker coefficient for
\varphi
is non-trivial and thus
\sigma
is globally generic. This finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
. Assume that

(1)

\sigma
is not globally generic;

(2)

there exists a finite place
p
such that
\sigma_{p}
is generic.

Then the big theta lift
\Theta(\sigma)
of
\sigma
to
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
is cuspidal.

Proof.

We show the result by using the tower of theta lifts from
G_{2}
and its properties studied in
[
GRS97b
]
. If
\sigma
lifts trivially to
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
then there is nothing to prove, so suppose that
\sigma
has a non-zero theta lift
\pi
to
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
. Then, by
[
GRS97b
, Theorem A]

\pi
is cuspidal if and only if the lifts of
\sigma
to
\mathrm{PGSp}_{4}
and
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
are both zero. By
[
GRS97b
, Theorem 4.1 (3)]
, the lift to
\mathrm{PGSp}_{4}
is zero if and only if

\varphi^{\mathrm{SL}_{3}}(g)=\int_{[\mathrm{SL}_{3}]}\varphi(xg)dx=0\text{ and }\varphi^{\mathrm{SU}(2,1)}(g)=\int_{[\mathrm{SU}(2,1)]}\varphi(xg)dx=0

for any
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, any
\varphi\in V_{\sigma^{\vee}}
. Here,
\mathrm{SL}_{3}
embeds into
G_{2}
as the stabilizer of a norm
-1
vector (cf. §
6.2.2
), while
\mathrm{SU}(2,1)
is realized as the stabilizer of a norm
-c
vector, with
c
not a square in
{\bf Q}
. We argue by contradiction. Suppose that
\sigma^{\vee}
has a non-trivial
\mathrm{SU}(2,1)
-functional. This implies that, at every finite
v
,
\sigma_{v}
admits one. By Frobenius reciprocity,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SU}(2,1)}(\sigma^{\vee}_{v},{\bf C})=\mathrm{Hom}_{G_{2}}({\text{c-Ind}}_{\mathrm{SU}(2,1)}^{G_{2}}({\bf C}),\sigma_{v})

and hence, since
\sigma_{v}
is irreducible, one deduces that each local component
\sigma_{v}
of
\sigma
is a quotient of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{2}({\bf Q}_{v})/\mathrm{SU}(2,1)({\bf Q}_{v}))
. In particular,
\sigma_{p}
is identified with such a quotient. This is a contradiction as, by hypothesis,
\sigma_{p}
is generic but, by
[
GS98
, Lemma 4.10]
,
C^{\infty}_{c}(G_{2}({{\bf Q}_{p}})/\mathrm{SU}(2,1)({{\bf Q}_{p}}))
does not admit a Whittaker functional. The same argument also shows the vanishing of
\varphi^{\mathrm{SL}_{3}}
. We claim finally that the theta lift of
\sigma
to
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
also vanishes.
Since
\sigma
is not globally generic, Lemma
7.1
shows that, for all
\varphi\in\sigma
,
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g)=0
. We can then apply
[
GRS97b
, Theorem 4.1(4)]
to deduce that the theta lift of
\sigma
to
\mathrm{PGL}_{3}
is zero and conclude the proof.

∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
. Assume that

(1)

\sigma_{\infty}
is a discrete series;

(2)

there exists a finite place
p
such that
\sigma_{p}
is Steinberg.

Then
\Theta(\sigma)
is cuspidal.

Proof.

We distinguish two cases. We first suppose that
\sigma
is globally generic. Then we apply
[
HKT23
, Theorem 1.7(ii)]
to deduce that its theta lift is cuspidal. If, instead,
\sigma
is not globally generic, the result follows from Theorem
7.2
as the Steinberg representation
\sigma_{p}={\rm St}_{G_{2}}
is generic.
∎

7.2.
Calculation of orbits

This preparatory section presents an elementary but crucial calculation needed in the proof of Proposition
7.7
.

Let
e:{\bf Q}\backslash\mathbf{A}\rightarrow{\bf C}^{\times}
be the standard non-trivial character introduced in §
5.2
and let
A\in J({\bf Q})
. We define the character
\psi_{A}:N({\bf Q})\backslash N(\mathbf{A})\rightarrow{\bf C}^{\times}
by
\psi_{A}(X)=e(\mathrm{Tr}(A\circ X))
where
A\circ X=\frac{1}{2}(AX+XA)
is the Jordan product. Recall from §
5.2
that, for any
B\in J_{3}({\bf Q})
, we define a character
\psi_{B}:U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})\rightarrow{\bf C}^{\times}
by
\psi_{B}(n(X))=e(\mathrm{Tr}(BX))
. In particular, we have denoted
\psi_{D}
the character associated to

\alpha_{D}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&\\
&-D&\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\in J_{3}({\bf Q}).

Define

\omega({\bf Q}):=\{A\in\Omega({\bf Q})\,|\,\psi_{A}|_{U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}=\psi_{D}\},

i.e. the set of rank 1 matrices in
J({\bf Q})
inducing the same character as
\alpha_{D}
on the unipotent radical of the Siegel parabolic. In the following, we will always see
\omega({\bf Q})
inside
\overline{N}({\bf Q})
. In particular, if
g\in\mathrm{GL}_{3}({\bf Q})\subseteq M({\bf Q})
, its action on
A
is the dual action to (
11
), namely
g\cdot A=\mathrm{det}(g)(g^{t})^{-1}Ag^{-1}
. Finally, denote by
A(x,y,z)
the matrix
{\begin{pmatrix}0&\overline{z}&y\\
z&-D&\overline{x}\\
\overline{y}&x&1\end{pmatrix}}\in J
.

Lemma 7.4
.

We have

\omega({\bf Q})=\left\{A(x,y,z)\,:\,\mathrm{Tr}(x)=\mathrm{Tr}(z)=0,\mathrm{N}(x)=-D,N(z)=0,z\in x^{\perp},y=-D^{-1}zx\right\}.

Proof.

Let

A=\begin{pmatrix}d&\overline{z}&y\\
z&e&\overline{x}\\
\overline{y}&x&f\end{pmatrix}\in J

Similarly to the proof of
[
GS98
, Lemma 3.4]
, the condition
\psi_{A}|_{U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}=\psi_{D}
is equivalent to

d=0,\;\;e=-D,\;\;f=1,

\overline{x}=-x,\;\;\overline{y}=-y,\;\;\overline{z}=-z

This together with the condition that
A
has rank
1
(Equation (
10
)) give

\mathrm{N}(x)=-D,\;\;\mathrm{N}(y)=\mathrm{N}(z)=0,

yz=0,\;\;zx=-Dy,\;\;xy=z.

We claim that these conditions imply that
z\in x^{\perp}
, which means that
z\overline{x}+x\overline{z}=0
, or equivalently
zx=-xz
. Indeed, multiplying
z=xy
on the left by
x
and using alternativity, we obtain

xz=x(xy)=(xx)y=Dy=-zx.

Finally, as
\mathrm{N}(x)=-D
and
\mathrm{Tr}(x)=0
, we have
x^{2}=D
and hence
x^{-1}=D^{-1}x
, which implies that

y=x^{-1}z=D^{-1}xz.

This shows one inclusion of the statement.

In the other direction let
x,z\in\mathbb{O}
be as in the right hand side of the statement. We have to show that
y:=-D^{-1}zx
has norm and trace equal to zero and that
xy=z
. We have

\mathrm{N}(y)=(-D)^{-2}\mathrm{N}(z)\mathrm{N}(x)=0

and

\mathrm{Tr}(y)=-D^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}(zx)=D^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}(z\overline{x})=0

as
z\in x^{\perp}
.
Hence
\mathrm{Tr}(y)=0
. Moreover

xy=-D^{-1}x(zx)=D^{-1}x(xz)=D^{-1}(xx)z=z.

This shows that
A\in\omega({\bf Q})
and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

As for any
A(x,y,z)\in\omega({\bf Q})
, the octonion
y=-D^{-1}zx
is determined by
x
and
z
and we will often denote
A(x,y,z)
by
A(x,z)
. Note that there is an action of
G_{2}({\bf Q})
on the set
\omega({\bf Q})
given by the action on the coefficients. The following proposition describing the orbits of this action will be essential.

Proposition 7.5
.

The group
G_{2}({\bf Q})
acts on
\omega({\bf Q})
with a finite number of orbits. Moreover, representatives of the orbits and their respective stabilizers are given as follows.

(1)

If
D
is a square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
:

(a)

A_{3}=A(x,0)
, where
x=(s_{4}-t_{4})\sqrt{D}
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}({\bf Q})}(A(x,0))\cong{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
, where
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
is embedded into
G_{2}
as §
6.2.2
.

(b)

A_{2}=A(x,t_{3})
with
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(A_{2})={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V\subset{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
, where
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
and
V
embed into
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
as in §
7.1
.

(c)

A_{1}=A(x,s_{3})
with
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(A_{1})={\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\overline{V}\subset{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
, where
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
is as in (1)(b) and
\overline{V}
is the opposite unipotent subgroup to
V
.

(d)

A_{0}=A(x,s_{1}+t_{3})
with
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(A_{0})=U_{D},
where
U_{D}
denotes the unipotent radical of the upper-triangular Borel of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
(denoted by
U_{{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}}
in §
7.1
).

(2)

If
D
is not square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
:

(a)

A_{1}=A(x,0)\in\omega({\bf Q})
, for any
x\neq 0
for which
N(x)=-D
, with

\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}({\bf Q})}(A(x,0))\cong\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D},

where
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}=\mathrm{SU}(x^{\perp})
is the unitary group for the restriction of the norm form to the
3
-dimensional
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
-subspace of
\mathbb{O}^{0}
orthogonal to
x

1
1

1

The unitary group
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
is a form of
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
which splits over
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
and which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{SU}(2,1)
, resp.
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
, if
D<0
, resp.
D>0
, over
{\bf R}
.

(b)

A_{0}=A(x,z)
, for any norm zero
z
in
x^{\perp}
, with
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(A_{0})\simeq U_{D}
, where
U_{D}
denotes the unipotent radical of the upper-triangular Borel of
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
.

Proof.

Step 1.
By
[
RS89
, Theorem 1]
, the group
G_{2}
acts transitively on the set of trace zero elements of norm
-D
and hence on the sets
A(x,0)
.
The description of the stabilizer in (1)(a) follows from
[
Jac58
, Theorem 4]
or
[
RS89
, Lemma 2]
. The description of the stabilizer in (2)(a) follows from
[
Jac58
, Theorem 3]
or
[
RS89
, Lemma 3]
. More precisely, according to
[
RS89
, Lemma 3]
the subspace
x^{\perp}
of
\mathbb{O}^{0}
of elements which are orthogonal to
x
has the structure of a
3
-dimensional
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
-vector space and the action of
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(x)
on
x^{\perp}
induces an isomorphism
\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(x)\simeq\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
.

Step 2.
We now study the remaining
G_{2}
-orbits when
D
is a square in
{\bf Q}
. Again, we can assume that
D=1
. Recall from §
6.2.2
that
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
embeds into
G_{2}
as the stabilizer of
s_{4}-t_{4}
. This identification is explicitly given as follows (cf.
[
RS89
, Lemma 2]
). An element of
g\in{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
induces an action on
\mathbb{O}^{0}
fixing
s_{4}-t_{4}
and given by the left multiplication by
g
on
\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle
and by
(g^{t})^{-1}
on
\langle t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\rangle
. One verifies that this actions respects multiplication and hence defines an element in
G_{2}
.
Assume
z\neq 0
is such that
A(x,z)\in\omega({\bf Q})
. Since
z
is trace zero and orthogonal to
x=s_{4}-t_{4}
we can write
z=z_{1}+z_{2}
with
z_{1}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}s_{i}
and
z_{2}=\sum_{i}\beta_{i}t_{i}
. Since the group
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
acts transitively on the non-zero elements of
\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle
and
\langle t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\rangle
, then the cases where
z_{1}=0
or
z_{2}=0
give rise to exactly two orbits. When
z_{1}=0
, taking
z_{2}=t_{3}
as a generator of this orbit, the corresponding stabilizer is

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}*&*&*\\
*&*&*\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{SL}}_{3},

which coincides with
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V
as in (1)(b). Similarly, when
z_{2}=0
, taking
z_{1}=s_{3}
as the generator of the orbit, then the stabilizer is

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}*&*&0\\
*&*&0\\
*&*&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}.

This is nothing but
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\overline{V}
, with
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
which again embeds in the Levi of the long root parabolic
P_{a}
and
\overline{V}
is the opposite unipotent subgroup to
V
generated by the negative roots
-a-3b
,
-2a-3b
. Finally we treat the case
z_{1},z_{2}\neq 0
. Write

z=\alpha_{1}s_{1}+\alpha_{2}s_{2}+\alpha_{3}s_{3}+\beta_{1}t_{1}+\beta_{2}t_{2}+\beta_{3}t_{3}.

The condition
N(z)=0
translates then in

(15)

\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}+\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}+\alpha_{3}\beta_{3}=0.

We can assume that
z_{2}=t_{3}
. Then
\alpha_{3}=0
by (
15
) and, using the action of the stabilizer of
t_{3}
, we can assume that
z_{1}=s_{1}
. It is then immediate to check that the stabilizer of
A(s_{4}-t_{4},s_{1}+t_{3})
is as in (1)(d). This concludes the proof of (1).

Step 3.
We finally deal with the case where
D
is not a square in
{\bf Q}
. By Witt’s theorem, the group
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
acts transitively on the isotropic vectors of the three dimensional
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
vector space
x^{\perp}
. We thus have two orbits for
G_{2}({\bf Q})
on
\omega({\bf Q})
, generated by
A(x,0)
and
A(x,z)
, where
z
is any non-zero vector in
x^{\perp}
with zero norm. We are now left with calculating the stabilizer of the latter orbit.
The action of
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
on
x^{\perp}
is given by its natural action on
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})^{3}
. More precisely, after extending scalars to
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
, we can decompose

x^{\perp}\otimes_{\bf Q}{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})={\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle\oplus{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})\langle t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\rangle.

The projection to the first component induces an isomorphism of
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
-vector spaces
x^{\perp}\simeq{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle
(cf.
[
RS89
, Lemma 3]
), with
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
acting naturally on the basis
\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}
. Here, we choose the Hermitian form (with respect to the extension
{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})/{\bf Q}
) defining
\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
given by

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&&\sqrt{D}^{-1}\\
&1&\\
-\sqrt{D}^{-1}&&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}_{3}({\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})).

We can then suppose that
z
is sent to
s_{1}
and the corresponding stabilizer is given by

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&*&*\\
0&*&*\\
0&*&*\end{pmatrix}\right\}\cap\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}=U_{D}.

∎

7.3.
Non-vanishing of Fourier coefficients I

Recall that we have denoted by
\Pi=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\Pi_{v}
the minimal representation of the group
E_{7}
. Moreover, in §
6.3
, for
f\in\Pi
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A}))
, we have defined the function
\Theta(f,\varphi)
on
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
by

(16)

\Theta(f,\varphi)(g)=\int_{G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)(g^{\prime}g)\varphi(g^{\prime})dg^{\prime}.

For any
A\in J({\bf Q})
and
f\in\Pi
, consider the Fourier coefficient

\theta(f)_{A}(g)=\int_{N({\bf Q})\backslash N(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)(ng)\psi_{A}^{-1}(n)dn.

We then have the Fourier expansion (cf.
[
HKT23
, §A.3]
)

(17)

\theta(f)(g)=\theta(f)_{0}(g)+\sum_{A\in\Omega({\bf Q})}\theta(f)_{A}(g),

where
\Omega({\bf Q})\subset J({\bf Q})
is the subset of rank
1
elements.

The following Lemma will be used in the proof of Proposition
7.7
. Its proof is similar to the one of
[
GS98
, Lemma 4.6]
but we give details for the convenience of the reader. Let
A_{0}
be the representative of the open
G_{2}
-orbit on
\omega({\bf Q})
given in Proposition
7.5
. Note that there is no harm in conjugating
A_{0}\in J({\bf Q})
by an element of the Levi
\mathrm{GL}_{3}({\bf Q})
of the Siegel parabolic of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
. Thus, conjugating by
\mathrm{diag}(n,n,n)
,
A_{0}
gets multiplied by
n^{2}
and so we can assume that the entries
x,y,z
of
A_{0}
are in
\mathbb{O}({\bf Z})
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
S
denote a finite number of places containing 2 and
\infty
, and let
f=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}f_{v}\in\Pi
be such that, for
v\notin S
, we have
f_{v}=f_{v}^{0}
where
f_{v}^{0}
denotes the spherical vector normalized such that
f_{v}^{0}(A_{0})=1
. Let
{\bf Q}_{S}=\prod_{v\in S}{\bf Q}_{v}
. If
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, we write
g=g_{S}g^{S}
where
g_{S}\in G_{2}({\bf Q}_{S})
and
g^{S}\in\prod_{v\notin S}G_{2}({\bf Q}_{v})
. Then there exists a non-zero constant
c_{A_{0}}
such that for every
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
we have

\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(g)=c_{A_{0}}f_{S}(g_{S}^{-1}A_{0})\prod_{v\notin S}\chi_{v}(g_{v})

where
f_{S}=\bigotimes_{v\in S}f_{v}
and
\chi_{v}
is the characteristic function of
U_{D}({\bf Z}_{v})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Z}_{v})
.

Proof.

By uniqueness of local functionals (
[
HKT23
, Theorem A.4]
), there exists a non-zero scalar
c_{A_{0}}
such that for any
g\in E_{7}(\mathbf{A})
, we have
\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(g)=c_{A_{0}}(\Pi(g)f)(A_{0})
. For
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
we have
(\Pi(g)f)(A_{0})=f(g^{-1}A_{0})
where
g^{-1}A_{0}
is the result of the natural action of
g^{-1}
on the off diagonal entries of
A_{0}
. Hence
\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(g)=c_{A_{0}}f(g^{-1}A_{0})=c_{A_{0}}\prod_{v}f_{v}(g_{v}^{-1}A_{0})
for
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
. Let us prove that for any
p\notin S
, we have
f_{p}(g_{p}^{-1}A_{0})=\chi_{p}(g_{p})
. So let
g_{p}\in G_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
be such that
f^{0}_{p}(g_{p}^{-1}A_{0})\neq 0
and let
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}
denote the off diagonal entries of
g_{p}^{-1}A_{0}
. According to
[
HKT23
, Theorem A.5]
the spherical vector
f_{p}^{0}
is supported in
J({\bf Z}_{p})
. Hence
x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{O}({\bf Z}_{p})
. Consider
\mathbb{O}({\bf F}_{p})
the split octonion algebra over
{\bf F}_{p}
. The projections of
(x,y,z)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
to
\mathbb{O}({\bf F}_{p})
are
G_{2}({\bf F}_{p})
-conjugated by the proof of Step 1 in Proposition
7.5
, which is still valid over the base field
{\bf F}_{p}
as long as
p\neq 2
. It follows from Hensel lemma that
(x,y,z)
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
are
G_{2}({\bf Z}_{p})
-conjugated. Therefore the function
g_{p}\mapsto f_{p}^{0}(g_{p}^{-1}A_{0})
is supported in
U_{D}({\bf Z}_{p})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Z}_{p})\subset U_{D}({\bf Q}_{p})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Q}_{p})
. Since
f_{p}^{0}
is
G_{2}({\bf Z}_{p})
-invariant, for
g_{p}\in G_{2}({\bf Z}_{p})
we have
f_{p}^{0}(g_{p}^{-1}A_{0})=f_{p}^{0}(A_{0})=1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
as in Theorem
7.2
and let
\varphi\in\sigma^{\vee}
be a cuspidal form. Then, the following conditions are equivalent

(1)

\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(1)\neq 0
for some choice of
f
.

(2)

\varphi^{U_{D}}(g)\neq 0
for some
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
.

In particular, if any of the conditions holds then
\Theta(\sigma)
is non-zero.

Proof.

Recall first that, according to Proposition
5.7
, we have
\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}\neq 0
if and only if
\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{3},\alpha}\neq 0
for some
\alpha\in{\rm Sym}^{{\rm rk}2}(3)({\bf Q})
with
\alpha\sim_{L({\bf Q})}\alpha_{D}
. We write

\displaystyle\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}\Theta(f,\varphi)(u)\psi_{D}^{-1}(u)du

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}\sum_{A\in\Omega({\bf Q})}\theta(f)_{A}(ug)\varphi(g)\psi_{D}^{-1}(u)dudg,

where in the second equality we used the definition (
16
) of
\Theta(f,\varphi)
and the Fourier expansion (
17
) of
\theta(f)
. Since
U_{3}\subseteq N
, we have that
\theta(f)_{A}(ug)=\psi_{A}(u)\theta(f)_{A}(g)
and

\int_{U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}\psi_{A}(u)\psi_{D}^{-1}(u)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{vol}(U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A}))&\text{ if }\psi_{D}=\psi_{A}|_{U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}\\
0&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Hence we have

(18)

\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(1)=\mathrm{vol}(U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A}))\int_{G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\sum_{A\in\omega({\bf Q})}\theta(f)_{A}(g)\varphi(g)dg.

Let
(A_{i})_{i}
be the finite representatives of the orbits of the action of
G_{2}({\bf Q})
on
\omega({\bf Q})
as given by Proposition
7.5
, and write
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}
for the stabilizers of
A_{i}
in
G_{2}
. The integral on the right hand side of (
18
) becomes

\sum_{i}\int_{G_{2}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\sum_{g^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}({\bf Q})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Q})}\theta(f)_{A_{i}}(g^{\prime}g)\varphi(g)dg=\sum_{i}\int_{\mathrm{Stab_{A_{i}}({\bf Q})}\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{i}}(g)\varphi(g)dg.

Observe now that, by
[
HKT23
, Theorem A.4]
we have
\theta(f)_{A_{i}}(g)=c_{A_{i}}f(g^{-1}A_{i})
for any
g\in G_{2}
. Hence, since
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}(\mathbf{A})
fixes the matrix
A_{i}
, we deduce that the function
g\mapsto\theta(f)_{A_{i}}(g)
is left
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}(\mathbf{A})
-invariant. Making an inner integration over
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}(\mathbf{A})
in each term of the outer sum, we deduce that the above equals

\sum_{i}\int_{\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{i}}(g)\varphi^{\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}}(g)dg,

where
\varphi^{\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}}(g)
denotes the period of
\varphi
over
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{i}}(\mathbf{A})
. We now analyse two different possibilities. If
D
is not a square in
{\bf Q}
, then, by
(2)
of Proposition
7.5
,
G_{2}({\bf Q})
acts on
\omega({\bf Q})
with two orbits, one closed and one open. Let
A_{0},A_{1}
denote representatives of these two orbits with stabilizers
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{0}}=U_{D}
and
\mathrm{Stab}_{A_{1}}=\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}
in
G_{2}
. By the proof of Theorem
7.2
,
\varphi^{\mathrm{SU}_{3}^{D}}(g)=0
, and hence the only surviving term is the one corresponding to the orbit represented by
A_{0}
. If
D
is a square in
{\bf Q}
, then by
(1)
of Proposition
7.5
,
G_{2}({\bf Q})
acts on the set
\omega({\bf Q})
with four orbits, three closed and one open. Let
A_{i},0\leq i\leq 3
denote representatives of those orbits, with
A_{0}
representing the open one. The corresponding stabilizers are are
U_{D}
,
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
,
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V
and its conjugate
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\overline{V}
. By the proof of Theorem
7.2
, we have
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}}(g)=0
. By hypothesis
\sigma
(and
\sigma^{\vee}
) is not globally generic, hence Lemma
7.1
implies that
\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}V}(g)=\varphi^{{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}\overline{V}}(g)=0
. From this, we deduce that, for any
D
,

(19)

\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(1)=\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(g)\varphi^{U_{D}}(g)dg,

where
\varphi^{U_{D}}(g)
is the constant term of
\varphi
along
U_{D}
. This shows that if
\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}(1)\neq 0
then
\varphi^{U_{D}}\neq 0
since the period appears as an inner integral of the Fourier coefficient.

We now show the converse, i.e. that if
\varphi^{U_{D}}\neq 0
then, for some choice of
f\in\Pi
, the Fourier coefficient
\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{3},\psi_{D}}
does not vanish. Let
S
be as in Lemma
7.6
. By enlarging
S
if necessary, we can assume that the cusp form
\varphi
is
G_{2}({\bf Z}_{v})
-invariant for all
v\notin S
. By Lemma
7.6
, the integral of (
19
) equals

c_{A_{0}}\cdot\left(\int_{U_{D}({\bf Q}_{S})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Q}_{S})}f_{S}(g^{-1}A_{0})\varphi^{U_{D}}(g)dg\right)\cdot\prod_{v\notin S}vol(U_{D}({\bf Z}_{v})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Z}_{v}),dg_{v}).

It remains to show that, when
\varphi^{U_{D}}\neq 0
then for a good choice of
f
at the places in
S
, the integral

\int_{U_{D}({\bf Q}_{S})\backslash G_{2}({\bf Q}_{S})}f_{S}(g^{-1}A_{0})\varphi^{U_{D}}(g)dg\neq 0.

It follows from
[
HKT23
, Theorem A.4]
that
f_{S}
can be any smooth compactly supported function on
\Omega({\bf Q}_{S})
. Let
g_{0}\in G_{2}({\bf Q}_{S})
be such that
\varphi^{U_{D}}(g_{0})\neq 0
. We can take a non-negative
f
supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
g_{0}
to ensure the non-vanishing of the integral. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

7.4.
Non-vanishing of Fourier coefficients II

The purpose of this section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
F
denote a quadratic étale algebra and
\sigma=\sigma_{\infty}\otimes\sigma_{f}
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
such that

—

\sigma_{\infty}
is a non-generic discrete series with infinitesimal character
r\varepsilon_{1}+s\varepsilon_{2}
.

—

there exists a finite prime
p
such that
\sigma_{p}
is Steinberg.

—

The representation
\sigma
supports Fourier coefficient associated to the cubic algebra
{\bf Q}\times F
.

The theta lift
\Theta(\sigma)=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\Theta(\sigma_{v})
is a non-zero cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
. Moveover, if
\pi
denotes any non-zero irreducible subquotient of
\Theta(\sigma)
, then

—

\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series of infinitesimal character
(r,\tfrac{1}{2}(r+s),\tfrac{1}{2}(r-s))
.

—

\pi_{p}
is Steinberg.

—

The representation
\pi
supports a non-trivial Fourier coefficient of type
(4\,2)
associated to
F
.

Remark 7.9
.

As it will follow from the proof, the condition of
\sigma_{\infty}
being not generic can be replaced by
\sigma
not being locally generic, i.e. that there exists one local component of
\sigma_{v}
of
\sigma
which is not generic.

Let us first fix some notations first. Recall from §
6.1.2
that the centralizer of
G_{2}
in
M
is
\mathrm{GL}_{3}
and let

U_{0}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&a&b\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}

be the unipotent radical of its Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices. Note that the unipotent subgroup
U_{0}U_{3}
is the unipotent radical of the parabolic subgroup
P
of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
of Levi
\mathrm{GL}_{2}\times\mathrm{GL}_{1}^{2}
appearing in §
5.2.2
.

Definition 7.10
.

Define the character
\psi_{0}:U_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{0}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
by sending

\psi_{0}(u)=e(a).

Note that
\psi_{0}\psi_{D}
is the character (simply denoted by
\psi_{D}
) on
U_{P}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{P}(\mathbf{A})
introduced in §
5.2.2
.

As explained in §
7.2
, we view the space
\Omega
of rank 1 elements in
J
inside
\overline{N}
so that
U_{0}
acts on
\Omega
via the natural right action of
\mathrm{GL}_{3}\subseteq M
on
\overline{N}
. Then, we let
U_{0}
act on the left on
\omega
and hence on the triples
(x,y,z)
of off-diagonal terms by the rule

(20)

u^{-1}\cdot(x,y,z)=(x+ay+bz,y,z).

7.4.1.
The relation between
U_{P}
and
U_{H}

In what follows, we relate the unipotent subgroup
U_{0}
to the unipotent radical
U_{H}
of the Heisenberg parabolic. Such a relation will be employed in Proposition
7.13
to establish a relation between Fourier coefficients for the Heisenberg parabolic of
G_{2}
-cusp forms and Fourier coefficients of type
(4\,2)
of their theta lifts.

Before stating our result, we make the following comments on the choice of representatives of the open orbits in Proposition
7.5
. First, suppose that
D=d^{2}
, with
d\in{\bf Q}^{\times}
. There is no harm in assuming
d\in{\bf Z}
. Recall that the stabilizer in
G_{2}
of the vector
s_{4}-t_{4}
can be identified with
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}={\mathrm{SL}}(\langle s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\rangle)
. Since the Heisenberg parabolic
H=L_{H}\cdot U_{H}
is the stabilizer of the flag
\langle s_{1},t_{3}\rangle
, its unipotent radical
U_{H}
contains
U_{D}=\mathrm{Stab}_{G_{2}}(A_{0})
, where

A_{0}=A(d(s_{4}-t_{4}),s_{1}-t_{3},d(s_{1}+t_{3}))\in J({\bf Z})

is the representative of the open orbit of the action of
G_{2}
on
\omega({\bf Q})
as in Proposition
7.5
. Moreover,
U_{H}/U_{D}
is 2-dimensional and supported on the roots
a+b
and
a+2b
. Let us now suppose that
D
is not a square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
.
The vector
x=s_{2}+Dt_{2}
is a trace zero octonion of norm
-D
and orthogonal to
t_{3}
. We choose the representative of the open orbit to be

A_{0}=A(s_{2}+Dt_{2},s_{1},t_{3})\in J({\bf Z}).

Lemma 7.11
.

There is a natural surjection
p:U_{H}\to U_{0}
inducing an isomorphism

U_{H}/U_{D}\to U_{0}.

Proof.

By the description of the action in (
20
) and the linear independence of the coordinates
(x,y,z)
of the representative of the open orbit, one sees that
U_{0}
acts freely on it. Hence, the result follows from showing that any element in
U_{H}
acts on the triple
(x,y,z)
as an element of
U_{0}
and vice versa.

Case 1.
We start with the case where
D
is a square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
.
The action of
U_{0}
is given by

(21)

u^{-1}\cdot(d(s_{4}-t_{4}),s_{1}-t_{3},d(s_{1}+t_{3}))=(d(s_{4}-t_{4})+(a+db)s_{1}+(db-a)t_{3},s_{1}-t_{3},d(s_{1}+t_{3})).

Since any element of
U_{H}
fixes
s_{1}
and
t_{3}
, it suffices to show that
U_{H}
acts on
(s_{4}-t_{4})
as an element of
U_{0}
. We verify this by studying the action of the Lie algebra. By (
13
), we know that the Lie algebra of
U_{H}
is generated by the Lie algebra of the unipotent upper-triangular subgroup
U_{D}
in
{\mathrm{SL}}_{3}
and by the vectors
v_{1}
and
\delta_{3}
. Using the explicit action of the action of the Lie algebra given in §
6.2.3
, one checks that

E_{ij}\cdot(s_{4}-t_{4})=0,

v_{1}\cdot(s_{4}-t_{4})=s_{1},

\delta_{3}\cdot(s_{4}-t_{4})=t_{3}.

The above equations show that, for
u_{1}=x_{a+b}(\lambda_{1})
and
u_{2}=x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{2})
for some scalars
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, we have

u_{1}\cdot(d(s_{4}-t_{4}))=d(s_{4}-t_{4}+\lambda_{1}s_{1}),

u_{2}\cdot(d(s_{4}-t_{4}))=d(s_{4}-t_{4}+\lambda_{2}t_{3}).

This gives the desired isomorphism: if
u\in U_{H}/U_{D}
is identified with the product of
x_{a+b}(\lambda_{1})x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{2})
, then, from Equation (
21
), we see that it gets sent to the element

\begin{pmatrix}1&d\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}&\tfrac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{2}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\in U_{0}.

Case 2.
We now suppose that
D
is not a square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
. Similarly to Case 1, it suffices to calculate
u\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})
for any
u\in U_{H}
. As above, one checks that

\displaystyle E_{12}\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})

\displaystyle=s_{1},

\displaystyle E_{23}\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})

\displaystyle=Dt_{3},

\displaystyle E_{13}\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle v_{1}\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})

\displaystyle=t_{3},

\displaystyle\delta_{3}\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})

\displaystyle=-Ds_{1}.

This implies that if
u\in V_{H}=U_{H}/[U_{H},U_{H}]
equals to
x_{a}(\lambda_{1})x_{a+b}(\lambda_{2})x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{3})x_{a+3b}(\lambda_{4})
, then

u\cdot(s_{2}+Dt_{2})=s_{2}+Dt_{2}+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}D)s_{1}+(\lambda_{2}+D\lambda_{4})t_{3}.

In particular,
U_{D}
embeds into
U_{H}
as the subgroup of matrices with
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{3}D
and
\lambda_{2}=-\lambda_{4}D
, and the map
p:U_{H}/U_{D}\to U_{0}
sends
u
to the element

\begin{pmatrix}1&\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}D&\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{4}D\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\in U_{0}.

∎

Corollary 7.12
.

Under the isomorphism
p:U_{H}/U_{D}\to U_{0}
, we have

\psi_{H,D}=\psi_{0}\circ p,

where
\psi_{H,D}:U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
is the character corresponding to the étale cubic algebra
{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
.

Proof.

We start with the case where
D
is a square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
. For simplicity, we can (and do) assume that
D=1
. From Lemma
7.11
, if
n\in U_{H}/U_{D}
is identified with the product of
x_{a+b}(\lambda_{1})x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{2})
, it is sent via
p
to

\begin{pmatrix}1&\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}&\tfrac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{2}\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\in U_{0}.

Hence, the character
\psi_{0}\circ p:U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
sends
n\mapsto e(\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2})
. We now show that this corresponds to the character
\psi_{H,D}
associated to
{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}
as in §
6.2.4
. Recall that each character on
U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})
is of the form
n\mapsto e(\langle w,\overline{n}\rangle)
, where
\overline{n}
denotes the projection of
n
to
U_{H}/[U_{H},U_{H}]
and
w\in U_{H}({\bf Q})/[U_{H}({\bf Q}),U_{H}({\bf Q})]
corresponds to a binary cubic form

f_{w}(x,y)=\lambda_{1}x^{3}+\lambda_{2}x^{2}y+\lambda_{3}xy^{2}+\lambda_{4}y^{3},

with
\lambda_{i}\in{\bf Q}
. Furthermore, as
\overline{n}=x_{a}(\lambda_{1}^{\prime})x_{a+b}(\lambda_{2}^{\prime}/3)x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{3}^{\prime}/3)x_{a+3b}(\lambda_{4}^{\prime})
corresponds to

f^{\prime}(x,y)=\lambda_{1}^{\prime}x^{3}+\lambda_{2}^{\prime}x^{2}y+\lambda_{3}^{\prime}xy^{2}+\lambda_{4}^{\prime}y^{3},
the pairing is

\langle w,\overline{n}\rangle=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{4}^{\prime}-\tfrac{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}^{\prime}}{3}+\tfrac{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}{3}-\lambda_{4}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}.

Then, the character
\psi_{0}\circ p
corresponds to an element
w_{D}
for which
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}=0
and
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}=1/2
, namely the binary cubic polynomial
f_{D}(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}(x^{2}y+xy^{2})
. The latter is in the
L_{H}({\bf Q})
-orbit corresponding to the cubic algebra
{\bf Q}^{3}
. Indeed, if we let
g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&\\
&-2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in L_{H}({\bf Q})
act on
f_{D}
, we get

g\cdot f_{D}(x,y)=-\tfrac{1}{4}f_{D}(2x,-2y)=\tfrac{1}{8}(8x^{2}y-8xy^{2})=x^{2}y-xy^{2},

which corresponds to
{\bf Q}^{3}
by Example
6.1
(1).

We now suppose that
D
is not a square in
{\bf Q}^{\times}
. Then, by Lemma
7.11
, if

n\equiv x_{a}(\lambda_{1})x_{a+b}(\lambda_{2})x_{a+2b}(\lambda_{3})x_{a+3b}(\lambda_{4})\,\text{ mod }[U_{H},U_{H}],

the character
\psi_{0}\circ p:U_{H}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\to{\bf C}^{\times}
sends
n\mapsto e(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}D)
. This character is associated to the binary cubic polynomial
f_{D}(x,y)=Dx^{2}y-y^{3}
, which corresponds to
{\bf Q}\times{\bf Q}(\sqrt{D})
by Example
6.1
(2).
∎

7.4.2.
Comparison of Fourier coefficients

The following proposition can be paired with Proposition
7.7
to give three equivalent ways of proving that the theta lift of an automorphic representation of
G_{2}
does not vanish.

Proposition 7.13
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
as in Theorem
7.2
and let
\varphi\in\sigma^{\vee}
be a cuspidal form. The following conditions are equivalent

(1)

\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(1)\neq 0
for some choice of
f\in\Pi
,

(2)

\varphi_{U_{H},\psi_{H,D}}(g)\neq 0
for some
g\in G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
.

In particular, if any of the conditions holds then
\Theta(\sigma)
is non-zero.

Proof.

Decomposing
U_{P}=U_{0}U_{3}
, we have

\Theta(f,\varphi)_{U_{P},\psi_{D}}(1)=\int_{U_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{0}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{3}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{3}(\mathbf{A})}\Theta(f,\varphi)(uu^{\prime})\psi_{D}^{-1}(u^{\prime})\psi_{U_{0}}^{-1}(u)du^{\prime}du.

As in the proof of Proposition
7.7
, this equals

\int_{U_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{0}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(ug)\varphi^{U_{D}}(g)\psi_{U_{0}}^{-1}(u)dgdu.

Exchanging integrals and making an inner integration over
U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})
, we get

\int_{U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\left(\int_{U_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{0}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(uu^{\prime}g)\psi_{U_{0}}^{-1}(u)du\right)\varphi^{U_{D}}(u^{\prime}g)du^{\prime}dg.

The isomorphism
p:U_{H}/U_{D}\cong U_{0}
of Lemma
7.11
induces

U_{0}({\bf Q})\backslash U_{0}(\mathbf{A})\cong U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})

such that
\psi_{H,D}=\psi_{0}\circ p
(cf. Corollary
7.12
).
Thus, we can write the integral as

\int_{U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\left(\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(uu^{\prime}g)\psi_{H,D}^{-1}(u)du\right)\varphi^{U_{D}}(u^{\prime}g)du^{\prime}dg.

Exchanging integrals, we have

\int_{U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\left(\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(uu^{\prime}g)\varphi^{U_{D}}(u^{\prime}g)du^{\prime}\right)\psi_{H,D}^{-1}(u)dudg

=\int_{U_{H}\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\left(\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\sum_{\gamma\in U_{D}\backslash U_{H}({\bf Q})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(u\gamma u^{\prime}g)\varphi^{U_{D}}(\gamma u^{\prime}g)du^{\prime}\right)\psi_{H,D}^{-1}(u)dudg

=\int_{U_{H}\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\sum_{\gamma}\left(\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(\gamma uu^{\prime}g)\varphi^{U_{D}}(\gamma u^{\prime}g)du^{\prime}\right)\psi_{H,D}^{-1}(u)dudg

Changing variable
u^{\prime}\mapsto u^{\prime\prime}=\gamma uu^{\prime}=u\gamma u^{\prime\prime}
in the inner integral, the above becomes

\int_{U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{H}({\bf Q})U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\left(\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(u^{\prime\prime}g)\varphi^{U_{D}}(u^{-1}u^{\prime\prime}g)du^{\prime\prime}\right)\psi_{H,D}^{-1}(u)dudg

which, after rearranging the integrals, equals to

\int_{U_{H}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash U_{H}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(u^{\prime\prime}g)\varphi_{U_{H},\psi_{H,D}}(u^{\prime\prime}g)du^{\prime\prime}dg

=\int_{U_{D}(\mathbf{A})\backslash G_{2}(\mathbf{A})}\theta(f)_{A_{0}}(g)\varphi_{U_{H},\psi_{H,D}}(g)dg.

This shows that
(1)
implies
(2)
. The proof of the converse is identical as the one given in Proposition
7.7
.

∎

We are now ready to give a proof of Theorem
7.8
.

Proof of Theorem
7.8
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation satisfying the hypotheses of the Theorem. We first apply Corollary
7.3
to deduce that
\Theta(\sigma)
is cuspidal. Moreover, by Proposition
7.13
, the theta lift supports a Fourier coefficient of type
(4\,2)
and, in particular, it is non-zero. Let
\pi
be an irreducible subquotient of
\Theta(\sigma)
. Its component at
p
is the Steinberg representation by the compatibility between the global and local correspondences (Proposition
6.5
)
and by Proposition
6.10
(2). We are now left to prove the statement on its archimedean component. As
\pi
is unitary,
\pi_{\infty}
is a unitarizable Harish-Chandra module by
[
Fla79
, Theorem 4]
. Moreover, as
\sigma_{\infty}
is a discrete series with infinitesimal character
r\varepsilon_{1}+s\varepsilon_{2}
, it follows from the discussion in
[
Li97
, p. 204]
and by table 1 on
[
Li99
, p. 375]
that
\Theta(\sigma_{\infty})
has infinitesimal character
(r,\frac{1}{2}(r+s),\frac{1}{2}(r-s))
, which is strongly regular in the sense of
[
SR99
, Definition 1.5]
. By another application of Proposition
6.5
,
\pi_{\infty}
is a subquotient of
\Theta(\sigma_{\infty})
, hence has a strongly regular infinitesimal character. As a consequence, we can apply
[
SR99
, Theorem 1.8]
to deduce that
\pi_{\infty}
is cohomological. By
[
KS22
, Corollary 2.8]
, since
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg and
\pi_{\infty}
is cohomological,
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series with infinitesimal character
(r,\frac{1}{2}(r+s),\frac{1}{2}(r-s))
.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{\infty}
is a discrete series in
\mathcal{D}_{3,1}
.
If
\Theta(\sigma_{\infty})
admits a unique irreducible quotient
\theta(\sigma_{\infty})
, then by the results of
[
Li97
]
,
\theta(\sigma_{\infty})
is a discrete series of Hodge type
(3,3)
. This implies that
\pi_{\infty}=\theta(\sigma_{\infty})
is a discrete series of Hodge type
(3,3)
. Although Howe duality conjecture for the pair
(G_{2},\mathrm{PGSp}_{6})
is known at non-archimedean places
[
GS23
]
, the conjecture is still open at the archimedean place.

8.
The cycle class formula and the standard motive for
G_{2}

We conclude this article with the arithmetic applications described in the introduction.

8.1.
The relation between
L
-functions of
G_{2}
and
{\rm PGSp}_{6}

The dual group of
G_{2}
is
G_{2}({\bf C})
, which can be realized as the intersection
{\rm SO}_{7}({\bf C})\cap{\rm Spin}_{7}({\bf C})
. More precisely, we have the commutative diagram

(22)

\textstyle{G_{2}({\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Std}}
\textstyle{{\rm SO}_{7}({\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm GL}_{7}({\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Spin}_{7}({\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Spin}}
\textstyle{{\rm SO}_{8}({\bf C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm GL}_{8}({\bf C}),}

where
{\rm Std}:G_{2}({\bf C})\to\mathrm{GL}(V_{7})
is the standard representation given by trace zero octonions,
{\rm Spin}:{\rm Spin}_{7}({\bf C})\to\mathrm{GL}(V_{8})
is the 8-dimensional spin representation, while the embedding
\zeta
is defined from the fact that the stabilizer in
{\rm Spin}_{7}({\bf C})
of a generic vector of
V_{8}
is isomorphic to
G_{2}({\bf C})
. From the commutative diagram, one immediately sees that

{V_{8}}_{|_{G_{2}}}=V_{7}\oplus\mathbf{1}.

In particular, if
\pi_{\ell}
is an unramified smooth representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}({\bf Q}_{\ell})
with Satake parameter
s_{\pi_{\ell}}
belonging to
\zeta(G_{2}({\bf C}))
, then

L(s,\pi_{\ell},{\rm Spin})=L(s,\pi_{\ell},{\rm Std})\zeta_{\ell}(s),

where

L(s,\pi_{\ell},{\rm Std}):=\frac{1}{{\rm det}(1-\ell^{-s}{\rm Std}(s_{\pi_{\ell}}))}

denotes the Euler factor at
\ell
of the 7-dimensional standard
L
-function for
G_{2}
.

Let now
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
, which is unramified outside a finite set of places
S
containing the archimedean place. As a special case of Langlands functoriality, one expects that if
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
has a simple pole at
s=1
, then
\pi
is a functorial lift from either
G_{2}
or
G_{2}^{c}
, where recall that
G_{2}^{c}
denotes the form of
G_{2}
which is compact at
\infty
and split at all finite places of
{\bf Q}
. We invite the reader to consult
[
GJ01
]
,
[
GG09
]
,
[
PS18
]
, and
[
GS20
]
for results in this direction. Moreover, the existence of a pole is usually related to the non-vanishing of a certain period. The following result
2
2

2

We point out that Proposition
8.1
is not really needed in the following (it is cited in the proof of Theorem 8.6, but only to know that the
L
-function of the lift from
G_{2}
to
{\mathrm{PGSp}}_{6}
has a pole at
s=1
), but it might be of independent interest.
, summarizing and complementing known results in this direction, gives equivalence conditions for
\pi
to be a weak functorial lift from
G_{2}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Suppose that
\pi
satisfies the hypotheses
(DS)
and
(St)
of §
2.7
. Then
\pi
is tempered and the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The partial
L
-function
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
has a simple pole at
s=1
,

(2)

For almost all
\ell
, the Satake parameter
s_{\pi_{\ell}}\in\zeta(G_{2}({\bf C}))
,

(3)

There exists a cuspidal automorphic representation
\sigma
of either
G_{2}
or
G_{2}^{c}
such that
\pi
is a weak functorial lift of
\sigma
.

Moreover, if
\pi
supports a Fourier coefficient of rank 2 associated to the quadratic extension
F
these conditions are equivalent to

(4)

\pi
is
\mathbf{H}
-distinguished, with
\mathbf{H}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}\boxtimes\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}
, i.e. that there exists a cusp form
\Psi
in
\pi
such that

\int_{{\bf Z}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\Psi(h)dh\neq 0.

If one of the first three conditions hold, the residue at
s=1
of the partial
L
-function
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin)}
is given by

{\rm Res}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin)}=L^{S}(1,\sigma,{\rm Std})\prod_{\ell\in S}(1-\ell^{-1}).

Proof.

Since
\pi
is cohomological and it is Steinberg at a finite place, we can apply
[
KS22
, Lemma 2.7]
to deduce that
\pi
is essentially tempered at all places. As
\pi
has trivial central character, this is equivalent to being tempered. The equivalence between
(2)
and
(3)
and the implication
(1)\implies(3)
follow from
[
GS20
, Theorem 1.1]
. By
[
GG09
, Proposition 5.2]
, if
\pi
is
\mathbf{H}
-distinguished then its big theta lift to
G_{2}
is non-zero and is contained in the space of cusp forms on
G_{2}
. By the compatibility between the local and global theta correspondence, this implies that every local component
\pi_{v}
appears in the local theta correspondence. When
v
is a finite unramified place for
\pi
,
[
GG09
, Proposition 5.1]
implies that
s_{\pi_{v}}\in\zeta(G_{2}({\bf C}))
. This shows
(4)\implies(3)
.

We next prove that
(1)\implies(4)
, for which we’ll use the hypothesis on the existence of a Fourier coefficient of rank 2. By
[
PS18
, Theorem 2.7]
(cf. Theorem
5.8
), given a cusp form
\Psi
in
\pi
, there exists a cusp form
\tilde{\Psi}
and a Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
such that

\mathcal{I}(\Phi,\tilde{\Psi},s)=\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi,\Psi,s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin)}

By
Proposition
5.10
, taking residues at
s=1
on both sides we have

\frac{\widehat{\Phi}(0)}{2}\cdot\int_{{\bf Z}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\tilde{\Psi}(h)dh=\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi,\Psi,s)L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})\right),

where
c>0
is the constant of Lemma
5.1
. We now use
[
GG09
, Proposition 12.1]
to deduce that there exists local data
\Phi_{\infty}
and
\Psi_{\infty}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi,\Psi,1)\neq 0
. Hence, up to modifying
\Psi
and
\Phi
at
\infty
, we obtain

\widehat{\Phi}(0)\cdot\int_{{\bf Z}(\mathbf{A})\mathbf{H}({\bf Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{A})}\tilde{\Psi}(h)dh=C\cdot\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin}),

with
C
a certain non-zero constant in
{\bf C}
. Note finally that we have the freedom to choose
\Phi
such that
\widehat{\Phi}(0)\neq 0
. This follows from the fact that, given the two non-zero linear maps
l_{1}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A}^{2})\to{\bf C},\Phi\mapsto\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(\Phi,\Psi,1)
and
l_{2}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A}^{2})\to{\bf C},\Phi\mapsto\widehat{\Phi}(0)
,
{\rm ker}(l_{1})\cup{\rm ker}(l_{2})\neq\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{A}^{2})
. This shows that if
L^{S}(s,\pi,\mathrm{Spin})
has a simple pole and
\pi
supports a Fourier coefficient of rank 2, then
\pi
is
\mathbf{H}
-distinguished.

We finally show the implication
(2)\implies(1)
. The commutative diagram (
22
) implies that

\displaystyle L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})=L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Std})\zeta^{S}(s),

where
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Std})
is the partial
L
-function of
\pi
associated to the standard 7-dimensional representation of
{\rm Spin}_{7}
. By
[
LS19
, Theorem 1.1.1]
, the restriction to
{\rm Sp}_{6}(\mathbf{A})
of
\pi
contains a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi^{\flat}
, such that (up to possibly enlarging
S
)

L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Std})=L^{S}(s,\pi^{\flat},{\rm Std}).

By
[
KS22
, Corollary 2.2 & Lemma 2.3]
, there exists a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi^{\sharp}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{7}(\mathbf{A})
such that

L^{S}(s,\pi^{\flat},{\rm Std})=L^{S}(s,\pi^{\sharp}),

where
L^{S}(s,\pi^{\sharp})
denotes the standard
L
-function of
\pi^{\sharp}
. We claim that
L^{S}(1,\pi^{\sharp})\neq 0
. By
[
JS76
, Theorem (1.3)]
,
L(s,\pi^{\sharp})\neq 0
for any
s
with
{\rm Re}(s)=1
. If we write

L^{S}(s,\pi^{\sharp})=L(s,\pi^{\sharp})\prod_{\ell\in S}L(s,\pi^{\sharp}_{\ell})^{-1},

then our claim follows from the fact that each
L(s,\pi^{\sharp}_{\ell})
has no pole at
s=1
(cf.
[
RS96
, p. 317]
).
This implies that

L^{S}(1,\pi,{\rm Std})\neq 0.

Thus,
L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})
has a simple pole at
s=1
. This proves that
(2)
implies
(1)
.

If now we assume
(3)
, i.e. that
\pi
is a weak functorial lift of
\sigma
, then (up to possibly enlarging
S
)

L^{S}(1,\sigma,{\rm Std})=L^{S}(1,\pi,{\rm Std})\neq 0,

where the first equality is a consequence of the fact that the Satake parameters of
\sigma
and
\pi
agree almost everywhere. In particular

{\rm Res}_{s=1}L^{S}(s,\pi,{\rm Spin})=L^{S}(1,\sigma,{\rm Std}){\rm Res}_{s=1}\zeta^{S}(s)=L^{S}(1,\sigma,{\rm Std})\prod_{\ell\in S}(1-\ell^{-1}),

showing the final claim.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

Let
\pi
be as in Corollary
5.12
. Assuming
(St)
,
\pi
is a weak functorial lift of
\sigma
as in Proposition
8.1
and Theorem
5.11
reads as

\langle\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{H}}^{[\lambda,\mu]},[\omega_{\Psi}]\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=C\cdot\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)\cdot\prod_{\ell\in S}(1-\ell^{-1})\cdot L^{S}(1,\sigma,\mathrm{Std}).

8.2.
Galois representations of
G_{2}
-type

The following result for the compact form of
G_{2}
is shown in
[
KS22
, Theorem 11.1 and Corollary 11.3]
. The same proof works for the split form of
G_{2}
as long as one has some information on its lift to
\mathrm{PGSp}_{6}
, and we only sketch it for the convenience of the reader.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}(\mathbf{A})
or
G_{2}^{c}(\mathbf{A})
which lifts to a non-zero cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
such that

—

\pi_{\infty}
is cohomological,

—

\pi_{p}
is the Steinberg representation at some finite prime
p
.

Then, for each prime
\ell
and
\iota:{\bf C}\cong\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell}
, there exists a Galois representation
\rho_{\sigma}=\rho_{\sigma,\iota}:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{{\bf Q}}/{\bf Q})\to G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
such that

—

For every finite place
v\neq\ell
where
\sigma
is unramified,
\rho_{\sigma}
is unramified at
v
. Moreover, the semisimple part of
\rho_{\sigma}({\rm Frob}_{v})
is conjugate to the Satake parameter
\iota(s_{\sigma_{v}})
in
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
.

—

\rho_{\sigma_{\ell}}
is de Rham, and it is crystalline if
\sigma
is unramified at
\ell
.

—

\zeta\circ\rho_{\sigma}=\rho_{\pi}
, where
\pi
is a theta lift of
\sigma
, and
\zeta:G_{2}({\bf C})\to\mathrm{Spin}_{7}({\bf C})
is the embedding appearing in (
22
).

—

the Zariski closure of the image of
\rho_{\sigma}
maps onto either the image of a principal
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
in
G_{2}
or onto
G_{2}
.

Proof.

By
[
KS22
, Theorem A]
, there exists a representation
\rho_{\pi}:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{{\bf Q}}/{\bf Q})\to{\rm Spin}_{7}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
attached to
\pi
. By the proof of
[
KS22
, Theorem 11.1]
, one has that the image of
\rho_{\pi}
is contained in
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
, and thus we have
\rho_{\sigma}
such that
\zeta\circ\rho_{\sigma}=\rho_{\pi}
for a suitable choice of embedding
\zeta:G_{2}({\bf C})\to\mathrm{Spin}_{7}({\bf C})
fitting in the diagram (
22
).
Hence, by
[
KS22
, Theorem A]
and Proposition
6.10
(1), the representation
\rho_{\sigma}:\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{{\bf Q}}/{\bf Q})\to G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
satisfies the desired first three properties.
Finally, by
[
KS22
, Theorem A
(v)
]
, the Zariski closure of
\rho_{\pi}
must map onto either a principal
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}
in
\rm{SO}_{7}\cap G_{2}
, or
G_{2}
.
∎

In the following Proposition, we describe several cases where Theorem
8.3
applies. Before doing that, we need to introduce some notation. Let
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
denote the two fundamental weights for
G_{2}
, where
\omega_{1}
is the highest weight of the standard representation and
\omega_{2}
of the 14-dimensional adjoint representation. According to our convention on the root system for
G_{2}
in §
6.2.1
,
\omega_{1}=a+2b
and
\omega_{2}=2a+3b
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}^{\circ}(\mathbf{A})
with
\circ\in\{\emptyset,c\}

such that
\sigma_{\infty}
is a discrete series of infinitesimal character
(r,s)
where
r-3\geq s-1\geq 0
and
r-s
is even (if
\circ=c
then
\sigma_{\infty}
is the irreducible algebraic representation of
G_{2}^{c}({\bf R})
of highest weight
(s-1)\omega_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}(r-s-2)\omega_{2}
) and
\sigma_{p}
is Steinberg at some finite place
p
.
Suppose that one of the following conditions holds.

(1)

We have
\circ=c
and there exists
\alpha\in\sigma
and a quaternion subalgebra
D
of the non-split octonions
\mathbb{O}^{c}
such that

P^{C}_{\alpha}:=\int_{C({\bf Q})\backslash C(\mathbf{A})}\alpha(v)dv\neq 0,

where
C
is the centralizer of
D
in
G_{2}^{c}
.

(2)

We have
\circ=\emptyset
and either
\sigma
is globally generic or
\sigma_{\infty}
is non-generic and
\sigma
supports a Fourier coefficient of type
(4\,2)
corresponding to
{\bf Q}\times F
, where
F
is a real quadratic étale
{\bf Q}
-algebra.

Then there exists a non-trivial small theta lift
\pi
of
\sigma
to
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
which is a cuspidal automorphic representation, such that
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series with infinitesimal character
(r,\frac{1}{2}(r+s),\frac{1}{2}(r-s))
and
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be as in assumption (1). Since the Steinberg representation is generic, then by
[
GS98
, Corollary 4.9]
the big theta lift of
\sigma
to
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
has a non-trivial cuspidal irreducible subquotient
\pi
, which is unramified at almost all places. The infinitesimal character of its archimedean component is given in
[
GS98
, Theorem 3.5]
, and by
6.5
and by Proposition
6.10
we have that
\pi_{p}
is Steinberg. Under the assumption (2), if
\sigma
is globally generic, the result follows from
[
HKT23
, Theorem 1.7]
, and if
\sigma_{\infty}
is not generic and
\sigma
supports a Fourier coefficient as in the statement, the result follows from Theorem
7.8
.
∎

By construction, the composition of the Galois representation
\rho_{\pi}
(and thus
\rho_{\sigma}
) with the Spin representation appears in
H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G},\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\ell}(3))
, where the latter denotes the direct limit of the cohomology at level
U
in coefficients in the
\ell
-adic lisse sheaf associated to an irreducible algebraic representation
V^{\lambda}
of
\mathbf{G}
, as
U
varies. This direct limit is a smooth admissible
\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell}
-representation of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}_{f})
, endowed with an action of
\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{{\bf Q}}/{\bf Q})
commuting with the one of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}_{f})
. Let
\sigma
and
\pi
be as in the statement of Theorem
8.3
. Choose an embedding of the rationality field
L
of
\pi
in
\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell}
. Then by Lemma
2.8
, the
\pi_{f}^{\vee}
-isotypic component of
H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t},!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G},\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\ell}(3))
is 8-dimensional
\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell}
-vector space, and we have

H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t},!}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G},\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\ell}(3))[\pi_{f}^{\vee}]=V_{{\rm Spin}\circ\rho_{\pi}}\otimes\pi_{f}^{\vee}=V_{{\rm Spin}\circ\zeta\circ\rho_{\sigma}}\otimes\pi_{f}^{\vee}.

If the image of
\rho_{\sigma}
is Zariski dense in
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
, we have
{\rm Spin}\circ\zeta\circ\rho_{\sigma}={\rm Std}\circ\rho_{\sigma}\oplus\mathbf{1}
, where
{\rm Std}\circ\rho_{\sigma}
is the irreducible “standard” Galois representation attached to
\sigma
. If not, by Theorem
8.3
, the image of
\rho_{\sigma}
is Zariski dense onto a principal
\xi:{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})\to G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
. Then, the branching law of
[
Gro00
, (7.1)]
gives that
{\rm Spin}\circ\zeta\circ\rho_{\sigma}={\rm Sym}^{6}\circ\rho_{\sigma}\oplus\mathbf{1}
, where
{\rm Sym}^{6}\circ\rho_{\sigma}
is the irreducible symmetric sixth power Galois representation attached to
\sigma
. Denote by
M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})
the Galois representation
V_{{\rm Spin}\circ\rho_{\pi}}
and let
M_{\ell}(\sigma_{f})
be either the Galois representation
V_{{\rm Std}\circ\rho_{\sigma}}
or
V_{{\rm Sym}^{6}\circ\rho_{\sigma}}
. Then, we have that
M_{\ell}(\sigma_{f})^{G_{\bf Q}}=0
and
M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})
decomposes as the direct sum

(23)

M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})=M_{\ell}(\sigma_{f})\oplus\mathbf{1},

where
\mathbf{1}
denotes the one dimensional trivial representation.

Remark 8.5
.

In the case where
\rho_{\sigma}
is not Zariski dense in
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
, the Satake parameter
s_{\sigma_{p}}\in\xi({\mathrm{SL}}_{2}({\bf C}))
for any unramified prime
p
. By Langlands reciprocity principle,
\sigma
should be the functorial lift of a cuspidal automorphic representation
\tau
of
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(\mathbf{A})
, while
V_{{\rm Sym}^{6}\circ\rho_{\sigma}}
should be a geometric realization of the motive of the symmetric sixth power of
\tau
.

8.3.
On a question of Gross and Savin

Tate conjecture predicts the existence of a cycle which gives rise to the trivial representation appearing in the decomposition of Equation (
23
). In
[
GS98
]
, Gross and Savin, inspired by local computations, conjectured that this cycle should come from a Hilbert modular 3-fold inside
{\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}
. Theorem
8.6
below supports this expectation for certain cuspidal automorphic representations
\sigma
of
G_{2}
and
G_{2}^{c}
.

Let
\sigma
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G_{2}^{\circ}(\mathbf{A})
with
\circ\in\{\emptyset,c\}

such that
\sigma_{\infty}
is a discrete series of infinitesimal character
(r,s)
with
r-3\geq s-1\geq 0
and
r-s
even and
\sigma_{p}
is Steinberg at some finite place
p
and let
\pi
be the small theta lift of
\sigma
given by Proposition
8.4
. Let
V^{\lambda}
denote an irreducible algebraic representation of
\mathbf{G}
of highest weight
\lambda=(r-3,\frac{1}{2}(r+s)-2,\frac{1}{2}(r-s)-1,0)
. Note that
V^{\lambda}|_{\mathbf{H}}
contains the trivial representation by Lemma
3.2
. Let
U\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A}_{f})
denote a neat compact open subgroup such that
\pi_{f}^{U}\neq 0
. For any
\frac{1}{2}(r+s)-2\geq\mu\geq\frac{1}{2}(r-s)-1
, let

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}:=\rm{cl}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]})\in H^{6}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\operatorname{Sh}}_{\mathbf{G}}(U)_{\overline{{\bf Q}}},\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\ell}(3))^{G_{\bf Q}}

be the étale realization of the motivic class
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\mathcal{M}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
(see Definition
3.9
), where
\mathbf{H}=\mathrm{GL}_{2}\boxtimes\mathrm{GL}_{2,F}^{*}
. Fix a vector
\Psi_{f}\in\pi_{f}^{U}
. By composing the projection to the
\pi_{f}^{\vee}
-isotypic component together with the projection given by the vector
\Psi_{f}
, we get

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{\sigma}:=\Psi_{f}({\rm pr}_{\pi^{\vee}}(\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}))\in(M_{\ell}(\sigma_{f})\oplus\mathbf{1})^{G_{\bf Q}}=\mathbf{1}.

By (the proof of) Lemma
2.8
, there exists a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi^{3,3}=\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}\otimes\pi_{f}
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{A})
whose archimedean component is a discrete series of Hodge type
(3,3)
with the same infinitesimal character of
\pi_{\infty}
and whose non-archimedean part
\pi_{f}
is the same as the one of
\pi
. Let
\Psi=\Psi_{\infty}\otimes\Psi_{f}
be the cusp form in the space of
\pi^{3,3}
such that
\Psi_{\infty}
is a highest weight vector of the minimal
K_{\infty}
-type of
\pi_{\infty,1}^{3,3}\subseteq\pi_{\infty}^{3,3}|_{{\mathrm{Sp}}_{6}({\bf R})}
. For any
\mu
as above, recall that we denote
\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]}=A^{[\lambda,\mu]}\cdot\Psi_{\infty}\otimes\Psi_{f}
, where
A^{[\lambda,\mu]}
is the operator that appeared in Proposition
4.8
.

Theorem 8.6
.

Assume that the integral
\mathcal{I}_{S}(\Phi,\Psi^{[\lambda,\mu]},1)
is non-zero for some Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
. Then the class
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{\sigma}
generates the trivial sub-representation
\mathbf{1}
of
M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})
.

Proof.

By the comparison theorem between étale and Betti cohomology
[
AGV73
, Exposé XI, Theorem 4.4 (iii)]
, Proposition
8.1
and Corollary
5.12
, we know that the projection
{\rm pr}_{\pi^{\vee}}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{[\lambda,\mu]}
to
M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})\otimes(\pi_{f}^{U})^{\vee}
generates a one dimensional subspace, which is trivial for the action of the Galois group. As we have explained above, the image of
\rho_{\sigma}
is either dense in
G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
or in
{\mathrm{SL}}_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})\to{\rm PGL}_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})\hookrightarrow G_{2}(\overline{{\bf Q}}_{\ell})
. In either case, the representation
M_{\ell}(\sigma_{f})
is irreducible and the trivial factor
\mathbf{1}
in
M_{\ell}(\pi_{f})
is hence generated by the image of
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{H},\text{\'{e}t}}^{\sigma}
.
∎
